## Section II b) The Blossom-Time of Lutheran Devotional Literature 1650–1680

*Source: II b) opens at source p. 218 and closes at p. 306. Große's own running-head sets the sub-title:* **"Große Gebetssammlungen; die drei größten luth. Erbauungsschriftsteller: Lütkemann, Müller, Scriver."** *The Section itself unfolds in an opening §1–§2 (Allgemeines and Frömmigkeit), then in three parts: **A. Die Großen** — Lütkemann, Müller, Scriver, Lassenius; **B. Vertreter der Erklärer** — Fibicius and the Württemberger Summarien / Altenburger Bibel; **C. Vertreter der von den Sprachgesellschaften beeinflußten Autoren** — Rittmeyer, Quirsfeld, Pfeiffer, Dilherr. Section II a) closed at Meyfart in the shadow of the Thirty Years' War; II b) opens as the same devotional-literary current gathers into its first blossom-time — a chronological paradox, since (as §2 at once concedes) the Blütezeit of the literature is not a Blütezeit of the piety.*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §1. General Remarks

*Source: II b) §1, pp. 218–221. Große opens the Blütezeit with a survey of the great prayer-collections which stand behind and beside the four *Großen,* and closes with a nota bene on the popular-exposition Bibles of the same age.*

The exceedingly rich devotional literature of the previous period continues in that now following. Yet within it certain unequally-significant devotional writers stand forth, whose works lift the whole literature to the highest step it ever attained. They created writings which from the outset rose over their own times, and therefore, as no others, were mightily-effective both in their own age and in the following ages down to today. These are **the three great, all-overtopping pray-ers: Joachim Lütkemann,** whose work already begins in the previous period, **and his even greater students Heinrich Müller and Christian Scriver.** In Müller's spirit then works also his own student **Johannes Lassenius.** Their works mark the *one* main direction in which the religious folk-literature — in closest attachment to that which the previous century had established as evangelical custom and piety — moved forward.

Alongside these stand the **prayer-collections,** in which the best prayers of unknown or known pray-ers — of a Luther, Habermann, Bienemann, Arndt, and others — are ever repeated, gathered up out of less-significant [prayer-books]. In them are sought possible prayers for all needs of the various estates. The most significant of these prayer-books — enjoyed by many pious souls throughout the whole hundred years [of the period], and serving us also as a source of the best prayers — is the ***Allgemeines Gebetbuch* of Michael Cubach.**¹⁹⁰ It deserves therefore to be more closely discussed.

**Michael Cubach** was around the middle of the century a bookseller at Lüneburg. The first edition appeared probably in 1655; the second, of 1657, and the third, of 1660, are still extant; from thence to 1791 **Beck** counts 28 editions. That of Wittgau's Widow, 1672, had 1,381 pages in 18°; in 1687 it appeared at Leipzig in the Weidmann press; on 7 [January] 1688 **Scriver** commended it with a preface. The title runs:

> ***Einer gläubigen und andächtigen Seelen vermehrte tägliches Bet-, Buß-, Lob- und Dankopfer*** — that is, a great prayer-book to be used in all manner of spiritual and bodily, common and singular distresses and cares. Gathered out of 75 *Autores* into ten distinct parts, as **1)** Day- and Week-book; **2)** Book of Teaching, Warfare, and Sustenance; **3)** Confession- and Communion-book; **4)** Feast-book; **5)** Singular book; **6)** Cross-book; **7)** War-, Dearth-, and Plague-book; **8)** Year- and Weather-book; **9)** Travel-book; **10)** Sickness- and Dying-book — whose contents are indicated by two appended registers, the first alphabetical over all prayers, the second directed to all Sunday-, Feast-, and Apostles'-days. **Lüneburg**, in publication of M. Cubach's, printed at Leipzig 1658, 8°.

Out of at first 65, later 75, and then more than 100 authors, Cubach — and his successors — gather the best (some 1,300) spirit- and Scripture-rich prayers, and prove thereby that he had an excellent acquaintance with the prayer-literature and a good religious judgement.

Cubach draws from all times of the Church, but especially from the post-Reformation age. Of the Church Fathers there are: **Ambrose, Augustine, Athanasius, Dionysius [Areopagita], Jerome, Polycarp;** from the Middle Ages, **Anselm, Bernard, Tauler.** From Luther are three prayers. Further the following authors are represented: **Johann Arndt** (very numerously), **Georg Zeaemann** (71 prayers, 1658) [OCR fragmentary here; also **Melchior] Ammermann,** **Ludwig Sciemer** [——], and various others [OCR broken through several lines]. Included are English authors like **[Lewis] Bayly**; the [subscription-]letters *M. C.* point most probably to Cubach himself. The prayers are for the most part genuinely the most excellent [OCR damaged]; only among those where prayers for all estates are to be found do some bear too much the stamp of the made-up.

Many prayer-collections come out one upon another. About the collection of the best prayers there earned much at that time, among others, **Johann Gottfried Olearius,** Superintendent at Halle, †1711 as Superintendent at Arnstadt, whose father **Johann Olearius** (†1684) had already written rich writings — his ***Neu-vollständiges Haus- und Kirchenhandbuch,*** Leipzig 1670, and his ***Himmelspforte,*** 1679 (following Grönnete's *Himmelspforte*) — and who published a new *"Vollständiges allgemeines Gebet- und [Kirchen-]buch,"* in which he had, [as the title runs,] *"attached the chosen kernel and extract of the most beautiful old and new prayers and sighs of holy Christianity,"* — **Gottfried Olearius,** preacher at Liebfrauen [Halle], with the press of Christian Weidmann, 1670; 1,244 pages, 8°. From these collections, e.g. **[Wilhelm] Löhe** drew for his house-book ***Samenkörner der himmlischen Erndte.*** In them one finds also the widely-spread morning- and evening-blessings for all weekdays which had been in circulation since the older time (ca. 1600–1650), as well as the spiritual songs of **Georg Schimmer** (b. 1662 at [——], †1695 at Wittenberg), ***Biblisches Seelenkleinod,*** which is turned to use for instance by *B. der Große Gebetsschatz.* On the whole the prayer-treasure is also enriched by new prayers of that and of the preceding period. Thus from 1679 come the prayers in **Arndt's *Wahres Christentum*** — worked by **Fischer in Livland;** see the notice at §10 above.¹⁹¹

That at that time also work was done on the *popular exposition* of the Bible is proved by the **Württemberger Summarien** and the **Altenburger Bibel,** both of which were reissued; no less than the 1650 publication of the German translation of **Lucas Osiander's glossed Bible** by the Stern press at Lüneburg; the 1677 **Biblische Konkordanz of Lankisch;** and the 1682 **Calov's German Bible.** To ease the reading of the Bible in the folk, **Konrad Sustmann,**¹⁹² Superintendent of the County of Lippe, wrote in 1650 his *Betbibel* — which contains prayers on every chapter of Holy Scripture, and which was three times published at Basel until 1705, appearing elsewhere as well as an appendix to Bibles. The new editions undertaken by the *Christlicher Verein im nördlichen Deutschland* (1863) and by Adelberg (1867 — first Erlangen, then Stuttgart, Evang. Gesellschaft) can unfortunately now only be found at antiquarian booksellers.

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §2. Toward the Piety of the Blossom-Time of the Lutheran Devotional Literature

*Source: II b) §2, pp. 221–228. The Blütezeit-paradox: the age of the greatest Lutheran devotional writings is not itself an age of blossoming piety. Große sketches the political-moral counter-current, the pastoral response through visitation and pre-Spener reforming voices, the courts of Ernst the Pious of Gotha and the Schwarzburg-Rudolstadt house as witnesses to inward princely piety, the daily prayer-discipline of the young Elector Johann Georg III of Saxony, and a gallery of hymn-writers whose lives make the same piety-under-the-cross tangible.*

Were one to conclude that the age which produced the most-significant Lutheran devotional writings was also a *blossom-time of Lutheran piety,* that conclusion would be false. The after-pains of the destruction wrought by the Thirty Years' War, and new war-troubles from the side of **France** and of the **Turks,** together with the moral corruption streaming in from France, and the — without regard for the folk's need — often over-zealously prosecuted controversies of the epigones of Lutheran Orthodoxy, prevented that. On the other side, the wars with France, the continuing persecutions in **Silesia** and **Poland,** and the cross-time of the great war bore the fruit that the faithful work of the Church, in many of her pastors and magistracies, sought — especially through *visitations* — to save what could be saved, and one began to build anew and to give life. **Ecclesiastical piety** thus takes, in many places, a fresh flight. In part the *hidden spiritual life* runs on further, which the confusion of the time could never extinguish; in part it begins anew, in attachment to the evangelical custom and piety that had grown up in the previous century. The great men of the Lutheran devotional literature had, with their sermons and writings, a truly far-reaching influence. This is proved already by the numerous editions of the same which were always again becoming necessary — [——] and it is upon the inward force and the for-the-age unusual purity of style [Reinheit des Stiles] of these writings that their [effect] rests. **(Tholuck.)**¹⁹³

So many things which **Spener's** mighty personality later carried through more generally, were in that time already emphasized by others and carried out within their circles. In his *Desideria* he gave only *words* to that which had already moved many. For it he received a *joyful greeting of 90 voices.* Through the numerous [educational reforms] youth was renewed, school-oversight was by the clergy freshly ordered and lifted up. The *Pia desideria* as such were prepared by the ***Pia desideria* of Balthasar Meisner,** issued nameless at Stettin in 1642; further by that of **Johann Quistorp II** in 1659, recommended by the Faculty at Rostock; further by the ***Wächterstimme aus dem verwaisten Zion*** of **Theophil Großgebauer** at Rostock, 1661, in which he speaks with warmth and inwardness of the defects of the Church as also of her means of healing; and further by the *bell-clear "Zuchtposaune"* of the same **Joachim Schröder,** 1666.¹⁹⁴ Already at Spener's beginning activity, — Tholuck emphasizes — many *living witnesses* stand on the lecterns and pulpits, who according to the principle *"for every soul one shall have to give account, and it shall one day be said: thy soul for his soul,"* and Scriver's word: ***"Preachers must consume themselves like the lights — only that they may give light to others,"*** strove to labour. Let there here only still be recalled the famous **Balthasar Schuppius**¹⁹⁵ (†1661 as *Hauptpastor* at Hamburg), a mighty soul-carer and devotional writer such as few in that century.

Apart from the *special piety-collegia* — which apart from **Moller's** house-assemblies at Görlitz (which even *Jakob Böhme* attended, from 1600 on), and other such gatherings, [remained on the whole] exceptions — there step forward from 1670 on with **Spener's *collegia pietatis*** at Frankfurt their like: gatherings in which the pious might, [as it seemed] purposefully, speak edifyingly one with another. And in [the same period] the [pastoral] house-visits [of the Superintendents at Ravensberg and at Lüneburg] were blessed vessels of the new life; and what was said in the previous [tranche] concerning the *domestic life* with its house-devotion holds also here. From the church and the house outward the ethical life in Christendom was likewise noticeably influenced: [profligacy], the sins of the flesh, [violence and perjury] which had bloomed in the Thirty Years' War, receded again.

In **Rostock,** the court of **Duke Gustav Adolf of Mecklenburg-Güstrow** (1654–1695) and his influence upon the Church of that time bear witness to this just as much as does *that which — already known from the previous period — still stood in glorious blossom.* The regiment of **Duke Ernst the Pious of Gotha** (†1675), and likewise that of the pious **Ahasverus Fritsch** [†1701 at Rudolstadt] and of the *Schwarzburg-Rudolstadt house* — with the Princesses **Ludämilie Elisabeth** (†1672) and **Amalie Juliane** (†1706) — are witnesses to the *power of an inward princely piety.*¹⁹⁶ Of the Prussian house we may name the **Electress Louise Henriette of Brandenburg,** who devoted her office to *worship and love of neighbour,* and, standing at the side of the [Great] Elector, the Reformed **Otto von Schwerin** [†1679], tutor to Friedrich I, with whom Schwerin diligently in that time [read the Gospel and the Psalms].

That *piety could develop, and did develop, in the most various life-circumstances,* is shown at this close by the glimpse into the prayer-discipline of that age, as also into the prayer-life of some of the aforementioned lives. For the prayer-discipline of that age, the *life-arrangement* which was made for the **young Elector Johann Georg III of Saxony** (born 1647) is instructive:¹⁹⁷ — At 7 o'clock the prince is to rise with the singing of *"Gelobet seist du, Gott Vater";* during his dressing, those standing round are to sing a spiritual song; then the prince goes with the [attending] court-staff to breakfast, into his chamber for particular prayer or into the church; then follow, from 8 to 10, two work-hours, each *"to be begun with a short prayer for God's assistance, and to be closed with a thanksgiving-psalm";* from 10 to 11 shall be play- and recreation-hour, then luncheon, then afternoon lessons; from evening about 5 the recreation-hour again; at 8 o'clock the prince turns with the court-staff to evening prayer, whereupon the prince ate, was undressed, prayed his *evening-prayer,* and precisely at 9 o'clock went to bed.

At the end of the century such *discipline* weakens under the French court-influence and the reasonings of Glory and Pleasure that are directed therefrom, and a hardened selfishness comes in. — And yet the *glorious working of the Holy Spirit* shows itself in the *prayer-life* of many, so far as tidings of this piece of evangelical piety have come down to us at all. How to a **Paul Gerhardt** and his friend **Michael Schirmer** prayer through the *cross-school* of their lives had helped them, and how *Arndt* above all had spoken to their hearts, is more widely-known than this — that **Runge,** printer of the *Runge Hymnal* (from 1650 onward), practised a *living* [prayer-life] and gave witness to the same.¹⁹⁸ Genuine *pray-ers* too were the singing **Olearius clan:** **Johann Olearius,** †1684 as *Generalsuperintendent* at Weißenfels; **Gottfried Olearius,** *Superintendent* at Halle, his brother, †1685; and his son **Johann Gottfried Olearius,** *Superintendent* at Arnstadt, †1741.¹⁹⁹ Through much distress and privation had **Christian Keimann,** rector at Zittau (†1662), prayed himself through, and not least in the last years of his life. Tribulations through a severe skin-disease, the heavy suffering of his wife, envy and hostility, made the *Rechtskonsulent* **Ernst Christoph Homburg** (†1681 at Naumburg) an anointed pray-er in spiritual songs; and rescue from plague-danger and from robbers' hands turned him to *sing of the cross and to praise God.*²⁰⁰ A tested pray-er and cross-bearer is displayed by the life of **Johann Georg Albinus,** poet of *"Alle Menschen müssen sterben,"* †1679 as pastor at Othmar, a suburb of Naumburg; likewise by the man who died at Weimar in 1681, the *Kanzleiregistrator* **Georg Neumark,** poet of *"Wer nur den lieben Gott läßt walten"*; in smallest and greatest concerns prayed himself through a **Gottfried Wilhelm Sacer** (†1699 as *Kammerkonsulent* at Wolfenbüttel), of which his *"Segenlied eines Jünglings"* and *"Betlied eines christlich studierenden Jünglings"* offer specimens. Of **Hartmann Schenk,** †1681 as pastor at Volkershausen and deacon at Ostheim, [the same is reported].

Within the *Silesian* nobility, further, since about 1620 several noble families of note had settled from **Styria,** and became — like **Gallus von Räglkhein** (†1658) — a salt for their surroundings. So likewise **Saxony** received [numerous] families from **Bohemia** — living and inwardly-active members of the Church, who became a good element for the congregations to which they came. In this connexion belongs also the still today in his writings living-on ***Pietismus praecox*** [——].

The other [thing to note] is that the *prayer-life and piety of that time* were also nourished by the excellent **English devotional literature,** which sought especially the heart of the individual and true conversion.²⁰¹ Thus in **Cubach's Prayer-Book** prayers of **Bayly** are received (Bayly †1632 as Court-preacher of James I), whose ***Praxis Pietatis*** appeared already in 1628 in German translation at Basel, and in 1632, 1633, 1634 at Lüneburg. Likewise from **Immanuel Sonthom's *Güldenes Kleinod,*** which since 1620 was widely spread — next to Arndt and *"Nosce te ipsum"* [of Simon Rosenmüller?] — the known [satirist] **Moscherosch** (†1669) received inward furtherance; in 1657 it was warmly recommended by **Dilherr,** afresh in German translated, and then in the Pietist age until 1748 many times reissued (Nordhausen, 1735).

*[Große closes §2 with a syntactic bridge into what follows.]* In what now follows there stand at the head the chief representatives of the Blütezeit: **Lütkemann, Müller, Scriver, Lassenius;** there follows a representative of the *Erklärer* — **Fibicius** — as example for the biblical exposition, together with the **Württemberger Summarien** and the **Altenburger Bibel;** then **Rittmeyer, Quirsfeld, Pfeiffer,** and **Dilherr** as representatives of the — by the language-societies influenced — authors.

## II b) A. Die Großen — Lütkemann and his Disciples

### §3. Joachim Lütkemann (1608–1660)

*Source: II b) §3, pp. 228–233. The first of the four *Großen* of the Blossom-Time: Rostock philosophy professor turned Wolfenbüttel *Generalsuperintendent,* teacher of Heinrich Müller and Christian Scriver, driven from Rostock in 1649 over a Christological disputation, and thereafter cross-carried into a decades-long Wolfenbüttel service under Duke August the Younger. Author of the *Apostolische Aufmunterung* Epistle-postille (1652) and the ***Vorschmack göttlicher Güte*** (1653). Große treats him as spiritual heir of Arndt and spiritual father of Heinrich Müller, surpassing both in *soberness.**

**Joachim Lütkemann**²⁰² was born on **15 December 1608 at Demmin in Pomerania.** He studied at **Greifswald** and **Rostock** under the strict Lutheran J. Schmid and the warm-hearted Lutheran [Cölestin] [——]; he was in later years a friend of **Spener,** who influenced [him] [——]. A wonder of a man, in whom encyclopedic scholarship was united with sound piety. After the usual academic journey through **France** and **Italy,** he began in **1638** to lecture in the Rostock philosophy faculty. In **1639** he became *Diaconus,* soon *Archidiaconus,* at St. Jakobi; in **1643** at the same time *Professor of Theology.* As professor and preacher he was the teacher of **Heinrich Müller, Christian Scriver, Johann Jakob Fabricius** — to him they owed their first deeper stirrings, and with them he remained ever inwardly-united.

In **1649** he had, on account of a disputation, — set upon by blind zealots — to leave Rostock. He had defended the proposition that Christ, on account of the separation of body and soul that had entered in with death, could not be called a *true man* during the three days until the resurrection.²⁰³ Lütkemann was without further hearing driven from pulpit and *Rathaus* by **Duke Adolf Friedrich of Mecklenburg;** he was to sign a *Revers* — or, failing that, *"to leave the land within 8 days and without safe-conduct."* To the great [pain] of his students and congregation he departed; on a hill outside the town he yet preached a mighty farewell-sermon; but he found a new sphere of work through **Duke August the Younger of Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel,** the consort of a Mecklenburg [——] Elisabeth, through whom²⁰⁴ he became *Generalsuperintendent* still in the same year 1649, and in **1658** also *Abbot of Riddagshausen.*

After [publishing] his excellent *Schulordnung* of 1651 (afresh issued by Bodemann), he already fell asleep, on [——], of a hot fever, before the completion of his *Kirchenordnung* which appeared in 1657. Among his writings the most significant are his ***Epistelpredigten*** of **1652** and his ***Vorschmack göttlicher Güte*** of **1653** — both which he issued himself in the rich [language] of his day and (like Arndt) [with the aim] to make them a [teacher of the folk].²⁰⁵

**1. The Epistelpredigten appeared afresh under the title:**

> ***Apostolische Aufmunterung zum lebendigen Glauben in Christo Jesu.*** After the sense and content of the customary Epistles, by God's grace set forth in public sermons, by Joachim Lütkemann, Holy Scripture Doctor, *Generalsuperintendent* at Wolfenbüttel; edited by **Friedrich Wilhelm Bodemann,** pastor at Finkenwerder near Hamburg. **Hildesheim,** in the press of G. Sinbuber, 1876. — The edition is in the form of the Postills. Gr. 4°, 525 pp., 47 lines two-columned, thin paper, in good print — 3 Mk.

They appeared first in **1652** — and indeed at Frankfurt and Rostock at the press of Joachim Wilden, with a dedication to **Duke August** dated 20 January 1652. In his preface **Bodemann,** after a brief life-sketch, writes:

> The most-blessed among his writings are without question his *Epistelpredigten* first appearing in 1652 — which, just as they proceed from the word of Scripture, so also everywhere therewith flow back into it, and set the riches, depth, and self-experienced power of the same into the light in such a manner as marks him for a *Master* and a *Model,* not only of the most thorough and comprehensive Scripture-exposition, but also of a marrow-strong, deeply-hearty, living, in high degree noble, and thoroughly *folk-tune-like* presentation. On that account they gained also the most-general approval and an almost example-lessly wide dissemination, according to which they in the most various lands, now here, now there, in numerous editions and printings came to light, and became especially in our Lower-Elbe region among the most-widespread and most-blessed means of edification.

Bodemann lists the editions known to him: **1652;** 1668 Rostock; 1688 Leipzig; 1707; 1722; Dresden 1705; Hannover 1721, 1734, 1738, 1748, 1759, etc.; Lübeck 1741; earlier Erfurt; 1744; Frankfurt — [Bodemann adds] up to some **30 editions** in all. And even in the *time of the desert unbelief,* the Epistelpredigten did not blow away: they remained *at home* in the [North-German congregations] and passed there for a *kernel* [Kern], upon which the pious warmed themselves on Saturday and Sunday, and out of which they drew their devotion. There was — as individual [book-]inscriptions witness — sometimes even, in the smaller [towns], no lack of demand: to this end at times high prices (of 7 to 19 Schilling) were paid by [worshipers, or from the hand of masters given to householders]. If then the sermons which one heard on Sundays in the [pulpit] were not always fit to shape the fleshly [heart] of the natural [man] into a *living building-stone of the Church of Christ,* yet the words of Lütkemann, in their regular repetition at *house-devotions,* held fast, and were a *blessed means* to the strengthening and support of *true evangelical faith and prayer-piety* in the [named] families and congregations. Even when the cold breath of unbelief here and there thrust the Word from the candlestick — if the Christian faith and the *domestic godliness* still stood firm in individual families and congregations, then we may in truth confess that among the honoured *tools of edification* [it was] especially the Lütkemann Epistelpredigten which nourished and cultivated the Christian [devotional life].

**Heinrich Müller,** his great pupil, gave him in the preface to the *Apostolische Aufmunterung* the testimony:²⁰⁶

> Even now to this day it is, and remains, in blessing wherever one [reads them]. [——] a truth-love that turns away from all sermon-artifice [Kunstmacherei]; a nobility of disposition that discloses itself in a for-that-age unusual *purity of style.* We know no preacher in this century whose sermons carry to such a degree — [OCR damaged] — [as those of Lütkemann]:

> In these sermons was something other than what the world calls *"witty"* [geistreich]: it was a Spirit which searches heart and reins and pierces through marrow and bone — the *Spirit of the Word itself.* Lütkemann's whole mind and steady striving went upon this — that in the souls entrusted to him he should present to the Lord a congregation that would be *without blame and without spot.* Therefore one heard in all his sermons the *voice of earnestness,* which calls the secure sinner to repentance; the *voice of love and mercy,* which directs the [awakened] to Christ, that he may be forgiven by him of his sins and cleansed by the blood of the Son of God from all sin. Above all, however, one heard here the *voice of Faithfulness,* which warned the become-believing man against the danger of standing-still or turning-back on the way of following, and admonished him to unceasing *watching, prayer, and struggle,* that he might become fit *to enter through the narrow gate.*

So we may understand it, that Lütkemann's sermons, in print, became especially in North Germany a devotional-book of the first rank. They can still today be so. It is worthwhile to consider Lütkemann as *heir and spiritual son of Arndt* and *spiritual father of Heinrich Müller.* Both of them, however, he surpasses by his **soberness**: Arndt in the *content* — for he is free of every false mysticism; Heinrich Müller in the [manner of presentation] [——].

Lütkemann does not confine himself to the text-word alone: he goes to substance, though even so — when he presents *doctrine* (e.g. at Pentecost) — [OCR broken]. His speech is pure, with the exception of a few [words] of his time such as *"Stanf"* [OCR: probably *Stanf* = *Stank* — stench, or a dialectal expression]; and it is [in] this that the abiding significance of the Epistelpredigten as a *devotional book* is secured. He keeps himself free of the accumulation of examples, quotations, similes, and — as was then beloved — [rhetorical dialogues]. — Examples are only the biblical ones used amply.

**2. Der Vorschmack göttlicher Güte** — presented by Joachim Lütkemann, *weiland Generalsuperintendent zu Wolfenbüttel;* [issued] by Bodemann, 1864. Leipzig, G. Sinbuber. 16°, 839 pp., 31 lines to the page. 1 Mk. paper, 1.40 Mk. bound.²⁰⁵

Bodemann remarks in his preface that the book, alongside the Epistelpredigten, *"takes an honored and unique place."* It appeared first in **1653** and soon found wide dissemination in new editions. **Beck** conjectures that *Vorschmack* is meant in the sense of ***Vor-schmack*** — *foretaste* — not the thought that the Christian tastes God's goodness *sparingly,* but that here below he already *tastes it in advance,* however imperfectly.²⁰⁷ — How significant the book became is shown by this: it gave occasion to other similar writings which set *divine love* as the all-leading [thread] of the doctrine of salvation — e.g. **Heinrich Müller's** works of the same period (see §4 below). The *Vorschmack* was one of the books which **Spener** had read aloud in his little gatherings; and from his time comes the anecdote in which the devil ensnares a young man [into hiding the book, at the recommendation]: *"Whoever comes upon it — to that man I wish from this good [book] that it may be a fire" —* [the tradition is preserved by Tholuck].²⁰⁸

The first 19 meditations (pp. 1–194) develop, in clear thought-sequence though in edifying breadth, the following themes:

> God will in his goodness be known — testimonies are found on every side. **God himself is the highest Good** (Nos. 1–3). His goodness is a **fountain of all benefactions** (4), it is **undeserved, free, unowed** (5), **willing** (6), [beyond measure], **wonderful** (7–8). Yet only the God-fearing partake of it truly (9–10); it awakens in them a *holy longing for God* (11), teaches them the [right pursuit] (12–13), gives *comfort in tribulation* (14), *urges to prayer* (15), does not cease even when prayer seems unheard (16), exhorts to *God's praise* (17), draws to *humility* (18), and *stills all envy* (19).

*Note:* In 1898 there also appeared, in the [series ——], a Lütkemann *Grundriß der [Sittenlehre],* 238 pp., 1.80 Mk.
