### §4. Heinrich Müller (1631–1675)

*Source: II b) §4, pp. 233–252. The second and greatest of the *Großen* — Rostock cathedral-preacher and professor of theology, disciple of Lütkemann, teacher of Scriver, and the *paradigm devotional preacher* of the Lutheran Blossom-Time. Author of the ***Geistliche Erquickstunden*** (1664/1666 — 300 house- and table-devotions), the ***Apostolische Schlußkette*** (1663–1668/1671), the ***Kreuz-, Buß- und Betschule*** (1661), the ***Geistlicher Dank-Altar*** (1669), the ***Thränen- und Trostquelle*** (1675 — Müller's dying book), the *Himmlische Liebeskuß / Himmlische Liebesflamme* (1659), and the posthumous ***Evangelischer Herzensspiegel*** (1679). Große treats Müller as the widest-read and most-beloved devotional writer of the Blossom-Time — a *"pregnant, short, marrow-strong, biblical, folk-tune-like"* prose of the highest order.*

**Heinrich Müller**²⁰⁹ was born on **18 October 1631 at Lübeck.** Thither his parents had fled during the siege of Rostock by **Wallenstein** and his host.²¹⁰ The father was **Peter Müller,** merchant and *Vorsteher* of the St. Marien parish at Rostock; the mother, **Elisabeth,** was a daughter of *Ratsmann* Stubbe. Godly fear ruled the parental house, and the first love to Christ was infused into the boy by the pious mother. The boy was small, weak, and sickly, and required special care. His first schooling he had at Lübeck; then his parents could return at last to Rostock. The early-ripe boy entered the university there in **1644,** at 13 years, and became the student of **Lütkemann.** On the counsel of **Quistorp** he went in **1647** to Greifswald, and there earned the Master's dignity. In **1650** he came back — already an admiration-worthy scholar — to Rostock, to continue his studies; then made the customary study-journey from university to university: to **Königsberg,** to **Helmstedt** where he heard *Calixt,* to **Wittenberg** where he heard *Calov,*²¹¹ then to Leipzig and Jena. At Leipzig he became the house- and table-companion of **Carpzov.**²¹² *Habilitated* in 1651, he held his own lectures at Rostock. His reputation led the Council, when he was but 20 years old, to call him — at Michaelmas 1652 — as *Archidiaconus* of the Marienkirche where his father was *Vorsteher.* He wedded there **Elisabeth Rutten** [——].

His office was, from the beginning, blessed and rich in fruits. He himself received his calling as a *Jeremiah 1* — *"Ehe ich dich im Mutterleibe bereitet."* Faith preached the theme *how faith [gives] the blessed one, and lets one be truly heart-glad.* His growing [reputation drew a growing hearing]. Alongside his preaching-office he served the University: from Helmstedt he received in his 22nd year the theological Doctor-title; Rostock confirmed it only in 1660, after he had also received in 1659 the *Professor of the Greek Tongue.* In **1662** he became — at Kaspar [Sagittarius'] place — *ordentlicher Professor der Theologie* and pastor at St. Marien. In **1671,** as **Johann Kengler's** successor, he became **Consistorial Councillor** and at the same time *Stadt-Superintendent* and pastor at [the Dom]. Only *under tears* did he take up the dignity — so that Superintendent **Sommerfeld of Parchim,** who ordained him, called out amazed: *"What see I? Tears of joy? I will mark this!"*

He became a **faithful pastor.** At Confession he divided with the poor his own [alms]; like Arndt and Spener — with whom he stood in correspondence — he pressed on ***living Christianity.*** When necessary, he polemicized against sham- and appearance-Christianity. Renowned is his **zeal-witness** (*Apostolische Schlußkette,* Dom. 10 p. Trin., on 1 Cor. 12:1ff.) against **the four dumb church-idols — Baptismal-font, Preacher's-chair, Confessional, and Altar** — on which the *dead members [of the Church]* rely for their trust;²¹³ [——]. His *maxim* according to 2 Cor. 6:10 was: *"As sorrowful, yet always rejoicing"* — his speech remained *"always joyful"* [Allezeit fröhlich] [——].

The source of his strength was **prayer.** [Nicolaus] **Barcläus,** who lived with him, says: *"How often have I, with his good [scholar-pupil], up on his study-room heard him pray so mightily and so [movingly], that I was moved thereby also to bend my knees with him before the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, and to join my [prayers] with his, that it might be more powerful; for many arrows pierce more strongly than one alone."*²¹⁴ Witnesses of his *pastoral wisdom, his Scripture-knowledge, and his spirit-power* are especially his devotional writings — to which also his sermon-collections belong:

- **1661** — ***Kreuz-, Buß- und Betschule*** (from Psalm 143)
- **1664** — ***Himmlischer Liebeskuß*** (later expanded as ***Himmlische Liebesflamme***)
- **1664 / 1666** — ***Geistliche Erquickstunden*** — 300 house- and table-devotions
- **1663–1668** and **1671** — ***Apostolische Schlußkette***
- **1669** — ***Geistlicher Dank-Altar***
- **1672** — ***Evangelische Schlußkette***
- **1673** — ***Festevangelische Schlußkette***
- **1675** — ***Thränen- und Trostquelle*** (Müller's dying book)
- **1679** (posthumous) — ***Evangelischer Herzensspiegel***
- **1684** — ***Gräber der [Heiligen]***

They all found very soon an enormous distribution. As professor Müller also wrote many writings of thorough *polemical* and *philological* learning. He remained at Rostock and refused many honourable calls — for instance to Hamburg — and often served princes, consistories, and cities through written *[Consilia].*

In this great work-field he wore himself out swiftly, given his fragile body. The funeral-sermon says with reason: *"What has brought him so early under the earth? His far too great solicitude for your soul-health. To death he has studied and meditated."* Already in the winter of 1669 he lay dangerously ill; the Lord rescued him once more, and in **1670** he wrote in thanksgiving his ***Geistlicher Dank-Altar.*** In **1675,** however, after 8 months of painful [sickness], he died of a completely scorbutic dissolution of all the humours. Shortly before his death he received in deepest humility and devotion the Holy [Supper], sang [his own last-hymn] [——], and preached, on the text that he had chosen himself, [——].

His many writings — all written in a *masterly style,* in a *sap-and-power-full, picture-rich prose* — are to this day spread among the Christian folk, brought near in new-print editions. One can easily distinguish four *groups* of them:

**Group I. Those which use the Scripture-word practically, sometimes single verses, sometimes teachings.**
**Group II. Those which take their starting-point from certain Scripture-passages.**
**Group III. Those which contain re-workings of other writings.**
**Group IV. His sermon-books.**

#### Group I — the *Geistliche Erquickstunden*

At the head of Group I stands ***Dr. Heinrich Müllers Geistliche Erquickstunden oder 300 Haus- und Tisch-Andachten*** (1664 and 1666 — first 150, then 250, then 300 devotions). In them all the advantages of Müller's own-kind come together, according to content and form. He has an admirable gift of observing the *situations of practical, everyday life* and setting them into the *centre of all faith and life,* in a folk-tune-like, primary-German manner that the simplest man understands, and yet the highest-educated also. **Beck** especially praised the *period-construction* which Müller so gladly uses:²¹⁵ because in his speech the *character of the pregnant* is preserved. All the more do these short sentences take on force when Müller — [with his knowledge of the Cross] — combats [world and] *[sham-devotion];* [——] "the *World* and *the Salvation* — those are the two poles between which the thoughts move." — Its first modern editor, **Peter Rupmwurm** at Ratzeburg 1822, says of it:

> *In spirit and form to Jesus Sirach and even to the Solomonic Sayings not unlike, they contain a treasury both of inner and of outer experiences and of Christian life-wisdom, in an earnest, living Trost- and Straf-tone, in a peculiar consecrated, hearty, sometimes allegorizing wit, and in a wholly own-quirked, with salt seasoned, short, marrow-strong speech, full of oil and light on the dark life-way.*

The great contrast **between God the [——], the Blessed, and the sinful, wretched world** dominates his view. Through this struggle the Christian is to strive through, with heavenly homesickness. All things he is to judge according to the *Word of Truth,* and from the standpoint of Eternity; therefore to preach against all worldly-lust, worldly-blessedness — as against sham-Christianity and dead works. Müller wrote the meditations as *house- and table-devotions,* and thereby desires to invite the Lord into the house, that at morning, noon, and evening he might be our Guest and remain, blessing all his gifts and graces upon us and teaching us the right *Gratias.* Earnest admonitions, friendly encouragements, quieting comforts alternate one with another.

How significant the book was from all beginning is proved by this: the ***Pegnitzschäfer of Nuremberg*** used it as a *model for their poetry* and poetically re-worked it. Thus there appeared them in **110 hymns of 29 shepherds,** 1673 and 1691.²¹⁶ One of these hymns on the devotion No. 280, ***"Wunderlich weislich..."*** [is preserved]; another — ***"Fürchterlicher Anfang, herrlich Ende"*** — is by **Stockfleth** (†1708 as *Generalsuperintendent* [——]). The *devotional-form* is the following: to each meditation there stands a *superscription,* then a short saying-like *proverb;* the *inner connexion* in the arrangement, however, is not to be discerned.

**Sample subject-mottos:**

- No. 300, *On the Love of Jesus:* ***"Meinen Jesum lass ich nicht!"***
- No. 261, *On Humility:* ***"Downward!"***
- No. 64, *On the End of Suffering:* ***"The measure is full."***
- No. 40, *On the Manner of Faith and Love:* ***"Ever unrestful. Never restful."***
- No. 24, *On perseverance in Good and Evil:* ***"Werden is good, bleiben even better; but Werden is bad, bleiben even worse."***
- (early number), *On the Friendship of the World:* ***"Nothing about nothing."***

Editions of the *Erquickstunden* in modern print:

1. **Hildesheim / Sinhuber, 1872.** With portrait of Müller. 512 pp., gr. 8°, narrow-medium print. Bd. 1.20 Mk.
2. **Hamburg, Agentur des Rauhen Hauses, 1889** — the *widest-distributed, best edition* according to print and paper. 16°, XII + 604 pp., 32 lines to the page. Preface with Müller's life-course (I–XII), the 300 devotions (1–575), Gospel-and-Epistle register (576–586), alphabetical subject-register (587–596), customary register (597–604). Paper 1.60 Mk., bound 1.80 Mk.
3. **Berlin, Evang. Hauptverein für christliche Erbauungsschriften.** 8°, XIV + 488 pp., larger and wider print. Paper 1 Mk., leather with gilt edge 3 Mk. Pp. II–V Müller's life; VI–XIV a preface *in Müller's own style* explaining the significance. Register-schema pp. 401–438.

#### Group II — the *Kreuz-, Buß- und Betschule* and the *Geistlicher Dank-Altar*

**1.** The ***Kreuz-, Buß- und Betschule aus Psalm 143*** (**1661** — thus somewhat earlier than the *Erquickstunden*) was Müller's own most-suffered book: he wrote it, as he says, after seven years' pastoral experience, and after much cross of his own. Modern editions:

- **Hildesheim / Sinhuber, 1872,** re-edited by Pastor Ludwig Siegmund. 294 pp., sm. 16°.
- **Hamburg / Rauhes Haus, 1858.** XL + 216 pp. two-columned. Preface at pp. VII–XL (Rostock, July 1844) speaks of the book as *a means to learn to pray:* — "There await thee here not the smooth [phrase-makers] of the present, who — without wholly uncovering thy heart-damage and calling thee wholly to repentance, grace, and reconciliation — flatteringly speak to thy natural man, only [to arouse some religious feelings which will not remain]. It is the *simple, mighty, but overflowingly lively and pungent speech of the old time* — inspired by the Holy Ghost and filled with his whole power. This speech cuts deep into thy heart; it takes all and destroys all of thy natural man; but it gives all again to the one born-again in repentance and grace — peace and joy in time and in eternity. It is the right *food* of which we have need, that we may be healed not on the surface but in the ground of the heart, and truly newly awakened in the Lord."

The book, after a thorough deep exposition of Ps. 143, unfolds in **22 meditations** on the close connexion of Repentance and Prayer with the Cross: to Verse 1 belong three (the golden art of prayer / the hearing of prayer / the golden faith-articles of a fervent pray-er); to Verse 2 three (the wrath-judgement / self-judgement / the common cross-refuge); further meditations on the [spiritual] school of the Cross, on the lively comfort of the Spirit, on rescue from anguish, on prayer against the enemies, on God's [——]. Müller here denies for once his usual short, terse manner: a *broader, more didactic* tone reigns, without wearying.

**2.** ***Dr. Heinrich Müllers Geistlicher Dank-Altar zum täglichen Lob-Opfer der Christen*** (1669/70) — new edition: **Barmen,** Verlag der Missionshandlung, 1885, edited by **Theodor Hardt** on his own sickbed and deathbed. This precious book, composed on his own recovery from severe illness, ties itself to *Ps. 68:20–21* and offers **18 glorious devotions** according to Müller's manner — the fullness of edifying God-thoughts, under ever-new points of view, tied to the individual words of the few divine words. To *"Blessed be the Lord daily"* belong the 3rd devotion; to *"God lays a [——] upon us"* the 3rd and 16th; to *"He burdens himself with our sorrows, and burdens us with salvation"* the 13th and 18th — then to [——] the [prayers on regeneration, Holy Supper, and blessedness]. It is a book of *longer devotions for still hours in good and evil days.*

**3.** ***Dr. Heinrich Müllers Thränen- und Trostquelle, oder Weinender Heiland und Sünder*** — Müller's *dying-year book* of 1675. Re-edited by **G. Schmidt,** Pastor at [Mittenwald], Halle 1858. 20 sermons on the great sinner-woman of Luke 7:36ff., unfolding the encounter of Christ with the penitent Magdalene: on the blind Pharisee (11); the meek Jesus and the obeying Pharisee (12); the wretched debtor and the merciful Lord (13); the friendly Jesus (14); the loveless Pharisee and the love-rich sinner (15); the loved and living pardoned sinner (18); the [tempted], the blessed, and the [she] who goes in peace (20). *— Note:* The 1659 tract of Müller, ***Himmlischer Liebeskuß*** (later expanded as *Himmlische Liebesflamme*), which derives the *whole of Christian doctrine from the love of God* (as Lütkemann had derived it from the *taste* of God's goodness in his *Vorschmack*), was re-issued in Hamburg *(Rauhes Haus)* in 1848 but is now out of print.

#### Group III — the *Gnade und Wahrheit* schools of *Schatzkästlein* extracts

Group III is formed by **compilations and re-workings.** Standing at the head: **Dr. Adalbert Jäger's** re-work — ***Dr. Heinrich Müllers Gnade und Wahrheit: Erbauliches Schatzkästlein in kurzen Betrachtungen auf jeden Tag im Jahr aus den Schriften desselben ausgewählt und zusammengestellt.*** Stuttgart, 1850, Chr. Belser (G. Walter). Gr. 8°, IV + 512 pp. 2.10 Mk. — a **daily-devotional Schatzkästlein** drawn from all of Müller's works. Jäger's preface (pp. II–IV) programmatically calls the book a *Präservativ wider den Schaden Josephs in allen [Gegenden]* [Amos 6:6] — a preservative against the ruin of Joseph in every quarter. The 365 daily devotions follow a **Brenz-catechism-thought-sequence:**

- Jan. 1–29: *Baptism*
- Jan. 30 – Feb. 5: *Faith in the Triune God*
- (13 March – June): *Christ,* corresponding to the church-year
- Sept. 6 – Oct. 14: *Prayer* and the [Lord's Prayer]
- Oct. 15 – Dec. 16: *the Ten Commandments*
- Dec. 17–23: *the Holy Supper* and the *Keys of the Kingdom*
- Dec. 24–31: *[Death and Eternity]*
- With special sections for Advent, Christmas, [Epiphany, Passion week, Easter, Ascension, Pentecost, Trinity, All-Saints']

Also in Group III: **Heinrich Müllers erbauliche Auslegung der Leidensgeschichte JEsu Christi nebst Trost und Rat für Kranke,** ed. Viktor Warlich, Stuttgart, Belser, 1858 — 6 meditations on the Passion (pp. 1–105), *Trost und Rat für Kranke* (106–366) drawn from Müller with supplements from Luther, Scriver, and the editor.

#### Group IV — the sermon-books: the *Schlußketten*

Group IV is formed by the ***Schlußketten*** — sermon-cycles. **Guitav Leonhardi** in the *Predigt der Kirche*-series issued a selection of Müller's sermons on 159 pages with an *orienting monograph.* The four *Schlußketten* are also all available in cheap re-prints:

**a. Evangelische Schlußkette und Kraftkern** (1672) — 53 sermons on the Sunday Gospels of the church-year. Große cites, with sample-motifs: on the Transfiguration (Matt. 17:1–10) — *Heaven on Earth in the Glorification of Christ;* on the Blind Bartimaeus (Luke 18:41–48) — *The Honey-Flowerlet of the Poor Sinner;* on the Miraculous Draught (Luke 5:1–12) — *The Golden Fishing-Art;* on the Ten Lepers (Luke 17:11–20) — *Christ's Wonder-Help to the Ten Lepers.* The 1855 Halle edition by G. A. Bandermann omits the long text-introductions and polemic and Latin-quotations, keeping Müller's *classical originality of speech* almost unchanged except for the very outdated syntactic constructions.

**b. Festevangelische Schlußkette und Kraftkern** (1673) — 17 sermons for the 3 Festival-days, plus Epiphany, Ascension, New Year, John the Baptist, Michaelmas, etc.

**c. Apostolische Schlußkette und Kraftkern** (1663–1668 / 1671) — 71 sermons on all the Sunday and Feast-day Epistles. Standard modern edition: **Dr. [Vitzsch]cher,** Preacher and Adjunct at [——], Neuruppin 1855, VIII + 668 pp. *Große quotes at length:*

> From beginning to end the sermons bear the *unmistakable stamp of a spirit that never grows worn out* — the stamp of the *individual own-mode* under the various and finest arts of form. The words are alive: they are pearls that he has found in the depth of a *God-hidden life;* they are *pure crystal-drops* — not needing his brand — whose water reflects heaven's clarity. Free of every fear-of-man, the man of God rebukes the *ungodly essence* in whichever guise it may appear before him, and his prophetic earnestness strikes his own and our time with equal sharpness. And yet he is *mild* when it is a matter of lifting up the smitten heart, and pointing it to the *fountain of healing.* His word is nourished from the Scripture and drives clearly and understandingly into the [heart]. Wonderful is the depth with which he grasps [the Scripture-passages] in their significance for the [inner life]; surprising is the application which he knows to give to the [known truths] for the shaking or the raising of the *Gemüt.* The rare skill of setting *learning and experience* into immediate relation to the Scripture-word, and of letting all *means of formation reflect themselves in the simple ray of Scripture,* also holds up his speech throughout at the height of that *noble popularity* which makes it as fitting for the highly-educated as for the countryman.

**d. Gräber der [Heiligen]** (1684, posthumous) — funeral-sermon collection.

#### The Evangelischer Herzensspiegel (posthumous, 1679)

The **widest-distributed** of all Müller's works from an early date (1679, edited by **Johann Kaspar Heunisch**) is the ***Evangelischer Herzensspiegel.*** New edition: **Hamburg, Rauhes Haus, 1882 and 1884** (6th unchanged impression). Two volumes:

- **Vol. I** — Gospel-sermons (Heunisch's edition), pp. 1–588.
- **Vol. II** — Epistle-sermons (Cannmann's edition), pp. 1–204.

Sermon-cycle appearing after Müller's death. It contains his *best sermons* — full of thoughts in close attachment to the exposition of the Scripture-word, and in a clear, pure, noble speech. The exposition is *analytic* — with a summing superscription and [a running division]. [——] "Not that I in preaching [aim at nothing but flowery-speech], but rather to make the reader take pleasure and to lead the lover of ancient piety to such *allegories* which not only do no violence to the fine [text] but at the same time are comforting and useful." The edifying breadth which now and then wearies in Arndt, and even in Lütkemann, one forgets [in Müller]. He opens the Scripture on all sides in often surprising trains of thought, and this holds one fast. In that he also uses passages which for edification at first glance seem to offer little, he is a *forerunner* of the manner of the *American-English preachers* — as [today's] evangelistic preaching demonstrates. Also he *individualises* highly. In his speech only occasional foreign-words of his own age fall on our modern ear (*Dexterität, alamodisch,* etc.), or his liking for pointing to *"Stanf und Stinken"* [as a strong image] [——]. On the Epistle-sermons, compare Lütkemann's *Apostolische Aufmunterung.*

*Note I.* From the Buchhandlung des [Erweckungs-Vereins] in Neukirchen near Mörs, since 1898 there has also been a new *Reihenausgabe* of Müller's works appearing (publisher Richard Kühne, Oberhausen; bound 6 Mk.). — *Note II.* In **Klaibers Volksbibliothek** IV, 222–302, **Pfarrer Wild** at Kirchheim gives *Müller's life and a selection of his writings.*²¹⁷

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §5. Christian Scriver (1629–1693)

*Source: II b) §5, pp. 252–277. The **largest entry** in the whole *Wegweiser* and the third and greatest of the *Großen* — Rendsburg-born, Rostock-trained under Lütkemann; 15 years pastor at Stendal (from 1653); 23 years pastor at Magdeburg (1667–1690); finally three years as *Oberhof-Prediger* and *Konsistorialrat* at Quedlinburg (1690–1693). Author of the ***Seelenschatz*** (5 vols., 1675–1693 — 1,965 folio pp., 35 years' work), the ***Gottholds zufällige Andachten*** (300 devotions 1669; 400 devotions 1674), the *Chrysologia catechetica,* and many funeral- and single-occasion sermons. Große treats Scriver as the *widest-read, most-comprehensive, most-beloved* Lutheran devotional writer of the age — surpassing every predecessor in *scope* and standing at the very *peak* of the Blossom-Time literary height.*

**Christian Scriver**²¹⁸ was born on **2 January 1629 at Rendsburg** in the Duchy of Holstein. His father, **Christian Schriver,** lived there as an *honourable and God-fearing merchant;* his mother, **Abigail Cube,** was a daughter of the local *Ratsherr* Michael Cube. The time of his birth fell in the war-atrocities of the Wallenstein army, which laid the town severely low. His mother had dedicated him to the *service of the LORD* still before his birth, and gave him for that reason the name *Christianus.*²¹⁹ *Three miraculous preservations* of his childhood — narrated by Scriver himself in the ***Seelenschatz*** III, cap. 108, §§ 21–23 (Vol. 2, pp. 74–76), and sung in his hymn ***"Jesu, meiner Seele Leben"*** — are:²²⁰

1. The three-year-old boy fell into a well in the [——]; but was pulled out unharmed.
2. On the Sabbath-day of the following year a raging dog fell upon him; his walking-stick broke; but the dog was struck down.
3. On another occasion he fell down [the church-tower stairs] without harm, and once again a heavy [beam] fell just before his feet [and did not touch him].

Wonderfully thus the LORD preserved him before deep falls. — His formation-course went through the pious and learned *Probst* **G. Kuhlmann,** an [uncle-ward] of the theosophist **Joachim Morsius,** in whom Scriver — after his father's death — found a new father-figure; but in **1635** he became fatherless again. In this need his mother's rich elder cousin, the merchant **Thomas Rosenkranz [Rosenauer],** came in, and bequeathed to Christian **900 Taler,** that he might study. With these means Scriver attended the schools of Rendsburg (until 1645) and of Lübeck (until 1647) for his preparation. At Lübeck he taught in the widow's house of his benefactor the three [daughters] [——].

On **9 October 1647** Scriver moved to Rostock for university study. **Mauritius, Quistorp,** and **Lütkemann** became his teachers in Theology; **Andwas [Andreas Tscherning]** in German language and Poetics.²²¹ Luther's writings he read with zeal, and *Arndt's Paradiesgärtlein* became from that time forward his dearest devotional-book. **Heinrich Müller** he did not meet, since Müller was then in Greifswald. In **1649** he became *Magister;* in **1650** *Hauslehrer* [private tutor] with Joachim [——] [at Stendal]. From there he traveled often to Stendal, where in 1652 his sister Lucia Kuhlmann had married the *Konrektor* **Triceus.** In consequence he was called on **11 May 1653** to serve as *Archidiaconus* at **St. Jakobi in Stendal.** On the Sunday [following] he entered his office; but life soon began there to grow bitter for him. On **10 [June] 1653** he wedded **Anna Schmieden,** daughter of the *Generalsuperintendent* [Georg Schmiedt of Stendal], who died already in **1654** at her first childbirth. In **February 1655** he wedded **her sister** [second wife], who also died soon after.

Magdeburg still lay after the horrifying destruction by **Tilly** (1631) sorely down. In **1667** Scriver was called there — for **23 years** it became now the *main scene of his spiritual office.* In 1674 he became *Assessor* of the spiritual court; in 1676 *Scholar;* in 1679 *Senior* of the spiritual ministry; in 1685 further *Kirchen-Inspector* over the *Holzkreis* with 40 pastorates. In Magdeburg Scriver unfolded his mighty preaching-activity as *"the fearless Elbe-preacher without turning-point."*²²² Notwithstanding his weak, gentle voice, — as his contemporary described him — *"the sluices of the life-stream opened up, and the floods of divine thoughts rose in surge; lightning-bolt on lightning-bolt gleamed forth, and the thunders rolled over the stream of his words, so that everyone had to listen to his speech as though under a spell."* Alongside he unfolded a sacrificial pastoral care.

In this Magdeburg-time Scriver also wrote his greatest and most-comprehensive writings. In **1669** appeared the ***Gottholds 300 zufällige Andachten;*** in **1674** they were increased by a fourth hundred. In **1675** appeared the first two parts of his imperishable ***Seelenschatz,*** which he had held in 1674 more briefly as week-sermons; Part III followed in **1678,** Part IV in **1681,** Part V in **1688** (Frankfurt) and **1692** (Leipzig). *The whole work, on which he laboured 35 years, and which encompasses 1,965 folio-pages, appeared complete only in 1693.*²²³ In 1684 [he gave out further single-sermon writings and the *Chrysologia catechetica*].

Amid this the LORD chastened him further. On **6 November** [1682] the Lord took to himself his third wife, [Anna, daughter of the former Bürgermeister,] after [some years] of happy marriage. On **28 [——] 1683**, as he wrote the 4th part of the *Seelenschatz* on *"Cross"*, new [suffering] came upon him: in **1679** his 23-year-old daughter, from his second marriage, [died]. In **1685** his third daughter [died also]. The LORD rescued him again from severe illness — his vow made in illness he fulfilled: he issued *Chrysologia catechetica* (fewer than 1685) as a source of comfort. On **19 October 1681** he entered — mightily, aged 52, longing for eternal rest — into his **fourth marriage,** with the daughter of the [Kurfürstlich Brandenburgischen] *Rittmeister at Fortress Spandau.* The *Sen-inspectorate* over the 40 Holzkreis-parishes was still further laid upon him.

Amid these many worldly-cares his *spiritual work* began to appear to him too much curtailed. In **1689** he asked **Spener** in a letter for a *Gutachten,* whether he should leave Magdeburg [for a lighter charge]. When shortly thereafter the *Äbtissin of the *weltliches Stift* Quedlinburg,* **Sophie Dorothea,** offered him her Court-preacher-office and *Konsistorialrat* dignity, Scriver accepted (having earlier declined a call to Brandenburg in 1676 and to become chaplain to the Queen [Ulrike] of Charles XI of Sweden in 1679). On **3 October 1690** he held his farewell-sermon at Magdeburg on **Gal. 6:14–16.** On 18 May 1690 [he entered Quedlinburg]. In **February 1693** he took ill — on 23 February [his] Sunday [pulpit-service was interrupted], so that he had to be brought home. On **4 March 1693** he received the Holy Supper; his hand was still weak. He was asked whether he had reconciled himself in the heart; he answered *"He is a friendly, kindly, dear God."* On the morning of **5 April 1693,** after having prayed the *Arndt-blessed-departure prayer,* he rejoiced: ***"I am glad!"*** — and prayed further: ***"Let me be and remain Thine, O Thou faithful God."*** Then he spoke nothing more and went softly to sleep. On **11 April 1693** he was buried at *St. [Katharinen-Kirche] Magdeburg* at the side of his three first wives. On **20 April** at Quedlinburg the Superintendent M. **Calvisius** preached his funeral-sermon on Ps. 37:4–5, and the *Gymnasialrektor* **Schmidt** on 2 Sam. 3:35. Of his 10 children survived him only *one son of the second marriage* — a merchant at Kiel — and *the only daughter of the last marriage,* **Katharina Elisabeth,** the later spouse of the Superintendent **Polykarp Leyser at Merseburg.**

To this day there stands in Magdeburg in the [St. Jakobi-Kirche] **Scriver's life-size portrait** with the motto **1 Cor. 15:10:** *"Not I, but the grace of God."*²²⁴

**Scriver's spirit and work were blessed through prayer.** His life was a **life of prayer.** Early at 4 or 5 o'clock he prayed one hour; at 9 o'clock in the evening he performed with his household, kneeling, his prayer *out of the heart.* Often he sought also *solitude* and prayed with loud voice. In summer he gladly chose the *garden* as the place of prayer. He gladly let himself be called by the **prayer-bell** to prayer. In child-like humility and *confidingness* he spoke with his Father. His *motto* from the year 1670 was 2 Cor. 6:9: **"Als die Sterbenden, und siehe, wir leben"** ("As dying, and behold, we live").

*As a writer* Scriver is **a painter without equal** — with his classical, clear speech which moves the heart into godly-fear, a bell-clear sound. *"The Christian religion lives"* in his eyes; and *no one has written on the Christian religion in so many writings [of every kind]* [across the ages]. **Far surpasses he all who came before him.** His life, rich in cross, fits him best to comfort; and yet the same life-experience has enabled him to write for the most various life-situations of Christians. The Christian in him finds nourishment for every side. **Church Fathers and historians,** spiritual and worldly *poets,* world-, nature-, and church-history — all are drawn in with *the Christian measure.* Everything serves in his writings to bring home the *Bible-truth* penetratingly and clearly. **Deepened by the Scripture, and measured against it** — out of the same he wins a *fullness of thoughts.* He stands *solid and sober on the Lutheran Confession.* **Justification through faith** is the heart-point of his teaching. Word and Sacrament are the *source of the outworking of the inwardness of Christianity.* [Bert] says that *next to Luther* one finds in Scriver **the most-excellent** [material] that has been written on the Sacraments. In this Scriver agrees also with Arndt, and especially in his speech. Though he lacks the *gripping, terse, high-poetic manner of Müller,* yet he never wearies in his longer sentences and never becomes *wooden* or *unclear.* All is well-articulated and translucent. In fullness of thought and depth he is **without equal.** High and low understand him ever. He is not only *the most significant appearance in Christian devotional literature,* but he is *significant in German literature and its history* — and (with Lütkemann and Müller) still much too little appreciated. In an age of *general decay of the German language,* he writes — with admirable mastery — **on the height of the age** [an unaffected, folk-tune-like, richly-imaged German]. It appears in him the reflecting influence of the *Lutheran Bible* on the man who has *found his model in her* and thereby stayed preserved from the *aping-lust of foreign forms.*

Let us hear him at last himself on his speech and manner (Preface to Vol. 1, 26 April 1675):

> I can truly say that the honest, well-meant purpose of all my thoughts, comparisons, narratives, teachings, and meditations is the **honour of my dear God** and the **edification of my so-dearly-entrusted hearers.** And to this end I am wont, whatever I find in old and new writings among the God- and world-scholars, jurists, doctors, and philosophers, to note diligently, and on given occasion, with mature reflection, to apply — as a gardener and lover of flowers transplants a noble herb and beautiful *[Blümchen]* from the wild field into his pleasure-garden.

And again from the same Preface:

> Souls it is, my Father! Souls have I sought! Grant to my soul the joy that what I have here written may be read with profit by many souls and be of service. Let this work be a *spring-fountain out of the wells of Israel* and out of the fountain of Thy love, thither directed to Thy Church-garden, that the noble flowers and fruits that grow therein may thrive, bloom, smell, and moisten. — Oh, that we, who confess and hold the *old apostolic teaching,* might through the same become **new creatures in Christ Jesus!** Ah, my God, let this Scripture through Thy blessing everywhere be *hot* [heiss]. Grant that all who read it, they may sense the power in the same, feel Thy Spirit, taste, enjoy Thy comfort, and be made cheerful to do Thy all-holy will.

And once more (from the same Preface — Scriver's own defense of his method):

> I have with faithful diligence [gathered]; no *new and unheard of* [thing] have I in a matter piled up; sometimes have I *one motive with another,* *one comparison with another* joined; and *one thing with some other* set forward — all with the intention that the [instruction and comfort might reach the] children of God, that they be *strengthened.* [——] I am accustomed to serve out with several dishes [Schüsseln].... Otherwise I have throughout [taken care] to speak *Scripture-conform* with the confession-books and with the famous and tested theologians of the ev.-Lutheran Church. And though I do not hope that in this work the least is what does not conform to the pattern of healthy words, yet I have before my God testified, that I have never had the *own intention* to write anything that departs from the unanimous opinion of his true-believing Church and that could burden the same with innovations. Yet I will nevertheless publicly declare, that if anywhere something has escaped me as a *weak man* in writing, that is not conformable to Holy Scripture and to the old confessional creeds, to the *unchanged Augsburg Confession* and to the ***Concordia-Book,*** I will not recognize such as my own, but on the first reminder of a godly Christian heart, gladly change it — to which end I herewith commit this work along with all my other writings to the *true right-believing Church for oversight and judgement.* Finally I wish from the ground of my heart, that the gracious and merciful God this to him sanctified labour would so bless, that thereby his glory-worthiest and most-holy name from many 1,000 souls might be *highly praised.*

In treating the modern editions of Scriver's writings four groups can be distinguished: **I. the *Seelenschatz* and its re-workings; II. the smaller devotions; III. the compilations from all writings; IV. the sermons.**

#### Group I — The *Seelenschatz* (1675–1693)

The Master-work is the ***Seelenschatz.*** Published: **1675** Vols. I & II Leipzig; **1678** Vol. III Magdeburg; **1681** Vol. IV Frankfurt; **1688** (Frankfurt) and **1692** (Leipzig) Vol. V; the whole in **1693,** Leipzig. Later often re-issued — e.g. **1744 Magdeburg** (Pritius, 2 vols. folio); with excerpts as **1697 Chr. Koch** *"Des Seelenschatzes Kern und Marck,"* Leipzig 4°; **1704 Wittenberg** *"Seelenschatzes Kraft und Saft, oder geistreiche und bewegliche Seelenandachten aus des wohlseligen Herrn Autoris grösserem Werke und mit dessen eigenen Worten..."*; **Magdeburg 1745.** Two full-editions still today available.

- **Vol. IV** (in 18 *Betrachtungen*): the *Seelenschatz* of *afflicted souls* — a *treasure-mine* for cross-bearers and for those who have to comfort in the pastoral office; note the headings: [Of the sickness-months of the] pious, the causes of the high spiritual assaults, the *comfort against the high spiritual assaults*, alone Bd. 3 pp. 116–[——]. *In Scriver's treatments of the Cross belong to the greatest and finest that ever has been written on this subject,* and are worth becoming ever again known. All is written *thoroughly in sound casuistry* — as e.g. Sermon 2, *Of the Cross in Marriage,* proves: cross of an ill-formed marriage, of a *childless* marriage, of *dying* children, of *sickly* children, of *[unruly]* children, and *sorrow of godly widows and widowers.*
- **Vol. V** (in 11 sermons): ***"Of the blessed departure of the believing soul out of the body, the triumphant entry into Heaven, and eternal joy, glory, and blessedness."*** Bd. 3, pp. 490–1021 — an entire book on the *Last Things,* stronger than his predecessors Meyfart and Nikolai.

The whole work gives an incomparable — in content as in form — presentation of the way of the *human soul of a God-child from God, into sin, out of sin through repentance and faith into the Christian life, patient suffering, up to God through the blessed death.* **Karl Krieg,** Scriver's best modern biographer,²²⁴ summarizes the content:

> He describes the way of the soul from her misery into the glory of *Eternal Life.* Starting from the *nobility of our soul,* Scriver leads us through the *death-valley of human need,* over the *steep mountain of Repentance,* to the *light-height of Faith,* along the *path of Life-in-Faith,* past all Christian virtues, through the middle of the *sea of tribulations and heaviest assaults,* before the door of Death and through it to the *blessedness by the Lord in the city of the golden streets.*

Modern editions and re-workings:

- **1. Riehm's *Predigten aus Scrivers Seelenschatz auf jeden Sonntag des Jahres bearbeitet***, ed. **Heinrich Riehm,** pastor at Rötteln (Basel and Leipzig, Königsitz 1887; VIII + 416 pp.; 3rd volume of the *"Predigten für häusliche Erbauung"* series). 52 sermons drawn from the *Seelenschatz,* each eight pages. Riehm in his preface: *"The strength of feeling gives the whole presentation an never-fading life-freshness, out of which every Christian in every generation can edify and refresh himself."* The 52 sermons are word-extracts from the *Seelenschatz,* re-set into the *frame of a sixty-page sermon* for each Sunday of the church-year, with a summary-superscription. Riehm's language is at times *modernised,* without disturbing Scriver's own-kind.
- **2. Die Heilsordnung: Betrachtungen zu kirchlichem und häuslichem Gebrauche aus Scrivers Seelenschatz ausgezogen** von **M. Westermayer,** pastor at *Sidersheim.* Nördlingen, C. Beck, 1865 (Stuttgarter Evang. Gesellschaft). VI + 338 pp. — a shorter re-work for *church prayer-hours* and for private-devotional use.
- **3. Anleitung zum wahren Glauben, gottseligen Wandel, geduldigem Leiden, erhörlichem Gebet und seligem Sterben** — from Scriver's *Seelenschatz* extracted by **M. Viktor August Häger,** pastor. 3rd ed., Belser, Stuttgart 1876. VIII + 710 pp., 37 lines to the page, gr. 8° — the largest single extract-volume.

#### Group II — Gottholds *Zufällige Andachten* (1663 / 1669 / 1671)

Scriver's **second-most-significant** work is the ***Gottholds zufällige Andachten*** — the "chance-devotions" of the shepherd *Gotthold* (a Scriver-persona formed on the archaic *Gott-hold* = *dear-to-God*). The first hundred appeared already in **1663:** *"On beholding manifold Things of Art and Nature, in various intention to the bettering of the Mind and the exercise of Godliness, set forth and drafted by M. C. Scriver."* The second and third hundred followed in **1669;** the fourth in **1671.** By **1724** already 19 editions had appeared; that of 1724 at Leipzig was the 21st edition. Newer editions came in **1744** and **1752** at Leipzig. After the dedication of 16 August 1671 to the *Triune God* — in which Scriver speaks of *his own suffering at the time of the composition* — the work presents **400 spiritual meditations on earthly matters.** Here shows itself Scriver's **nature-sense and nature-love:** every appearance in world, in life, in nature has to him *something to say for God's Kingdom* — becomes for him an image of some *fact,* some *event* in Heaven or [Earth]. In simple, but *from holy enthusiasm dared* speech, he lets *all things in the world* speak to *God's honour* — sometimes for teaching, sometimes for comfort, sometimes for warning.²²⁵ Beck's own appreciation stands at the head of Scriver's preface:

> Scriver develops in these devotions a talent to make out God's greatness, wisdom, endless love in a leaf on the tree, in every flower on the way; teaching, comforting, warning as the messenger of God — and all so unsought, so childlike, as scarcely something similar in the whole range of our devotional books could be found.

Another man of God compares this collection to *"a rich diamond-decor of genuine, noble stones, in nature's fresh embellishment, that reflect Heaven."* Professor **Hagenbach** in his lectures said of it: *"A book that we may without hesitation number among the finest of our folk-devotional books" — "We may with the truly-poetic appearances on the religious literature-field reckon it."*

Scriver himself, in the dedication, discloses his intent:

> I would gladly, my [Reader], through this book let *all creatures speak with thee;* I would gladly *interpret and translate* to thee the *secret language of nature,* and show thee, in my simplicity, how the various things — thou canst make into a remembrance of thy God, a *bettering of thy Christianity.*

He ties his method to **Balaam's ass** (Num. 22), to **Job 12:7–8,** and to Wisdom 6, and says: *"The book of Nature has many leaves, on which the finger of God has written his love. The LORD scatters these leaves round about everywhere, that we may not be lacking in occasion to consider his high, deep, wide, and broad goodness. Blessed is he who reads them with godly reflection."* — His forerunners in the *reading of Nature* he names as Adam, Noah, Abraham, David — and above all *the LORD himself* (cf. John 4:6 ff., John 15:1 ff., Luke 14:16). Especially to *preachers* Scriver directs a word:

> Perhaps also many a preacher, when he finds himself in company, at feasts, on walks or on journeys, may take the trouble to entertain his hearers each time in such or similar ways with pleasant and yet useful conversations, and to *edify their Christianity.* May he in this way but pitch his pulpit everywhere where need requires it and his duty demands it. One can not only in, but also outside the church, in particular tell each one the truth — only it must happen with *godly and rational modesty,* which is the right seasoning of such reminders and teachings.

The Basel edition of the *Zufällige Andachten* gives, after Scriver's dedication and preface, on pp. 1–366, the **366 devotions.** A short abridgement for the youth: ***Jugendspiegel aus Gottholds zufälligen Andachten*** von M. Christ. Scriver, with an appendix of *prayers for the Confirmed* by **Christoph Stähelin** (weiland Pfarrer at St. Gallen), issued afresh Belser Stuttgart, 3rd ed. 1878, IV + 80 pp. — 44 shortened devotions for youth, with corresponding hymn-verses, followed by five prayers of Stähelin in fervent prayer-speech.

#### Group III — Compilations from all Scriver's writings

Beside the three chief works, Scriver also composed still some smaller writings — e.g. ***Das verlorene und wiedergefundene Schäflein*** (1672); posthumously (1696) ***Witwentrost an eine vornehme Witwe, beim Absterben ihres Eheherrn gefertigt*** (already written in 1672 at Magdeburg); a *Bet-Altar* which is now printed together with **Schmolck's** *Devotions* (see the Schmolck-entry below in Tranche 7). Two further compilation-books remain today in print:²²⁶

**1.** ***Der Hausfreund*** von Christian Scriver. Aus dessen Schriften, mit Beigaben von **Martin Luther, Johann Arndt,** und **Heinrich Müller,** zusammengestellt von M. Viktor A. Jäger, weiland Pfarrer in Köngen. 6th ed. Stuttgart, Belser, 1892. XI + 516 pp., 36 lines to the page. 2 Mk.; cloth 2.80 Mk.; half-leather 3.20 Mk. — Jäger's principal sources: Scriver's ***Die heilige und Gott wohlgefällige Haushaltung zur Erbauung und Besserung der im Hauswesen fürlaufenden Mängel*** (10th ed. Frankfurt 1734, first ed. 1686), the *Seelenschatz,* and — for the first section *"Whence take we bread?"* — the smaller work ***Die güldene Kunst reich zu werden.*** Jäger stresses how Scriver's own life-path fitted him especially to be *counsellor for domestic life.* The volume is organised in 14 sections: **I.** The best household [——]; **III.** The Spouses; **IV.** Children and Servants; **VII.** The Neighbourhood; **IX.** The Trade; **X.** The Sunday (with many morning- and evening-prayers for the Church-year); **XI.** The Holy Supper (with a full week's preparation-prayers); **XII.** Spiritual [distress] of a household-member; **XIII.** Sicknesses; **XIV.** On Dying (in which the dyings of Luther, Arndt, and Scriver are portrayed).

**2.** ***Christian Scrivers erbauliches Beicht- und Kommunionbuch,*** nebst einem Anhang täglicher Morgen- und Abendgebete, 2nd expanded ed., Ernst Bredt, 1864. Kl. 8°, VII + 224 pp. 75 Pf. — Already in 1768 **Paul Simons** had drawn from the *Seelenschatz* a *Buß-, Beicht- und Kommunionbuch,* which is here newly issued by **P. G. Leonhardi.** Pp. 1–96 give longer and shorter prayers of Scriver in his own sense and spirit from the 17th century; sections: **I.** In days of preparation for confession and Supper; confession-prayers; **III.** The Holy Supper: [instruction on 1 Cor. 11:23–32]; **IV.** Morning- and evening-[prayers] and hymns; morning- and evening-prayers for the whole week, a daily morning- and evening-blessing, various *renewals of the baptismal covenant* from Luther, Melanchthon, Habermann, out of the *Rigaischen Sammlung,* and from **Löhe's** collections.

#### Group IV — The Sermons

The last group is formed by Scriver's **sermon-collections,** which also found wide distribution as devotional-books.²²⁷

**1.** ***Chrysologia catechetica, oder Goldpredigten*** — "in which the saving Catechism-teaching is summarised most briefly and explained by means of the *color of gold.*" Leipzig, 1663 [OCR: "1658" — is a copyist's mis-read]. New edition Leipzig, G. Sinhuber, 1873 (ed. Ludwig Siegmund). 8°, 272 pp. — As Scriver writes in his preface (16 December 1658), these sermons were held after old custom at Stendal, in the course of a week in 1658 from Trinity to 1st after Trinity, and indeed *in substitution* for Pastor Belcovius at St. Jakobi. Attached to Ps. 119:72 as text of the preparation-sermon, each catechism-main-piece is with its parts compared under *gold-images:* the 1st sermon treats the 1st–3rd Commandments on Acts 3:6; the 2nd the 4th–10th on Rev. 3:18; the 3rd Faith on several passages; the 4th Prayer on Isa. 55:1; the 5th Baptism; the 6th the Holy Supper on general gold-images. In the *form* the young Scriver here shows himself most as a *child of his time,* over which in his later works he so grew out. But *in content* he excellently expounds the Catechism *from the heart-speaking.* A more modernised edition: ***Goldpredigten über die Hauptstücke des Luth. Katechismus (Chrysologia catechetica) von M. Chr. Scriver,*** ed. W. L. Ergenzinger, 2nd revised ed., Stuttgart 1861. 8°, 223 pp. — Ergenzinger's re-work has translated the *200-year-old German* of Scriver into the sober Bible-speech of Scriver's spirit, and has omitted the citations from the ancient poets. But the catechism-hymns are still printed.

**2.** ***Die Herrlichkeit und Seligkeit der Kinder Gottes in Leben, Leiden und Sterben*** — nach Predigten Christian Scrivers, ed. W. L. Ergenzinger, Stuttgart, Belser, 1865. VIII + 342 pp., gr. 8°. 3.60 Mk., bd. 4.80 Mk. — Ergenzinger judges the sermons *"in the golden speech of an unfeigned yet child-firm faith, in the simplicity and power of the language, as in an own-particularly-church-directed manner... quite exceptionally fit for [reading and use for the Sunday house-devotion]."* Pp. 8–14 give a Scriver-life-sketch. From it we may take that Scriver held sermons *after [his own written] thoughts,* and issued (or they were issued): **1685** (and again **1701**) *"Die neue Kreatur oder das erneuerte menschliche Herz";* **1692** *"Die lebendige Erkenntnis Gottes";* **1697** *"Evangelische Gedenktage";* **1698** *"Fürnehmes Absehen eines Christen auf Gott, sich selbst";* **1696** (until 1784 ten times reissued) the already-named ***Die heilige und Gott wohlgefällige Haushaltung.*** — Scriver's *son-in-law* **Johann Heinrich Häveder** issued, at Nuremberg and Leipzig 1685, in Scriver's spirit: ***"Christian Scrivers Herrlichkeit und Seligkeit der Kinder Gottes, deren sie vor den ungläubigen Weltkindern als sonderliches Vorrecht im Leben, Leiden und Sterben genießen dürfen,"*** *at the entreaty of many godly, from the last-obtained Senior-consent, out of Scriver's own writings and meditations, together with additions of the highly-thought-of Herr Schwager's own trust to the Christian [Church] for use and profit given.*²²⁸ Häveder in his introduction writes on the *care that this work caused* him: *"I fear I should not be able to speak in the same way as he, since he did not himself set the sermons to writing. For unequal gifts do not [——], and I cannot be [equal] to him."* Ergenzinger has only lightly abridged, or cleaned out what was outdated. We find sermons for the [feast-days], for three Mary-days, and for [——]. Pp. 201–296 give notes; pp. 9–291 give thus the sermons.

**3.** ***Zehn Kasual- und Festpredigten von M. Chr. Scriver (1653–1690).*** Gesammelt und herausgegeben von W. L. Ergenzinger, Pfarrer in [Frankenbach]. Stuttgart 1862, Kircher, VIII + 296 pp., paperback 1.50 Mk. — Ten sermons, some occasion-sermons at office-changes. Each sermon dates supplied. Pp. 201–296 give notes. Pp. 9–291 give three Passion-sermons of **1653** (the first is the Entrance-sermon at Stendal); the New-Year sermon of **1657**; the Farewell-sermon at Stendal (**1667**); the Entrance-sermon at Magdeburg (**1667**); a Funeral-sermon of 1667; the Magdeburg **Huldigungspredigt vor dem Großen Kurfürsten** (**1681**); the Magdeburg Farewell-sermon (**1690**); and the Quedlinburg Entrance-sermon.²²⁹

### §6. Johannes Lassenius (1636–1700)

*Source: II b) §6, pp. 277–284. The Pomeranian-Polish-noble by birth, Copenhagen St. Petri Hauptpastor by the last thirty years of his life, and disciple of Heinrich Müller. Author of the ***Perlenschatz*** (1676/1687), the ***Biblischer Weihrauch*** confession- and communion-prayer-book (first 1687, many reprints to 1751), the ***Passionsandachten*** (1687), and the earlier ***Geistliches Rüsthaus*** (1663). Große treats him as the *Blossom-Time exemplar* of Müller's spirit carried through into the *Danish Lutheran Church* — the Copenhagen *Petri-Gemeinde* being the German-speaking mother-parish of Danish Lutheranism.*

**Johannes Lassen** (or **Lassenius**)²³⁰ was born on 26 April 1636 at **Waldau in Pomerania.** His father was there *[Rektor];* he sprang from a Polish noble-house *[Lassocius]* of [Kraśnik]. His mother was **Anna Sieverts,** daughter of *Diakonis* Dietrich Sieverts. After private-instructions at **Stettin** (whither the father moved in 1642) and at **Danzig,** he was sent in 1650 to the city-school at Danzig for three years, and in 1653 to the Gymnasium at Danzig where the *Order-poet* **Johann Mochinger** was his teacher.²³¹ In 1654, after the father's death, he was sent to his sister at [Varchow] and back to the Gymnasium at Stettin; in 1655 to the University of Rostock, where **Heinrich Müller** — who most influenced him — was his teacher. His journeys took him afterward through Holland, England, and even **Ireland,** through Poland and the Turkish border-lands. In one adventure he was thrown for nine days into a *mud-pit* [by robbers]; another time he was brought to the *Turkish-Hungarian border,* to be handed over *unnoticed as a slave into the hands of the Turks;* yet he escaped and fled to Magdeburg.²³² From there he went further to Helmstedt, where he came to know **Cellarius** and **Titius.** Thence to **Holstein,** where he was called as *Monday-preacher* and *Konrektor* at Itzehoe by *Bürgermeister* Woldenberg. In the same year (1666) he wedded **Elisabeth Meissler,** daughter of the *Statthalter* Count of [——] in Holstein. Later he entered a second marriage with **Pauline S.,** daughter of Pastor **Peter Goldschmidt** — a young widow. On **5 January 1676** he was called by King Christian V of Denmark as **Hauptpastor** of the *German Petri-Gemeinde* in Copenhagen; there he was also **Professor of Theology** and *Konsistorialrat.* Here he laboured *in great blessing* for 24 years, as **doctrinally solid Lutheran** — from all *pietistic-fanatic* aberrations he stood apart. His sermons were *from the depth of the Word.* Especially well he understood how to summon the *distressed hearers* to bear witness of the LORD. Sometimes at *soul-attacks* [he faced troubles in his own pulpit-service]. Of the mightier heralds of the divine Law and the tender preachers of the divine Gospel — *"one has not seen and one will not see one in the future."* [funeral-address].

So he wrought also through his writings. He wrote **very much.** The first was his ***Geistliches Rüsthaus*** of 1663: *"Of Needy and [Storm-tossed] Souls Geistliches Rüsthaus, consisting in Petitions, Devotions, and Songs, upon the general and most-common Need of the Christian Church, in 91 Divisions, brought together according to the Word and the old Teachers."* Copenhagen, 1663.

Most-significant became his ***Sionitische Erquickstunden, gefaßt in heiligen Andachten und christlichen Vor- und Nachbesinnungen aus heiliger Schrift und ausserlesenen Sprüchen auf alle Tage des Jahres gerichtet.*** First appeared in 1676 (also 1687, 1694). Continued for another quarter-year (Vols. 3 and 4 from July to December) in **1689** by a country-preacher **Peter Goldschmidt** [Lassenius's father-in-law]. Lassenius offers here the first *example of daily-devotions,* with *2 devotions for morning and evening of each day.*

In the same year also appeared the ***Heiliger Perlenschatz.*** Under the tender presentation over the months January to December of God's *care from his revealed Word* [the work draws on all Old- and New-Testament passages]. — Then in his ***Sionitische Erquickstunden*** (reissued in the middle of the century and later) the disciple of Müller treats as *theme* the vanity of the world and sets against it the *heavenly things.* True, he here steps behind his master, not always sober enough. Nevertheless he draws Scripture in all its parts profitably [——] into the discussion — [in a short, terse, characteristic speech that reminds of Müller]. But the *unsought, childlike Müller-manner* does not fit him as well; his sentences are sometimes without connexion set beside one another, so that the writing becomes uneasy, *choppy.* The *quieting-manner* of his mighty teacher he does not always keep. He labours — *especially in his first writings* — [in his own manner] and in *holy zeal to castigate* the world-sinners in the manner in which he comforts.

His writings were nevertheless worthy to be reissued. Especially through **G. Schubart,** ev.-luth. Pfarrer at Hohenstädt bei Grimma, there appeared in 1858 in the *Christlicher Verein im nördlichen Deutschland* a new edition of the ***Perlenschatz*** — this side-piece to Müller's *Erquickstunden* and Scriver's *Zufällige Andachten* — under the title: **"Das menschliche Leben, geschöpft aus Joh. Lassenius' heiligem Perlenschatz, neugeordnet dargeboten."** Unfortunately this six-section abridgement (Life-Formation and Life-Renewal, Life-Development and Life-[Reform], Life-Rules and Life-Needs, [Life-Rule and Life-Reflection], Life-[Guidance] and Life-Experience, Life-[Passing] and Life-Future) — 241 out of 730 devotions — is now out of print. In them Lassenius knows *short, terse, and fresh* to set everything in the world in connexion with Scripture: sometimes taking his start from individual words, sometimes from proverbs or general life-truths.

Two writings of Lassenius remain today in print:

**1.** ***Johannes Lassenius, weiland der heiligen Schrift Doktor, Biblischer Weihrauch zum süßen Geruch gottseliger Andachten*** — "gathered out of the Holy Scripture, so that with the Holy Ghost's own words the loving souls may daily speak with God in prayer in all their concerns, and prepare themselves for the Christian life, also for a blessed dying. Together with a saving preparation for the confessional and the Holy Supper." Barmen, Missionshandlung, 1878. XV + 280 pp., cl. 8°, bd. 1.80 Mk. — First appeared in 1687 with hymns; very often reissued: 1689, 1692, 1696, 1699, 1701, 1708, 1739, 1751, 1764. — The present edition contains, after Lassenius's dedication *"To all who are in the northern Zion, the darlings of God and my called, chosen confession- and communion-children in the Lord JEsu, of the German congregation at St. Petri, etc.,"* and his life-course, the table of contents. — Then follow **87 prayers on 280 pages,** ordered after Habermann's manner; to the longer morning- and evening-prayers are added shorter petition-, thanksgiving-, and [supplication]-prayers. Thereupon come **19 confession- and communion-prayers,** with sections *"Some brief devotions and meditations for going to the holy Table of the Lord,"* and *"After receiving the Holy Supper — some heart-sighs and delicious prayers."* Lassenius here — unlike Habermann — goes his own way: one finds (Friday excepted) hardly any determinable inner connexion of the prayers with the individual days. The prayers always return to the *spiritual goods.* To each morning- and evening-prayer are appended particular prayers for forgiveness or for blessed end, for comfort in the hour of [death] and the like, with changing superscriptions — so that at the individual days one finds together 10 prayers for forgiveness, 8 respectively for death and [judgement]. The **contemplation of the Passion of Christ** is found five times. Notable is that *Sunday* forms the *close.* The prayers all tie to a hymn-verse.

The language, being *"gathered out of the Holy Scripture with the Holy Ghost's own words"* (as Lassenius says on the title-page), is *through and through biblical,* and therefore *consecrated and anointed.* Through the *brevity of its wording* it is not always clear and easily-understandable. He who wants to *learn to pray in the fitting tone of God's Word* can here learn much. The at-times *edifying breadth of Habermann* is here overcome in the *spirit-full brevity of the sentence-construction* — although individual prayers, especially for morning and evening, are long. The short Supper-prayers are full of *glorious, thoughtful, biblical relations.* The **prayer for gracious protection from enemies** (p. [——]) is Christian, in that it prays *"that the suffering enemies may [be won]"* — [——] "sighs and comforts of the loving soul from the Song of Songs" are, save for a few passages that fall under the time-conditioned interpretation of the *Song,* [preserved]; the [15-verse] then very-loved **Litany on the Name JEsu** stemming from Bernard is [received]; the short expositions of the Lord's Prayer, Friday morning (p. 131), and Sunday morning (p. 189), have as pattern Luther's and Melanchthon's paraphrases and correspond to them in expression and in the accumulation of words entirely to their [manner]. The prayer-book will *ripe Christians* [profit].

**2.** ***Dr. Johannes Lassenius' heilige und erbauliche Passionsandachten.*** Freshly published and with a *very significant preface* by Prof. Dr. K. F. A. Kahnis, ed. Ernst Bredt, [1867]. VII + 200 pp., 31 lines to the page, bound 1.50 Mk. — **Kahnis** in his preface of 18 January 1867 speaks of the *significance of the re-issue of Devotional writings* of an earlier time, since *the present is not in the position to bring forth true edification-books itself.* Lassenius's writings have been highly praised: in 1709 by D. Masius in his *Geist- und Lehr-reiches Kompendium.* *"That the man who wrote these Passion-devotions was at home in Scripture, firm in doctrine, free from all fanaticism, marrow-strong in presentation — every reader will feel."* The devotions in their **peculiar arrangement and their strange superscriptions** appear as *own-drafted-devotional-books:* here Lassenius's spirit gushes with *witty finds* and a *fullness of thoughts.*

They can also *for spiritual delight and thought-enrichment* serve every preacher who lives himself into them. Especially valuable are the *[hymn-verses]* in elevated speech at each devotion. The peculiarity of the devotions — distributed over the *Passion-weeks* — is that Lassenius does not tie directly to the Passion-histories, but treats them under *witty [superscription]-titles:*

- **Week 1** — Morning-hours: *The Seven Cross-Mirrors* for the [Christian life]. Evening-hours: *The Seven Foot-steps* [of Christ]. (The Seven Cross-Mirrors: teaching-, wrath-, grace-, comfort-, life-, suffering-, [decay]-mirrors. The Seven Foot-steps: of Love, of Suffering, of [Faithfulness], of [Transience], of [Obedience], of [Steadfastness]; and [of the Cross].)
- **Week 2** — [Christ as Sacrifice + saving benefits]: The Seven Sacrifices of Christ. The Seven [Chief] Benefits of the Saviour.
- **Week 3** — Twice-seven [Assailings] on the Mount of Olives: *The trembling Rock; the Sun on Earth; the Heaven in Hell; the cold Fire; the strengthened Almightiness; the Devil among the Apostles; the despondent Heroes; the blind Lucifer; the war-like Priests; the cruel Kiss; the unwritten Letter of Protection; the two [——]; the bound Freedom; the un-stedfast [Standing].*
- **Week 4** — [The Dying-Word].
- **Week 6** — Twice-seven Passion-pieces.
- **Week 7** — The Seven Wonders at the Death of Christ; the Seven-fold Funeral-array.

*Note:* Lassenius's ***Sonn- und feittägliche Frühglocken*** (Berlin, Wohlgemuth 1859, IV + 224 pp.) is out of print.

## II b) B. Vertreter der Erklärer — the Bible-Expositors

### §7. Georg Fidicinis (c. 1640–1687)

*Source: II b) B. §7, pp. 284–288. The **Hungarian-Slavic Lutheran exile,** driven from **Leutschau in Upper Hungary** in the 1674 imperial-Catholic persecution — one of the most-atrocious anti-Lutheran actions of the seventeenth century — into a wandering exile through the Reich. Author of the ***Unüberwindliche feste Burg*** (1677) and the ***Todspeise*** (1680) — devotional-books composed *im Exilio* explicitly *"for the distressed, assailed, despondent, and also persecuted Lutherans."* Große treats Fidicinis as the **representative Exulanten-witness** of the Blossom-Time — the pastoral voice of the Lutheran ***diaspora*** which the Habsburg Counter-Reformation had scattered across the Reich.*

**Georg Fidicinis**²³³ was by his birth an *evangelical-Lutheran Hungarian of Slavic origin.* Between **1656 and 1660** he attended the Gymnasium in the free Imperial mining-town of **Leutschau in Upper Hungary.** Then he studied theology at **Leipzig** and **Wittenberg,** and was set at Leutschau as *Gymnasialteacher of the 3rd–4th class,* and further as *Senior of the Colleagues.* There he also married. In the year **1674** there broke out a **persecution of the Lutherans and Reformed by the Roman [Catholics].** All officials of Church and School were cited before an *extraordinary court* at **Preßburg.** Those who first appeared were condemned to death, or thrown into prisons and tortured with hideous martyrdoms. Whoever would escape had to go into **banishment.** So Fidicinis left [Leutschau] and wandered — [now to Silesia, now to Saxony, now to Hamburg]. In [Hamburg] he lived under [difficult circumstances], and helping his family was hard. So he issued in **1677** and **1680** his two most-significant writings — the ***Unüberwindliche feste Burg.*** — Since as a Slav he was not fluent enough in German, [the pastor Christian Schwarz] corrected his books; **Olearius** took [him in]. Fidicinis then went to **St. Georg near Preßburg,** but public preaching was forbidden him, so he gathered his students privately and held for the *oppressed Lutherans* prayer- and edification-hours. He died in **1687.**

His two devotional-writings were originally *one single work* — the ***Unüberwindliche feste Burg*** (1677). Fidicinis himself soon divided it into two parts, and later the *first six books* were issued afresh in 12° format under the title ***Geistliche Wehr;*** the *last six books* were printed as the ***Unüberwindliche feste Burg*** — e.g. **1685 at Hamburg** through the *St. Nikolai* pastor **Johann Winkler.** In **1882** the *Unüberwindliche feste Burg* was newly issued at **Hermannsburg;** 1888 the 3rd edition added the *life-circumstances of the author;* 1886 (in the 4th ed.) **W. Steib** further expanded these. The new edition bears the following title:²³⁴

> ***Unüberwindliche feste Burg, darinnen zu finden die trefflichsten Waffen und wohlbewährte Mittel wider die vergiftigen Anschläge und Mordpfeile des Teufels, Todes, der Welt,*** as also against all *Not, Angst,* and *Anfechtung* which a *right-shaped Christian* in this Zeitlichkeit commonly encounters. **Written in Exilio for distressed, assailed, despondent, and also persecuted Lutherans.** By **Georg Fidicinis,** *gewesenen Seniorem inf. Docent.* at the *whilom widely-famed evangelical* [Gymnasium at Leutschau].

The last five chapters (Book 8, pp. 412–[——]) offer *"comfort against the trials and all-manner misfortune, need, and danger."* If we mark its contents more briefly: Fidicinis will *— generally in the light of the Gospel, and* [with attention to the sinners on earth] *— show whence the comfort comes* — 1st Book at the [judgement of the world]; 2nd Book [——]; 3rd Book, [Cross of the Christian]; 4th Book, in the face of *earthly and* [heavenly] *distresses* (5th Book). Here Fidicinis touches especially on the *distresses of the persecuted* and *of exile* — since he himself experienced them. The superscriptions point to *edifying breadth* and to teaching intent, sometimes with heavy repetition (especially in Books 4 and 6 compared to 2 and 3).

**1893** appeared from the same author afresh:

> ***Todspeise*** [Death-food] von Georg Fidicinis, *Verfasser der "Unüberwindlichen festen Burg."* New edition, proceeds to the benefit of the Hermannsburg mission. Hermannsburg, in publication of the Missionshandlung. VI + 167 pp., cl. 8°, 1.20 Mk. paper, 1.80 Mk. bd.

Part 1 (26 chapters, pp. 1–106) sets **Christ before us in the loveliest way** — treats of *Christ's Person and Office* (and against the [Jewish reading] of Christ as [——] Judge). Part 2 (28 chapters, pp. 107–[167]) sets **God before us in glorious way** — treats of *God's mercy and attributes.* Part 3 [treats] especially the *Anfechtungen.* Central to the whole is Christ's call: *"Come unto me, all ye that are weary and heavy-laden."* — In both books the *language is here and there archaic;* the *Todspeise* is in its sentence-construction after Luther's *baroque-broad* manner; though not fully so [——]. Occasionally there are foreign-words: *Modus* [——], *maiestätisch,* *Aqua vitae,* *konvertieret,* *exzellieren.*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §8. The Bible-Expositions — Vertreter der Bibelwerke

*Source: II b) B. §8, pp. 288–291. Two collective Bible-commentaries of the seventeenth-century Lutheran Blossom-Time: the ***Württemberger Summarien*** (from 1667, driven by Duke Eberhard III and the *Landessynode*), and the ***Altenburger Bibel*** — the latter set forth in the same spirit and afresh issued in the modern age by the ***Concordia-Verlag* of St. Louis,** and thus of special interest to the American-Lutheran devotional-scholar market of Große's own day.*

#### a) The Württemberger Summarien

The ***Württemberger Summarien*** are a **Bible-commentary of medium extent** from the middle of the seventeenth century. In them we find a *Scripture-exposition* such as our fathers heard *from the pulpits* — an exposition set forth to be read *also in the house.* Especially in **Old** and **New Testament** they [treat] with attention to the *historical sequence,* and with the *unity of the whole Scripture-word before their eyes.*²³⁵

Große's title for the newly-issued Summarien reads:

> ***Die Württemberger Summarien: Ausführliche Auslegung der ganzen heiligen Schrift Alten und Neuen Testaments.*** [In greater format].

From the preface we see that in **1699**²³⁵ **Joh. Weyrich Rößlin** at Stuttgart brought forth the first edition; a second at **Joh. Friedrich Gleditsch;** in **1721** at Frankfurt **Johann Heinrich Löwe** the third; and in the same year **Dr. Pritius** at Leipzig the fourth. The impulse for the first edition **Duke Eberhard III** gave already in **1634:** he commissioned the *Abt* **Johann Jakob Heinlin** with the drafting, since the Summarien of **Veit Dietrich** which had until then been used were now nearly all lost from the market. **Heinlin** worked out the New Testament and completed the Prophets in **1662.** The Old Testament was drafted by **Zeller** (Genesis 1667), and the *General-Superintendent* **Joh. Konrad Heller** completed it in **1667** by taking up the rest; so that in **1669** the two *Chronicles* appeared. In **1672** the Bible-work stood complete — a *foundation-laying that the Munich* [——] *would not have approved.* Changes were made only in the [content] at points of *clearly incorrect exposition* — e.g. at too-narrow or [too-broad] *moral teaching;* the *historical certainty* was reshaped to fit modern times [——].

The **language** is *broad, but understandable.* By sub-divisions of the chapter, with Roman numerals for the chapters and Arabic figures for the *teachings,* the exposition is set out clearly. The impulse for this new edition came from the **Landessynode.**

The Scripture-exposition of the Summarien was from the beginning intended not only for the *ecclesiastical use,* but also for **private use** — especially for the *common domestic Hausandacht.* In great warmth and with a *plain, simple speech,* they expound to serve the Christian life *the Word of God in its context — verse by verse, or thematically summarised.* *"For the Church"* — so runs their own preface — *"but also for every householder's peculiar Hauskirche are they set."* There is here truly a *Bible-exposition full of richness, depth, and purity of* [teaching] — as the old title says, *"an instruction to the beneficial use in Faith, Life, and Suffering."*

The editors of the new edition are listed in the 4th ed. preface (pp. V–IX): **Bahmann,** Dean at Thurn; [——] **Ebart,** Pastor at [——]; **Küster,** Pastor at [——]; **Richard Hetz,** Pastor at [——]; **Dr. Weber,** Pfullingen; from the year 1886: **Rudel,** Ermershausen.

#### b) The Altenburger Bibel

From a somewhat later time is the **Altenburger Bibelwerk,** set forth in similar spirit. It rests upon **Luther's** and **Veit Dietrich's** expositions and Summarien, and stands as a *worthy side-piece to the Weimar Bible.* It is newly issued at **St. Louis** by the ***Concordia-Verlag:***²³⁶

> ***Das Neue Testament unsers HErrn JEsu Christi, verdeutscht durch Dr. Martin Luther, mit dessen Vorreden und Randglossen, sowie mit den Summarien M. Veit Dieterichs, nebst den Vorreden und Auslegungen*** [——] ***Francisci Vierlings.*** St. Louis, Mo., Concordia-Verlag, **1888.** III. Band (XX + 588 pp.).

There is also **Vol. I** — the 4 Gospels and the Acts (XL + 792 pp., gr. 8°, **1879,** 4 Mk., bd. 5.20 Mk.) — and **Vol. II** — the Epistles and Revelation (XL + 742 pp., **1880,** 4 Mk., bd. 5 Mk.). The work reached its close with a **Psalter-volume** (IV + 340 pp., gr. 8°, **1890,** 2 Mk., bd. 2.80 Mk.), as **Möller's** preface remarks. In the Psalter especially are drawn upon **Augustine, Luther, Bengel,** occasionally **Jerome, Melanchthon, [Vilmar]** († 1868). Follows the Bible-text of **1676;** chapter by chapter is set with a preface *addressed to the common Christian,* with short explanations and a *Votum;* after individual pericopes come longer or shorter *Summarien* of Dietrich.

*Note:* This is a significant work of the meritorious **[Concordia-]Verlag,** which — as its own preface states — began to issue *the Books of the Old and New Testament according to Dr. Martin Luther's [translation] with the exposition of the most-excellent Scripture-scholars of the ev.-Lutheran Church.*

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
