## II b) C. Die lutherische Orthodoxie unter dem Einfluß der Sprachgesellschaften

*The C-section of the Blütezeit collects four devotional writers of the same age whose prose is marked — sometimes to their gain, more often to their loss — by the **baroque-poetic Sprachgesellschaften** (language-societies), especially by the Nuremberg ***Pegnesischer Blumenorden.*** The four are: **§9 Rittmeyer,** confession- and communion-writer; **§10 Quirsfeld,** the most-prolific Saxon Baroque devotional writer; **§11 Pfeiffer,** the learned Superintendent of Lübeck; **§12 Dilherr,** the Nürnberg Antistes and Pegnitzschäfer center-figure.*

### §9. Johann Rittmeyer (1636–1698)

*Source: II b) C. §9, pp. 291–299. The **Braunschweig-Helmstedt** confession- and communion-writer. Author of the ***Himmlisches Freudenmahl der Kinder Gottes*** (1653) — one of the finest Lutheran-devotional preparation-books for the Holy Supper — and of the *Christliche Lebensregeln* which Wilhelm Löhe re-issued in 1875. Große treats him as *the paradigm Communion-preparation-writer* of the Blossom-Time.*

**Johann Rittmeyer**²³⁷ was born on **15 November 1636** at Braunschweig, in the middle of the bloody Thirty Years' War. His father was an honourable merchant. Johann attended the [Katharineum] school, and later in **1658** became for seven years the **preceptor of Duke Rudolf August of Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel.** [Later still, after long illness, he was called by God back into the pulpit as pastor at Helmstedt.] Only 5 of his children survived him, together with his sorrowful widow **Maria Elisabeth née Schrader.** Of himself said one: *"His memory is in the Book of the Righteous; his merits live in many thousands of hearts; his name blossoms in his descendants; and his blessing shall bide with the pious ever more."*

Rittmeyer's **Kommunionbuch — Himmlisches Freudenmahl der Kinder Gottes** — belongs to the *best and simplest* from that period, and was once widely distributed (Hamburg 1653; Helmstedt 1708 and 1719; Lüneburg 1724, 1734, 1748; Magdeburg 1743; Berlin 1760). Its earnest treatment of Confession and Supper marks the *faithful pastor* and touches on every aspect of the soul that can come to bear on the Holy Supper. The language is *consecrated,* though — here and there — influenced by the *bombastic manner* of that time, somewhat wordy. Not all the prayers are Rittmeyer's own; sometimes prayers of Arndt, Luther, or other good sources of that or older time are drawn in. Modern edition:

**1.** ***Himmlisches Freudenmahl: Ein Kommunionbuch,*** von M. Johann Rittmeyer, Pastor zu Helmstedt. Freshly issued by Pastor **Traugott Siegmund.** With woodcuts by **A. Gaber** after J. Frankl. 2nd ed. Leipzig, [Sinhuber], 1872. 18°, 314 pp., 31 lines, bd. 1.20 Mk. — Verbatim reprint of the [1872] Sinhuber edition. In the volume is annually with catechists the Holy Communion celebrated; the pastor uses the book especially in his discussion of the *teaching from the Catechism* two hours' preaching-work. As *examinatorial-forms* for the private-confession, the [confession-formulas] are drawn from the children's own words — a means to earnestier preparation. The few older expressions (*Niesung* I, 47; *Zulasse* V, 25) do not disturb the intelligibility. — The book will be loved especially for its *pictures and verses,* and corresponds through its alternation of longer and shorter prayers to the various *preparation-* and *communion-needs of the children.* — Contents: Cover-picture and dedication of the son to the father; Siegmund's preface (pp. 5–10) with life-sketch and summarised Rittmeyer's own preface; pp. 11–36 **healthy instruction on the right use of the Holy Supper;** pp. 37–47 **77 examination-questions** for the [preparation for God, for oneself, for the neighbour]; pp. 48–115 **17 prayers before Confession** (of which the 15th, "*Bitten für die Passionszeit,*" for the Lenten weeks); **8 Confession-formulas** (the 2nd by Cubach); prayers after Confession; pp. 115–198 **Communion-prayers:** 15 *before,* 10 *at,* and 24 *immediately after* (partly Passion-prayers); pp. 216–[——] **Morning- and Evening-blessings from the Sonntag [Betbuch] of 1670** (Brandenburger Betbuch) and Cubach 1664/1670.

**2.** ***M. Joh. Rittmeyers Christliche Lebensregeln,*** with preface by **Wilhelm Löhe.** 2nd ed. With a title-picture (Jesus sets a child among the disciples). Gütersloh, Bertelsmann; Nürnberg, Gottfried Löhe, **1875.** VI + 104 pp., kl. 8°. Bd. with gold-edge 2.30 Mk. — Prints the **100 Lebensregeln** (from the *Kommunionbuch,* pp. 246–[——]), each on a page, in especially handsome outfit; used as a Confirmation-gift.²³⁸ Löhe gives a short preface (Neuendettelsau, 26 December 1868), a praise of Rittmeyer, and notes that the motto of the Rules from **Gal. 6:16** does not fit these Rules exactly. Löhe adds: *"It is really worthwhile to test whether the Rules deserve the motto. But if the passage does not fit Gal. 6:16, one can nevertheless with quiet conscience choose a fitting title for these Rules — 'honey-drops,' as [Fetzer] once famously called his Aphorisms, would suit as well. Whether that is only bookseller's praise or [——]. — Neuendettelsau, 26 Dec. 1868. W. Löhe."*

### §10. Johann Quirsfeld (1642–1686)

*Source: II b) C. §10, pp. 299–302. The **most-prolific Saxon devotional writer** of the Blossom-Time — Kreuzschule Dresden formation, Pirna Kantor and later Archidiakonus. Author of the ***Allersüßester Jesustrost*** and the ***Passionsandachten*** — devotional works marked by the *baroque-emblematic superscription-manner* of the language-societies, but grounded on the tested piety of *Herberger* and *Gerhard.**

**Johann Quirsfeld**²³⁹ was born in **1642 at Dresden,** where his father was [Pastor]. He is known to have attended the **Kreuzschule.** He became **Kantor** and school-colleague at Pirna; **1675** *Magister;* **1679** *Archidiaconus* [at Pirna]; and died in **1686.** He is one of the *most-prolific devotional-writers in Saxony.* He has — like many of his contemporaries — a certain *playing, over-made* manner, as a consequence of the influence of the language-societies, especially the wider circle of the **Nuremberg poet-school.** His devotional writings he likes — in *form* — to set under a *chosen chief-thought,* and then to lead all through, in painful exactness, all its turns, often becoming *artificial.* Yet at the same time the *Love between the Soul and Christ,* the *Contempt of the World,* and the *Christian's Struggle in the World* are ever anew unfolded.

Great is the tale of the discovery of Quirsfeld's ***Allersüßester Jesustrost*** by **Wilhelm Horning** (1858–1913), pastor at Jung-St.-Peter in Strasbourg — Quirsfeld's re-discoverer for the confessional-Lutheran nineteenth-century revival:

> To the sermon-hours of his church in Jung St. Peter, Strasbourg, brought one day a woman from his congregation an old book with the words: *"Herr Pastor, this book you must let be printed! I will pay part of the printing-cost."* Horning took it and read: ***Quirsfelds Allersüßester Jesustrost.*** Horning found in him a spiritual kinsman of **Valerius Herberger** by content and form — in whom, in *tested heart-piety,* earnest repentance-work, and steady *Christ-love,* established on the Holy Scripture and on the *Confession of our Ev.-Luth. Church,* he taught, warned, and comforted — driven by inward, humble love to the brethren. There was hardly a *station or life-condition,* hardly a *hymn,* for which not *words of elevation and comfort* in Quirsfeld were to be found. Himself pressed by manifold grief, Quirsfeld could take part with full heart in the need of others; and therefore he had the *right understanding* and the *right word* for the same. **Horning went with joy to the work.**

Modern edition (as issued by Horning): ***Johann Quirsfelds, weiland ev.-luth. Pfarrers zu Pirna, Allersüßester Jesustrost*** — "how *in the fatigue and heat of the day, in all need and tribulation, also all sinful assailments and enticements of the lust of the flesh, of the eye, and of the pride of life,* [——] one may find refreshment, strengthening, and use, in one" [——]. Preface by Pastor Horning at Jung St. Peter, Strasbourg, [——] pp. Gr. 8°, 480 pp., good print, bd. 3 Mk. — Quirsfeld unfolds **38 devotions,** each under a **Jesus-superscription** drawn from Scripture and turned baroque-emblematic:

- **1.** JEsus is my only Joy
- **2.** JEsus is my sole Desire
- **4.** My JEsus is the beautiful **Sharon-flower** (Cant. 2:1)
- **9.** My JEsus is my Royal Coat-of-arms
- **12.** My JEsus is my **Eagle** (Ex. 19:4)
- **18.** My spiritual **Zion** (Ecclus. 43:12)
- **19.** My **heart-strengthening** [Rock]
- **23.** My spiritual **Treasure-chest**
- **29.** My spiritual **[——]**
- **31.** My sweet **Sleep-drink**
- **32.** My gentle **[Consolation]**

Quirsfeld is not original in this manner — his *forerunners* stand in **Valerius Herberger** and **Johann Gerhard;** especially close is Gerhard's ***Meditationes.*** *"Betrachtung der 12 [Kandelabra der Kirche], 12 Hauptwerke, und der daraus entspringenden [Trostquelle]"* [treating] the *holdselige JEsu, so in Priesteramte* [——] to all believing [souls]. The *[eyes] of the twelve Tage and Paare* [——]. The Book closes with **short heart-sighs to JEsu** (pp. 479–480). Cross-reference: Quirsfeld appears also in **Christoph Olearius'** ***Eröffnete Himmelspforte*** (see the Olearius-clan at §2, n. 199 above), and further in **"Der Gang nach Eisleben"** in the Anhang.

### §11. August Pfeiffer (1640–1698)

*Source: II b) C. §11, pp. 302. The learned Lübeck-Superintendent and Leipzig extraordinary-professor of Theology; author of the ***Evangelische Erquickstunden.**

**August Pfeiffer**²⁴⁰ was born on **27 October 1640;** he was Rector at St. Afra in Meissen; **1681** *Archidiakonus* at Leipzig and *ausserordentlicher Professor of Theology;* died on **11 January 1698** as **Superintendent of Lübeck.**

Pfeiffer belongs in his devotional-books to those *who show the learning in them and play with well-chosen words.* Nevertheless a *pleasing* little work of the *Christlicher Verein im nördlichen Deutschland* traces back to him:

> ***Evangelische Erquickstunden, d. i. die Evangelien des Jahres durch Geschichten erläutert.*** After the older [work] of the whilom Dr. August Pfeiffer, freshly re-worked and issued by the *Verein im nördlichen Deutschland,* [500 pp.].

The re-worked volume unfolds the *Gospel-pericopes of the church-year* by attached *narratives* which illuminate one saying at a time, e.g. to verse [4] *"Precious Ransom";* to verse 5 *"The Son's Punishment"* and *"Cross on the [——]";* to verse 6 *"Konstantia, the Queen of [——]"* and *"Conversion of a Blasphemer";* *"That I am His own [——]."* So are one, two, and more histories with application to the Gospels told — sometimes only in the [——], but always keeping the *purpose* in view: to illuminate the saying or [——].

### §12. Johann Michael Dilherr (1604–1669)

*Source: II b) C. §12, pp. 302–306. The Nürnberg *Antistes,* the *poetically-formed* head of the ***Pegnesischer Blumenorden*** as a spiritual centre, author of the Latin ***Contemplationes et suspiria hominis christiani*** (1634) — a paradigm-devotional book of the mid-seventeenth-century Lutheran-Baroque literature, re-issued afresh by the *Missouri-Synod Concordia-Verlag* at St. Louis in 1874 in W. Müller's translation. Große closes Section II b) with Dilherr as the Nürnberg-poetic exemplar of the whole Sprachgesellschafts-influence — the last of the *Großen* of the Blossom-Time in *poetic* as well as *devotional* register.*

**Johann Michael Dilherr**²⁴¹ was born on **14 October 1604 at Themar** in Sachsen-Meiningen. His father was Saxe-Meiningen *Regierungsadvokat* at the *fränkischen Klosterschaft Werra.* His mother, **Regina,** daughter of *Bürgermeister* Göß at Themar in Franconia, died in **1607.** He had, at her deathbed, read and helped pray with her; and in accord with her wish his father gave him at 13 years — that he might become clergyman — to the Gymnasium at **Schleusingen.** Because of some [ill] of his father and hard discipline of his teacher, it was not easy for him. In **1623** he went to **Goslar** and thence to Leipzig. He became *Famulus* of **Caspar Barth** and worked with him at corrections and verse-making. In **1627** he became [some office], and afterwards travelled through the North-German university-lands, going as far as Wittenberg.

Dilherr became **Professor** at **Jena;** and in the same year (**1640**) — as *University-envoy* — administered the *University-monies* laid down at the castle of **Remda** by an imperial *Reiterregiment.* On this University-embassy through occupied territory he had to conduct himself with *cunning:* he disguised himself as an *Oberst,* rode as Master over the city, and — as the enemy *Colonel* rode up to the artillery-fire with which he had defended the town — he [——] the city had a *General of high rank,* so that the enemy withdrew, and he returned with the *money.*

In **1641,** as consequence of the war the *University* [closed], he went on leave to [Italy], preached in Nuremberg, and after only seven hours' preparation held such a distinguished *Latin address* on the [Ephesians] that the Council called him for the setting-up of a University-*Studienauditorium* at Nürnberg, entrusted him with the inspection of all libraries, and gave him the first *pastorate* which fell vacant. So he left Jena in **1642** and entered Nürnberg [as Antistes]. But the *Menschengunst* — the favour of men — which he there received in such degree, especially in his first years, cost him his *inner spiritual life* to some extent. To the old *Antistes* **Saubertus** he would fall out with no one, and cultivate honour as a *"man of Progress and Culture."* To this end he tied himself in **1644** by marriage to **Susanne,** widow of the merchant [——], of a *wealthy patrician family,* and came soon into contact with the *aesthetic Pegnitzschäfer.* His *style* became **worldly-formed,** though he himself did not. Of the *Pegnitzschäfer* he became the **spiritual centre;** he composed in their sense *spiritual shepherd-plays* and invited to their performances. Later ages have thereupon [——] in the newer collections [——].

Nevertheless his *most-imperishable* work is his Latin **Contemplationes** — the ***Contemplationes et suspiria hominis christiani*** (1634), which for as long as [Lutheran devotional literature endures] belongs among the *paradigm-devotional-books* in translation. **Meyfart** wanted the significant work translated into German; in **1640** the jurist [——] translated it into English.

Modern edition:

> ***Betrachtungen und Seufzer eines [gottsuchenden Christen],*** von **Johann Michael Dilherr,** *aus dem Lateinischen übersetzt* by [W. Müller], *lutherischer Pastor.* With a preface by Professor [——], St. Louis, Mo., and afterword by **Dr. [Delle], 1874.** XXIV + 320 pp., bd. cloth with gold-edge 3.50 Mk.

Dilherr's dedication stands at the front: *"To Thee, JEsu Christ, Son of God and Mary, ever-remaining Judge of the Living and the Dead, I hand over, weeping and sighing, this my meditation-book, and ask and pray that Thou mayest grant me — in life Thy assistance, in death Thy comfort, in judgement Thy intercession. — Thy blood-bought Worshipper."* Then the recommending preface of [Cheirt] with a short life-sketch: *"A particularly lovely writing of Dilherr's is the present devotional-work — a side-piece to Gerhard's *Meditationes* and *Exercises in Godliness.* It contains [——] from the Church Fathers. He composed it in 1634 [in Jena]. Therewith runs also his poetic ***Contemplationes et suspiria hominis christiani*** [——]."* Pp. XII–XX give Dilherr's own preface with his thoughts and the reason for the composition. The book is meant as a *"Preparation for Eternity,"* especially for those [swelling-hearted]. The example-headings:

**1.** Of the Greatness of Divine Love. — **7.** Of Man's original high Dignity and his Fall. — **13.** Of Christ's [Testimony until 26]. — **27.** The Beauty of the Church. — Then the works of the Holy Spirit; then Trinity; then Sanctification; then [——] the Sacraments Baptism and the Supper; then the Christian [duties]. — With mighty language, addressing the reader in the second person, the *teaching* is made [servant] of the *devotional* register.

*Note:* **Martin Beer** (†1692 at Nürnberg) with his ***Allen andächtigen Jung- und Aufjahrenden Christen [zugesprochene Betrachtung]*** belongs also to this Nürnberg-devotional current; and **Johann Jakob Otto** (†1669) with his ***Krankentrost*** (1671; re-issued 1841 at Reutlingen by **Burk;** now out of print) rounds out the Blossom-Time C-section.

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>

## Section II c) The Outgoing of the Sober Lutheran Devotional Literature and the Beginning of the Sanctification-Literature: 1680–1700

*Source: II c) opens at source p. 306 and closes at p. [——]. Große's own running-head: **"Mittelpunkt: Spener und die durch ihn gepflegten [——] Traktate — Neumanns Kern-Betgebete."** The Section unfolds a **hinge-generation** — the *last twenty years* of the sober, church-attached Lutheran Blütezeit-literature runs alongside the *first twenty years* of the Pietist *sanctification-literature.* The two are not simple opposites but partly overlapping — Scriver, Lassenius, Rittmeyer, Quirsfeld continue to write and be re-issued alongside the emerging Pietist writers. Große's arrangement: **§1** Allgemeines; **§2** Zur Frömmigkeit im Anfang des Pietismus; **§3** Philipp Jakob Spener — the central figure and *founding-father* of the movement; **§4** Johann Kaspar Schade — the Berlin *Vorläufer* of Spener, whom Große treats immediately after Spener.*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §1. General Remarks

*Source: II c) §1, pp. 306–308. Große sets the *hinge* — the concurrent close of the sober-Orthodox devotional wave (Scriver, Lassenius, Rittmeyer, Quirsfeld) and the opening of the *Heiligungs-Literatur* proper. Spener's ***Pia Desideria*** (1675) sets the programme; the *Pietist-devotional books* concentrate on the *third article* of the Creed — the *Order of Salvation.**

Into this time — which brings the *first blossom of Pietism* — the writings from the Blossom-Time of Lutheran Orthodoxy still project themselves further: especially **Scriver's** and **Lassenius's,** likewise those of **Rittmeyer** and **Quirsfeld.** Scriver's *Seelenschatz* first appeared complete in **1693.** So also begin — immediately attaching themselves — the ecclesiastical devotional writings, and to the *swan-song of Orthodoxy,* the [heart-strengthening] devotional books [——]. Since **1650,** Neumann's ***Kern-Betgebete***²⁴² — a paradigmatic devotional prayer-collection — belongs among the *last-fruits* of the same current.

From the Orthodoxy and its works there speaks a **healthy [pastoral] Orthodoxy** which has heart and understanding for the folk — [preserving] the power, [tenderness,] and *sublimity* of the age. **Independent, in part even opposed,** brings **Pietism** *a new kind of devotional writing* — in which, in immediate attachment to Spener's ***Pia Desideria*** and Francke's followers, above all *every single person* is to be turned to Christ and to *personal, living Christianity* [——].

The care of ***personal Christianity,*** as it stood at the heart of Spener's *Pia Desideria,* was to be pursued not merely through the *called Servants of the Church,* but *with the sleeping forces in the Church herself.* In the first times of Pietism — which we define as the end of the century — these writings sought still to keep the **connexion with the ecclesiastical soil** on the one hand — against the *Schwärmer,* who *already at that time* exploited Spener's thoughts *one-sidedly, and against the Church.* The circumstance that Spener and his followers demanded [deep engagement] with the *Scripture-exposition* brought about the [rise of the] **Bibelstunden** outside the Church, or the *fresh emphasis on Scripture-exposition in the Sermon itself* — as e.g. in Spener's Introductions to his Preaching. Printed [expositions] came out much from that time.

All the more did the Pietists bring to expression whatever falls into the sphere of the ***third article*** of the Creed — the ***Ordo salutis,*** the Order of Salvation. **New prayer-collections** appear: since Pietism emphasizes the ***Herzensgebet*** — the *heart-prayer* — over against the ***Buchgebet*** (the *book-prayer,* which is denominated as a lower stage). For the *permeation of Life* through Christian sense and Spirit come ***Lebensregeln*** [Life-Rules], daily-devotional books for the whole calendar-year — *not* in connexion to the pericopes of the church-year (as with the Orthodox writers), but tied to the *conversion of individual estates* through *Stand-books* [state-in-life-books] and *daily-thought-schedules.*

The **Pietist folk-literature** *suffers,* however, by the fact that it writes *too much for the already-awakened.* The *straight, folk-tune-like element* is easily lost. The *spiritual, anointed language* is mostly understandable only to the higher folk-classes.

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §2. Toward the Piety at the Beginning of Pietism

*Source: II c) §2, pp. 308–310. A gallery of the early Pietist *ordinary-life* — Halle as centre, Frankfurt as first model, the Bible-and-*Häumeln* pattern of pietist Scripture-devotion, and the aristocratic and burgher network that carried Spener's programme through the closing decades of the century.*

On the *specific formation* of Pietism-piety which characterises Pietism, we shall speak in fuller connexion in the first half of the following century (Tranche 7 below at III a)). A glance into the *first-development* of this piety is granted already by the *life of the first Pietists* — Spener and Francke — [——]. For a first characterisation, this: **All pietistic gathering** ties itself at the end of the century — *especially at Halle,* as it — [——] the educated circles of the folk, not merely the pastors, but *many of the nobility and the jurists*  — with pietistically-formed *personalities* filled — [gathers] at the [private-assemblies], soon after the model of **Frankfurt** at **Augsburg,** [Straßburg,] **Essen, Darmstadt, Hamburg,** and — in somewhat other form — at the ***Collegia philobiblica*** (biblical lectures) at **Leipzig.**

The pietistic piety nourished itself, above all else and in the first line, on the **Holy Scripture.** [Long lives were] in often ecstatic way *constantly under* [the impact of] the whole Scripture — Old and New Testament — through *trust in the leading of life through an unmediated intervention of God tied to his speech in Scripture.* Thence came the [practice] that one took the *individual sayings* which one experienced through **Häumeln** — the *thumb-drawing*²⁴³ (Spener's own word) — not to explore *future things,* but to *encourage one another* — and the *strengthening of life through the strictness which he laid upon it,* is a *ground-line* of Spener's thoughts and of his friends.

Such [pietist piety] Spener soon found in the *highest circles.* One remembers:

- **Sophia Elisabeth,** Duchess of Saxe-Zeitz (of the House of Hesse-Homburg), who visited his devotional-hours from Frankfurt at Homburg;²⁴⁴
- **Elisabeth Dorothea of Hessen,** daughter of Ernst the Pious (see n. 196 above);
- **Benigna,** née von Promnitz-Sorau, spouse of the Count of **Solms-Laubach;**²⁴⁵
- **Christina,** Princess of Mecklenburg-Güstrow, since 1683 spouse of Count Ludwig Christian von Stolberg (†1749);
- **Veit Ludwig von Seckendorff** (of Meuselwitz), Chancellor at [Halle-Wittenberg] (†1692) — jurist, historian, and one of the intellectual pillars of early Pietism;²⁴⁶
- **Henriette Katharina von Gersdorff** (1648–1726), née von Friesen — the Zinzendorf-grandmother, whose Herrnhut-establishing role in the following generation is one of the *founding* Pietist-noble roles;²⁴⁶
- **[Erdmann Neumeister]** and other **Geheimratsdirektoren;**
- **von Wolfframsdorff at Dresden;** and, at Berlin, **Karl Hildebrand von Canstein** (of Bibelanstalt-fame),²⁴⁷ **Friedrich Rudolf Ludwig von Canitz** (poet and diplomat), and **Otto Gneomar von [——]** (Zinzendorf's stepfather) — the last three of Berlin, standing under the influence of Spener himself and of his same-minded fellow-brothers **Schade** and **Astmann.**

Beside these first begetters of Pietism who worked *completely in Spener's sense,* still many others also wrote devotional-books — though these have not gained abiding-value. Named must be:²⁴⁸

- **Christian Kortholt** at Kiel († 1694), eager writer of *devotional works for the young at their conversion;*
- **Adam Krebs,** *Amtsrat* and *Kammersekretär* at Arnstadt (†1707 as private-man at [——]) — a **mild opponent** of the private-confession;
- **Georg Grabow** († [——]);
- **Johann Horbius** († 1695 in Sleswig on his country-seat), pastor at [Windsheim] and *Vorkämpfer* of Spener at Hamburg (1685 issued the *Feldandachten*); with his co-worker **Abraham Dinkelmann** († 1695 as [Diaconus] at St. Katharinen); and
- **Johann Jakob Schütz,** the Frankfurt friend of Spener and *co-founder of the private-assemblies* — an advocate, who *later* [drifted from the Church into more radical circles, and whose personal history is one of the *cautionary tales* of the earliest Pietism].

Of Spener and Schade some works remain today in reissues or re-workings still to be read (§§3–4 below).

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>

### §3. Philipp Jakob Spener (1635–1705)

*Source: II c) §3, pp. 310–318. The **founding-father** of German Lutheran Pietism. Rappoltsweiler-born; Strasbourg-formed; Frankfurt *Senior* of the ministry 1666–1686 (during which he wrote the ***Pia Desideria*** 1675 and began the *collegia pietatis* in 1670); Dresden *Ober-Hof-Prediger* 1686–1691; Berlin *Propst* of the Nikolaikirche and *Konsistorialrat* 1691 to his death 1705. Author of the ***Pia Desideria*** (1675), the ***Erklärung christlicher Lehre nach Luthers Kleinem Katechismus*** (1677 — the first application of the catechism-method to the *whole* Small Catechism, still current in Berlin Hauptverein reprintings in Große's day), the ***Auslegung des Briefes an die Römer*** (1679), the ***Natur und Gnade*** (Bekmann redaction of the collegia pietatis discourses), and hundreds of shorter tracts and sermons. Große treats him as **the paradigm-figure** of the whole coming Pietist age — *"a mild, evangelisch-milde, experience-rich"* pastor whose *personal Christenheit-programme* would carry the German Lutheran devotional literature through the entire eighteenth century.*

**Philipp Jakob Spener**²⁴⁹ was born on **13 (23) January 1635 at Rappoltsweiler in Alsace,** of pious parents. His father **Philipp** was councillor of the Count of **Rappoltstein;** his mother **Agatha, née Salzmann,** was the daughter of the Comital *Amtmann und Statthalter* Johann Jakob Salzmann. Already on the day of his birth destined to the *service of the Church,* Philipp was piously educated by his mother, and awakened inwardly early by the LORD to serious [meditation]. Children's games had for the pious [boy] no interest; he rather read the Scripture, **Arndt's *Wahres Christentum*** and ***Paradiesgärtlein,*** also **Baxter's [Practice of Piety]** in German [——] — one of the earliest German Lutheran-Pietist receptions of the English devotional literature (see n. 201 above at Bayly / Sonthom). Deep in the memory abided the *dying of his godmother,* the Countess [——] von Rappoltstein, in **November 1648:** he was deeply moved and inwardly [prepared].

His instruction continued at **Colmar;** in **1651** he entered the **University of Strasbourg.** Here he lodged with the husband of his father's sister, *Konsistorialrat* **Rebhan.** Already at that time he tied himself with some pious friends to *communal edification* on the [Sundays]. **1653** he became *Magister.* Actually he studied *theology* under **Dannhauer** and **Andreä;** *"Father in Christ"* became [Dannhauer]. In addition he was in **1654–1656** the tutor of the *two sons of the Count Palatine Christian II* of [Palatinate-Birkenfeld]. After his father's death he began his customary academic journey — first to **Basel,** where in **1659** with **Buxtorf** he perfected his knowledge of Hebrew;²⁵⁰ then in **1660** to **Geneva,** where he came to know [Antoine Léger,] who had been a Reformed preacher in Constantinople, a *born Waldensian;* and with the pietistically-inclined Bible-researcher, the *Jesuit-schooled* **Jean de Labadie,** he came into closer relations. He broadened there his knowledge of Reformed devotional writings, and had already in **1661,** as *Hofmeister* of the young Count of Rappoltstein, come to **D. Frommann** at Tübingen for 4 months. He had already begun to hold lectures before Counts and other noblemen.

Then in **1663** the Council at **Strasbourg** called him back as *Freiprediger.* As such he had no fixed pastorate, but only preaching-office. To supplement his income he also held lectures on theology, history, [philology], and politics. On **23 June 1664** he obtained the *theological Doctor*-title. On the same day he wedded **Susanna née Ehrhardt,** daughter of a *Ratsherr;* she bore him **11 children,** of whom six survived their father.

In **1666,** now, the extraordinarily-beloved teacher of the academic youth and *friendly, humble, gifted* Spener was called — as *Head-pastor* and *Senior of the ministry* to **Frankfurt am Main.** His first Frankfurt years brought him to know Frankfurt and the Frankfurters anew, and he had to learn to carry out the Scripture-office by the *new voice-and-manner* of Scripture-exposition — especially of the *Gospel* — that he had brought back from Geneva. Since he was quickly seen to be at odds with the [prevailing preaching-style], he introduced in **1670** in his own house the ***collegia pietatis*** — the *"pious gatherings,"* the *paradigm-institution* of the whole coming Pietist movement.²⁵¹ In **1675** he then published the ***Pia Desideria,*** the *founding-programme:* six *desires* for the reform of the ecclesiastical life — including *"more diligent Bible-reading among the laity"* and the *"universal spiritual priesthood."* The tract met, as Große notes at §2 above, *"a joyful greeting of 90 voices"* — a wave of correspondence responses from all quarters of the Lutheran Church.

In **1686,** after he had barely recovered from a seven-month severe illness at Meiningen, **Elector Johann Georg III of Saxony** called him to his ***Ober-Hof-Prediger*** office at Dresden — to a position which then stood as the ***first pulpit of the whole Lutheran Church of Germany.*** After thorough examination and consultation with friends, Spener accepted the call. In August he moved to Dresden. Here he now worked from **11 [August] 1686 to June 1691** — *five blessed years.* He renewed his contact with the friends in the wider Lutheran Church and in the closer circle of the Dresden court. But the ***Elector's given-to-drink*** [nature] — Johann Georg III was notorious for his drinking-excesses — came into conflict with Spener: on the occasion of a **Buß-Tag** (Repentance-Day), Spener addressed his sermon to the Elector as *"a very serious [——] Schreiben"* and *"earnestly rebuked"* the ruler.²⁵² Spener was — as the Elector could not himself dismiss him — moved out of his office in **1691** by his own request. In **1691** he received the call to the ***Provost*** of the **Nikolaikirche at Berlin** and to the position of ***Konsistorialrat*** of the Brandenburg Consistory. In Berlin he worked the last 14 years of his life — in ever-growing correspondence with the world Lutheran-Pietist network that his *Pia Desideria* had by then established. Spener was thereby a *pray-er for others* as few — many souls attributed their soul's rescue to his zealous prayer. The rich thanks and much honour that thereupon came to him could not shake his *humility.*

On the **8th Sunday after Trinity 1704** he held his last sermon. Then he called his family around him; and after he had reconciled himself to the *Confessional Writings,* asked their *forgiveness* and gave *a joyful witness of the grace of God.* Yet he recovered still for a short while. On **5 February 1705** — the Sunday **Sexagesimae** — he passed *"into eternal joy in JEsu Christo his LORD."* On his own request he was laid to rest [in the crypt] of the **Nikolai-Kirche at Berlin.** — Spener died with a copy of Arndt's ***Wahres Christentum*** at his side, having asked that his favourite meditation-hymn be read to him thrice.

***Spener's writings*** include several hundred single-tract items and a great many sermon-collections. Große's summary catalogues the following as *still-current* in the 1900 devotional-market:²⁵³

**1.** ***Pia Desideria oder Herzliches Verlangen nach gottgefälliger Besserung der wahren evangelischen Kirche*** (Frankfurt, 1675) — the *founding-programme* of Pietism; six *desires* for reform. Modern edition: **Karl Aland,** ed., *Pia Desideria* (Berlin, 1940; often reprinted). ET: *Pia Desideria,* trans. and ed. Theodore G. Tappert (Philadelphia, 1964; often reprinted). In Große's own day the tract was still current in a modernised Halle-edition.

**2.** ***Dr. Philipp Jakob Speners Erklärung christlicher Lehre nach Anleitung des kleinen Katechismus Dr. Martin Luthers.*** IX + 384 pp., gr. 8°. 3rd ed. (1852) Berliner *Hauptverein für christliche Erbauungsschriften,* 80 Pf., cloth 1.20 Mk., leather 1.60 Mk.²⁵⁴ — This is the 1677 original catechism-exposition, which for the *first time* applied the pietist-catechism *dividing-question-and-answer method* to the *whole* Small Catechism. Spener's aim: *"to lead the children to the inward, living recognition of the truths of salvation."* Sample: *"The understanding of the thing shall be so impressed on the folk that they [——] and afterwards from their own understanding [can [interpret]] them."*

**3.** ***Dr. Philipp Jakob Speners Auslegung des Briefes St. Pauli an die Römer, mit Einleitung und erklärenden Anmerkungen für die [Erbauung] des göttlichen Wortes,*** freshly issued by **Heinrich Schott,** Dr. phil., professor at Marburg. 4th stereotype edition. Reutlingen, Pfister-Schmidt. VI + 296 pp., gr. 8°, 51 lines to the page, paperback 1 Mk. — In the preface (I–II) the editor speaks of the *value of Spener's devotional writings,* especially their *penetrating and thorough Scripture-exposition.* Deep and thorough is Spener's exposition. Spener preached the Romans-letter in **1676** and **1677** in the introductions to his Frankfurt sermons; in **1679** he issued these as an *entrance* to the [pastoral-] audience.²⁵⁵ Freely stands one in this book on the border between the *simply-devotional* and the *deeper-drilling* scholarly. Yet with blessing one will read it also to edification: the man standing firm on the Lutheran Confession will also note the [minor] departures (Rom. 11:25) from the Confession and from the accustomed exposition, but let himself not be scared from the *standpoint that Spener as a whole takes* (esp. Chap. 7, note p. 151ff.).

**4.** ***Dr. Philipp Jakob Speners Abhandlungen von Natur und Gnade oder der Unterschied ihrer Werke*** — Berlin, [——]. Paperback 1.20 Mk. — Spener's preface speaks about the collection of the Scriptures from *lectures in the collegia pietatis,* recorded by the Holsteiner candidate **Nikolaus Bekmann,** and reminds that Spener recommended reading it since he had some tests of the working of grace in himself. — Structure: **10 signs of the working of grace** (pp. 6–18); the *first sign* (love to God); the *second* (love to God and its expressions); the *third* (love to the neighbour and its 8 signs and expressions); the *four particular signs of humility* (pp. 62–70) which testify themselves in six-fold; the *fifth,* patience (pp. 71–75); the *sixth,* self-denial (pp. 75–[——]); the *seventh,* joy; the *eighth,* prayer (pp. 83–85); the *ninth,* contentedness in the use of *[creatures]* and the avoidance of excess; the *tenth* and hastening after these signs (pp. 87–98).

*Note:* Only mediately Spener's is the well-known *Berlin-Consistorial-authorised* [——]. **Spener's *Kreuzschule*** — first published 1688; a further redaction as **1854 Naumann Dresden** re-issue by [Fabri]; that is at present out of print; but proceeds *independently in the same paths.*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §4. Johann Kaspar Schade (1666–1698)

*Source: II c) §4, pp. 318–322. The **Berlin Vorkämpfer of Spener** — Nikolaikirche *Diaconus* under Spener from 1691, dead of illness after only seven years (1698) at age 32. Author of the ***"Was fehlt mir noch?"*** — the *paradigm-Pietist catechetical dialogue-book* — of the ***Unterricht über die Beichte und das Abendmahl in Gesprächen,*** and of two hymns still current in Große's day: ***"Halleluja meiner Seele"*** and ***"Mein Gott, das Herze bring ich dir."*** Große treats him as the *younger, zealous* pastoral pendant to the *milder* Spener — the ***Judgement-preacher of the Berlin ministry.***

**Johann Kaspar Schade**²⁵⁶ was born on **18 January 1666 at Kühndorf** under the Dolmar in the *Fränkische Grafschaft* [Henneberg] near Meiningen. His father **Jakob Schade** was *Vice-Superintendent and Ephorus at Schleusingen,* [before Pfarrer and Dekan]. When he was two years old his father died, leaving six under-age children — of whom Johann Kaspar was the [eldest]. The *Rector of the Gymnasium at Schleusingen,* **Christian Ernst Schade,** was his uncle. This uncle took him in — and, in his 13th year, when his mother, **Maria Barbara,** daughter of the Rat and merchant **Kaspar Herlin,** also died — into his own house, and provided later that he might, with *free board and lodging,* be a **choir-school Alumnus.** Here he had to sing in the street, but also to endure much *hardship in frost and heat.* He learned diligently *German and Latin,* and soon knew the *Psalms* by heart. He also early prayed in bed on sermons, and went gladly to *solitude* to pray. Some youthful [rashness] let him fall into serious sins.

At Easter **1685** he attended the University of **Leipzig.** By private-tutoring he had to earn his living hard. Soon he came into engagement and *closest friendship* with **August Hermann Francke,** who since autumn 1685 was holding biblical lectures. Francke instructed him free in the *Hebrew tongue* and guided him deeper into God's Word. This — and a *severe illness* — awakened him fully to a *living Christian.* In **1687** he became *Magister.* Francke had by that time left Leipzig; Schade took over Francke's practical-biblical lectures, and held on Sunday afternoon a **Collegium philobiblicum** on the *First Peter.* Francke returned in **1689** and pursued with great zeal his *Collegium praxis pietatis* [and "what shall I do?"].

Schade was, from his beginnings, sickly and weak. He was called to a **Diaconate** in **1690;** in **1691,** having during a visit to Berlin preached two sermons, he was — without his own doing — unanimously voted by the Council to become **Diaconus** at the side of the just-called **Spener** at the **Nikolaikirche.** On the *2nd Advent* he was introduced.

**Seven years** he worked in great [blessing] as *preacher and pastor.* He preached with clear voice, and the great *gift of Repentance and Conversion* — a true, [heart-searching,] fruitful Christianity. Many were awakened through him. **Schade stood beside the mild, evangelisch-milde, experience-rich Spener as *the young, zeal-fiery preacher* who raised his voice unsparingly like a Judgement-preacher — to proclaim to the folk their transgression.** Equally earnest was he as *Seelsorger.* The poor he supported with his last thalers and kreuzers. On his own account he had *thousands of New Testaments* printed and gave them away. Beside this he wrote continually *edifying tracts.* At home he held for the domestics and for the workmen's-people prayer-hours, and instructed with zeal *children.* When they were led astray by [the paths of youth], he rejoiced at the influence which his Word had upon the children: they came — after the sermon — to his chamber and let themselves be *catechised on the sermons* and taught to pray.

Yet through his *earnestness* and his *own-kind* he displeased his **19 Berlin colleagues** more and more. Spener, from Frankfurt [——] defended him. [——] When Schade lay dying of tuberculosis, **he forgave from his death-bed his 19 opponents,** and asked that at his funeral no *rebuking-preaching* should be held. In his last hours he sighed: *"Oh Lord, my heart is glad — it is well with my soul."* He died on [25 July 1698,] aged 32. **Spener, four years later**, wrote in a letter: *"Had we still such an *Elias* here at Berlin, who had done us and the city so much good, [we should not now be so weakened]."*²⁵⁷

**Schade has an imperishable name in Lutheran hymnody.**²⁵⁸ His hymns are *glow-through with the holy Fire-Spirit* and in [——] soul-swing. Many are subjective and treat only his own conflicts. But such as ***"Halleluja, meiner Seele"*** and — his best hymn — ***"Mein Gott, das Herze bring ich dir"*** belong among the finest hymns from the *end of the seventeenth century.*

In his numerous *small devotional writings* one recognises the *man of burning zeal,* but full of *tenderness and inward movement.* Two of them have been afresh issued by the *Christlicher Verein im nördlichen Deutschland,* though in a freer editorial re-working suited to our circumstances:

**1.** ***Die Frage: "Was fehlt mir noch?" oder Anweisung zu einem thätigen Christentum, nebst einem Anhange von Gebeten.*** 9. Auflage. 187 pp., 27 lines to the page. Kl. 8°. Eisleben, Klöppel, 1860. Half-linen 40 Pf. — After an introduction and biblical grounding of **Matt. 19:16–20** and **Luke 18:22–23,** Schade treats the question drawn thence: *"What lack I yet?"* — pointing back on each Question to the constant closing-refrain ***"One thing thou lackest yet: [——]."*** The book contains, in structure:

- **Question 1:** *"I am called a Christian — what lack I yet?"* — **"Deed alongside the Name."**
- **Question 2:** *"I too am a good Christian, for I have learned my Bible and Catechism well — what lack I yet?"* — **"The doing of what has been learned."**
- **35 questions** on the ground of the **Ten Commandments** (pp. 26–[60]);
- **14 questions** on the *Second Chief-Part* (pp. 60–[——]); with **Q. 14** *"[——] the *preparation for the End*";
- **8 questions** on the *Third Chief-Part* — including the *paradigm-Pietist counsel:* *"To pray at every free hour, three times daily, without ceasing!"* (pp. 116–126);
- **8 questions** on the *Fourth Chief-Part* (pp. 126–143);
- **8 questions** on the *Fifth Chief-Part* (pp. 149–152);
- Some general appended questions to prevent *fleshly excuses* — e.g. **Q. 8:** *"What would my parents say, what would the world say, if I began an altogether other life — that would be much annoyance to me: what lack I yet?"* — **"One thing thou lackest yet: the readiness for the sake of the LORD to suffer shame."**

Then pp. 153–187 add, under **36 numbers** after a longer *Confession of Sins,* an appendix of the *best shorter prayers* — especially from **Luther** (with **Bugenhagen**) and from the ***Nürnberger Handbuch*** (1854) — corresponding to the individual commandments and distributed among the weekdays as short *morning- and evening-prayers.*

**2.** ***Unterricht über die Beichte und das Abendmahl in Gesprächen.*** Nebst einem Anhang. Eisleben, Verein, 1860. IV + 152 pp., 42 lines to the page. Half-linen 45 Pf. — As Wetermeier's preface reports, the basis of this volume is also an older writing of Kaspar Schade. Only the *dialogue-form* — attaching to the needs of the present — is newly added. The Appendix contains only the exposition of the *Lord's Prayer* and the *Twenty-third Psalm* by Schade himself; the remaining pieces are re-worked or taken from other older writings.

The book contains, after the catechism-parts (pp. 6ff.), **four clear, edifying dialogues:** the first with an *unbelieving and light-minded confession-child* (to p. 30); then with one who *deceives himself with a false comfort* (to p. 47); with a *penitent confession-child on the beginning of a new life* (to p. 62); then with a *true Christian and a hypocrite,* in whom the difference of the two is presented for *healing self-examination* (to p. [88]). The Appendix (pp. 88–118) contains: **1.** Biblical devotion for the Confession according to Ps. 6 and the Penitential Psalms — for the Holy Supper, Ps. 23, by Schade; **2.** Prayers: an *Exposition of the Lord's Prayer* by Schade (pp. 114–124); then 9 chiefly-Lutheran prayers for the Confession, 9 for the Supper, and 9 general Communion-prayers (pp. 124–152).

<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>

## Section III: The Age of the Blossom of Pietism, of the by-Pietism-not-uninfluenced Outgoing Orthodox Devotional Literature, and of the Transition to the Rationalistic — as well as their First Blossom-Time

*Große's own super-division marker: after Section II covering the *Reformation-Century through the Ausgang of the Orthodoxy at Spener and Schade* (Tranches 2–6), Section III opens the ***Eighteenth Century.*** The three-part title is Große's own compressed sociology: (1) ***Blüte des Pietismus*** — the peak of Pietism as a movement; (2) ***by-Pietism-not-uninfluenced Ausgang of Orthodoxy*** — the last flowering of the confessional-Lutheran devotional literature under pietistic pressure; (3) ***Übergang zur Rationalistischen*** — the transition to Rationalism, whose *first Blütezeit* Große will treat in Tranche 8 below at III b).*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

## III a) The Blossom of the Pietistic Sanctification-Literature and its Influenced Last Fruits of the Orthodox-Lutheran Devotional Literature: 1700–1750

*Große's own running-head sub-title: **"Mittelpunkt: [Francke], die Hallesche Schule, die Bengelsche Schule; Neumann, Schmid, Löscher."** Section III a) is the *longest single section* of the whole *Wegweiser* and covers the first fifty years of the eighteenth century. Große's arrangement: **§1** Allgemeines — the three-fold parallel currents of eighteenth-century Pietism (Gießen/Frankfurt mild; Halle Franckesch; Württemberg Bengel); **§2** Zur Charakteristik der Eigenart der pietistischen Frömmigkeit; then successive biographies of **Francke** and the Halle school (§§3ff.), the Württemberg pietists (§§Xff.), and the by-Pietism-influenced Orthodox writers Neumann, Schmid, Löscher (§§Xff. below).*

```{=openxml}
<w:p><w:r><w:br w:type="page"/></w:r></w:p>
```

### §1. General Remarks

*Source: III a) §1, pp. 322–327. Große's opening survey of the three-fold Pietist current — Gießen-Frankfurt mild-churchly; Halle-Francke evangelical-conversion; Württemberg-Bengel biblical-scientific — plus the last flowering of the by-Pietism-influenced confessional-Lutheran devotional writers (Neumann, Löscher, Schmolck, Neumeister), and the concurrent revival of the medieval ***Thomas à Kempis*** tradition in ascetic-devotional editions.*

Setting aside the fact that, *ecclesiastically-historically viewed,* the **Pietism in its blossom** stands at the head of that time, several **parallel currents** are to be perceived in the devotional literature.

**The first direction** attaches — consciously and unconsciously — to the *mild manner of Spener,* who at all his [——] was a *faithful churchly Lutheran.* In folk-tune-like preaching, in folk-tune-like Scripture-exposition, in the *deep-going presentation of the Way of Salvation,* in the caring for the Soul and for the Evangelical House with prayer-books ordered by *the estates of Life* or by *the days of the Year,* one seeks a **healthy connection of Orthodoxy and Pietism** — a Pietism to cultivate ***on churchly foundation.*** The striving is to lead the folk raised in Lutheran Orthodoxy to new life *on churchly ground,* in leaning on the new thoughts of the time. Such **mild, free-from-all-excess Pietism** is represented, after Spener, especially in **Gießen** and **Frankfurt:**²⁵⁹

- At **Gießen** worked in this sense Spener's and Francke's student **Johann Christian Lange** (b. 25 Dec. 1669, †1756 as *Superintendent* at Idstein); **Johann Ludwig May** (†1719); and **Johann Jakob Rambach.** Rambach — although he had gone through the *Hallesche Schule* and was there Francke's successor — kept himself yet a *churchly-sober standpoint* and overcame the *one-sidednesses* of Halle Pietism.
- To Gießen and Frankfurt belonged **Johann Philipp Fresenius** with his famous *Kommunionbuch,* which, as few of the pietist books, gained *church-wide distribution.*²⁶⁰
- In **Hessen** worked in the same sense Fresenius's brother **Johann Friedrich Fresenius,** with his ***Abhandlung über die Rechtfertigung eines armen Sünders vor Gott*** (which Vilmar newly issued in 1857, but which is now out of print).
- In **Frankfurt** itself a healthy Pietism was above all represented by **Starck,** and through his *most-influential, most-solid writings* nourished.
- A mild, church-healthy Pietism was represented also by the famous writings of **Georg Nitschke** (b. 1663 at Alt-Strelitz, 1692 *Kollaborator* at Braunschweig, 1693 preacher at Wolfenbüttel, 1709 *Generalsuperintendent* at Gotha, †1729), especially his ***Theologische Sendschreiben.***²⁶¹ In 1841 and 1863 these appeared under the title ***"Übung in der Heiligung"*** at Halle in the Mühlmann press, edited by **Besser,** but are now out of print. The same holds of ***Die thätige und wahrhaftige Christenliebe*** (Hamburg, 1859), freshly worked by B. Stein, with a preface by *Generalsuperint.* Hoffmann. — The ***Unterricht vom Sabbath und Vorsabbath der Seele*** (received into ***Löhes Hausbuch,*** appearing in **Caspar Calvörs,** *Generalsuperint.* at Klausthal, ***Erbaulichen Schriften*** 1691 and 1711) belongs likewise in this direction, and gives sound norms for a blessed prayer-life.
- A sober pietistic Scripture-exposition was shown by **Christoph Starcke.** Similar in character is the ***Basler Bibel,*** stemming from Reformed circles.

**The second direction of Pietism** — leaving Spener's sober manner — attaches itself to **August Hermann Francke** and his students, and found through the University of Halle and the ***Franckesche Anstalten*** *dissemination throughout all Germany.*²⁶² The kernel and the star of all writings of this direction is the *aim upon hearty conversion,* or upon giving *nourishment to the already-converted Christian.* Since it is mostly the **converted Christians** that are to be served with edification, the *folk-tune character* suffers. The [pastoral-experiential] life-conditions which are represented cannot grant the ***creaturely world*** its full [rights], and the folk-tune language is *lost;* only for the *awakened* is the speech comprehensible. It comes in connection with the *favourite expressions of the Halle school.* *Wordy and stiff on the one hand, hearty and gripping on the other,* the by pietist principles deeply-grasped authors often speak. In the *latter* [gripping] direction stand especially the **Silesian-stemming** writers, much influenced also by the direction of the ***second Silesian poet-school,*** which prefers a *bombastic, ink-adorned* language.

The names of **August Hermann Francke** (with his many small edifying tracts for the deeper cultivation of the inner life), **Joachim Justus Breithaupt** († 1732), and **Joachim Lange** († 1744) — these three theological professors — and of **Christian Friedrich Richter** the physician (with many small edifying tracts for the deeper interiorisation of life), mark at Halle *this direction itself.*²⁶³ Closely attaches itself to it also, — who likewise belongs to Halle — **Carl Heinrich von Bogatzky,** with his in part manifold-imperishable writings which appeared for the first time from **1718 to 1782,** most numerously between **1740 and 1760.**

Otherwise the names of other Pietists of that time — such as **Johann Gottlieb Hillinger** († 1732 as *Superintendent* at Saalfeld), with ***Prozeß der Ratschlüsse Gottes*** (newly 1858 and 1867 at Stuttgart, now out of print) and ***Die Rechtfertigung des armen Sünders vor Gott*** (1865 Ansbach, out of print); or **Samuel Lau** († 1746 at Wernigerode), now little-known — deserve to be named. But a number of the *best representatives* of the Pietists that Halle has formed deserve — for their *significant, imperishable edifying writings* — more than others to be named: so **Johann Adam Steinmetz** († 1762 as *Abt* at Bergen), **Prince Heinrich XII. of Reuß-Schleiz** († 1784), and **Johann Porst** († 1728 as *Propst* at Berlin), whose ***Göttliche Führung der Seelen und Wachstum der Wiedergeborenen*** from 1722/28 — like few books — bore the Pietism into the *Christian folk.*²⁶⁴

Forgotten must not be **David Hollaz,** the representative of Pietism in Pommern, with his ***Gnadenordnung,*** ***Bahnbrecher nach dem Berge Zion,*** etc.; **Friedrich Grothe** († 1741 at Leipzig) with his ***Richtiger und untrüglicher Himmelsweg;*** **Ernst Gottlieb Woltersdorf** († 1758 at Sauban) with the tender Pietist tone and the [great] edifying writings; **Kreisberg** [from] 1747 [——]; and also [several other lesser-known] Pietist devotional writers of that time.

**The third direction of Pietism** is represented by the **Württembergers.**²⁶⁵ Both the Pietism of Spener and that of Francke found in Württemberg great influence. Here it came through **Andreä,** the well-churchly-oriented [Württemberg reformer] who worked at the *enlivening of the Christian folk* — [into an especially fertile soil]. Through an independent connection of the Württemberg wesen with the new influences of the [Pietism] it took on a *special form.* To this came, that the leaders of Pietism in Württemberg — a **Hedinger** and above all a **Bengel** — were through-and-through **original personalities,** who also kept an open eye for the [dangers] of the *other* Pietism.

So combined itself in the Württemberg Pietism *a sense that held to what Bengel and* [——] *gave,* on the other side also **freely researched and everywhere back to the Holy Scripture went.** The spiritual [striving] found its goal in this: to deepen themselves *in the Scripture.* Marker of the pietistic literature of Württemberg is the *distribution of Scripture-conforming preaching* and *deep folk-tune exposition of the Scripture.* The names **Hedinger, [——], Urlsperger, Konrad Rieger, Bengel** stand at the end of the previous [century] and at the beginning of the 18th [century]. **Steinhofer** stands soon in connection with, soon [independent] of, the **Brüdergemeinde** with his own [devotional works]. Belongs also **Marie Katharina Sophie von** [——], from Bengel's mediate or immediate school. — **Bengel, Fabricius, Rieger, Steinhofer,** and *Pietism* runs into the [nineteenth] century, and there stands now the **under Hofacker blossoming Pietism.**

**Beside the three main directions,** the reaching-around *pietistic ascetic literature* — which sought the depths of *every* [Lutheran] devotional-book — took from the Pietism much [learned]: hymns like **Schmolck's** ***Morgen- und Abendlieder*** were, partly in the 17th century still, [known throughout Germany and] became rapid favourites in the *circles of the folk in all Germany.* Also to be named are:

- **Erdmann Neumeister** († 1756 at Hamburg) — cantata-libretto poet of the *first grade* whose libretti Bach set to music in **five Cantatas** of the Leipzig cycle;
- The widely-distributed ***Schatzkästlein Luthers*** by **Schinmeyer;**
- The great Dresden **Löscher's** edifying smaller and larger works — the noble ***Andachtsschule,*** the ***Unterricht vom wahren Christentum*** (1711), etc.;
- **Franz Schaitberger** with his hearty-edifying ***Sendbrief*** — the paradigm *Salzburger-Exulanten-devotional writing;*
- [several other] pietistic and orthodox writers of the transition-generation.

The [Basel-Reformed] ascetic writing, as also the [Lutheran] devotional [literature altogether], often once again reaches back to the medieval **Masters** — especially **Thomas à Kempis** — whose ***De Imitatione Christi*** was in Große's own day in fresh Lutheran-devotional editions across the whole German market.

### §2. Toward the Character of the Peculiarity of Pietistic Piety

*Source: III a) §2, pp. 327–329. Große's compressed sociological-and-psychological characterisation of Pietist piety: diary-keeping, *Wiedergeburt*-emphasis, means-of-grace-diminishment, aristocratic-and-institutional demographics, and the 1730–1740 tipping-point at which Pietism became "the ruling ecclesiastical direction" — with its inevitable admixture of hypocrisy.*

It is impossible in the frame of this book to give a [meaningfully-complete] characterisation of the *Pietist piety.* Some pointers must suffice. The Pietist [worldview] developed through many schools, so that a [full analysis would be a book in itself]. The [——] here will be given: at points a longer development in a determined limited time; at points a single [example].

**The leaders of Pietism kept exact Diaries,** wherein they recorded their *self-observations* and [inner experiences] — [Woltersdorf II, pp. 2ff. is a paradigm sample]. **The significance of the *Means of Grace*** is thereby [diminished]; instead, the individual **differentiates himself from the "uncircumcised"** [world]. It is above all the **inner relationship of the [Christian] to God** that is emphasized; the [work-] hereupon [——]. Of the **visible Church** one sees mostly only *deficiencies.* The Means of Grace — with their power *independent of the movings and inner-conditions of the Heart* — step *into the background.* One *sinks oneself* into the Scripture — but partly to *observe* after her [——]. One wants only through *Sanctification* to be sanctified, and forgets over it to seek the *comfort of Justification.*

Because one lays all emphasis on the *subjective side of Sanctification* — with its *self-observation* of the *step upon which one stands* — the Pietism found its *echo* especially there, where one had **much time** for this: before all in the **aristocratic circles,** or where one was **forced to take the time** — in the *institutions for the education of the youth,* or where — as in the *spiritual office* — the *Seelenpflege of others must go hand-in-hand with the own Seelenpflege.* The **Folk** as such, which had *still much to work under great pressure,* remained to this special manner of pietistic devotion mostly rather *distant.* Among the **Educated** it was again the [aristocratic-and-clerical circles] to which it *most spoke,* corresponding to the [broadest] distribution.

Nevertheless, it is a fact, that in the years **1730–1740** the **Pietism had become such a power in the folk,** that on *spiritual ground* Pietism became the **ruling direction** — and, as it had been in the previous century with **Orthodoxy,** so was it now with **Pietism** — that it *also drew in* those who had not inwardly bowed to it. The Pietism — which had also excited its Adherents for the *conversion under Israel,* as also under the *Heathen in India and North America* (compare Ziegenbalg / Tranquebar at n. 180 above, and Zinzendorf's later Herrnhut-Herrenhaag-Herrnhaag mission) — was especially in **Saxony, Württemberg,** and all the smaller states **publicly acknowledged.**

That naturally, especially since the aristocratic circles of that [century] — almost all the small **Fürstenhöfe** having become *Mittelpunkte des Pietismus* — much **Hypocrisy** was found in those circles, who would expect otherwise? Even the Pietist Piety could not conquer the *Old Adam* when he would not [be conquered]. So complained *Amadeus* [Creutzberg / Woltersdorf?] and other of the *sober-pietistic voices* over the *appearance-piety* of the ruling-classes.
