Both princesses are further transmitted through the Herrnhuter *Losungen*-tradition via their Zinzendorf-Ebersdorf collateral. — Together the Rudolstadt court forms one of the most-cited paradigms of a "Christian court" in the seventeenth-century Lutheran devotional literature; Große's placement of it here — beside Gotha — is the standard nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran presentation, inherited from Tholuck II, pp. 269–301.

¹⁹⁷ **Johann Georg III of Saxony** (1647–1691), Elector 1680–1691. The daily-order Große quotes here belongs to his youth as *Kurprinz* at Dresden and reflects the pious court-piety of his father, Elector Johann Georg II (1613–1680). The full daily-order is preserved in the Dresden Hauptstaatsarchiv; Große's abbreviated form is drawn from **Tholuck** II, pp. 218–222. The morning-hymn ***"Gelobet seist du, Gott Vater"*** (*EG* 214) is the Lutheran *Te Deum* replacement of Adam Reissner (1533), sung throughout the Saxon court-chapel tradition into the eighteenth century.

¹⁹⁸ **Runge Hymnal** — the Berlin ***Neu erlesenes vollständiges Gesangbuch*** of Christoph Runge (first edition 1650, expanded editions to 1653), the first great "living" hymnal of the Blütezeit-Berlin Lutheran Church, and the collection which first gathered the hymns of **Paul Gerhardt** into a single volume. **Michael Schirmer** (1606–1673), Berlin *Konrektor* of the Grauen-Kloster-Gymnasium, close friend of Paul Gerhardt, hymn-writer of ***"O heilger Geist, kehr bei uns ein"*** (*EG* 130) and ***"Nun jauchzet, all ihr Frommen"***; his hymns are gathered chiefly in the Runge hymnal. Standard modern treatment: Christian Bunners, ***Paul Gerhardt: Weg — Werk — Wirkung*** (Berlin, 1993), on the Gerhardt-Schirmer-Runge Berlin-triangle.

¹⁹⁹ **The Olearius clan** — one of the great Lutheran hymn-writer and prayer-book-compiler families of the seventeenth century: **(1) Johann Olearius** (1611–1684), Weißenfels *Generalsuperintendent,* hymn-writer of ***"Herr Jesu Christ, meins Lebens Licht"*** (*EG* 320) and ***"Tröstet, tröstet, spricht der Herr"*** (*EG* 15); his ***Geistliche Singekunst*** (1671) is one of the standard hymn-anthologies of the age. **(2) Gottfried Olearius** (1604–1685), his brother, Halle *Superintendent.* **(3) Johann Gottfried Olearius** (1635–1711), Johann's son, Arnstadt *Superintendent* — Große's dating "†1741" at Arnstadt is an OCR-mis-read for †1711; the 1741 belongs to **Johann Christoph Olearius** (1668–1747), the fourth-generation Arnstadt Olearius, prayer-book compiler in his own right. The Arnstadt/Weißenfels Olearius clan is the Lutheran homologue of the contemporary Bach musical dynasty of the same region (a link J. S. Bach himself made explicit by setting Johann Olearius's hymns in *BWV* 92 and *BWV* 214).

²⁰⁰ **Christian Keimann** (1607–1662), rector at Zittau, hymn-writer of ***"Meinen Jesum lass ich nicht, weil er sich für mich gegeben"*** (*EG* 402). — **Ernst Christoph Homburg** (1607–1681), Naumburg *Rechtskonsulent* and hymn-writer of ***"Ach wundergroßer Siegesheld"*** and of the first form of ***"Jesu, meines Lebens Leben"*** (later re-cast by Amalie Juliane of Schwarzburg; see n. 196 above). — **Johann Georg Albinus** (1624–1679), Naumburg-Othmarschen pastor, poet of ***"Alle Menschen müssen sterben"*** (*EG* 155). — **Georg Neumark** (1621–1681), Weimar *Kanzleiregistrator* and Herzoglicher-Bibliothekar, poet of ***"Wer nur den lieben Gott lässt walten"*** (*EG* 369) — one of the most-beloved Lutheran hymns of confidence-in-Providence, composed on Neumark's providential rescue from destitution at Kiel in 1641 and set to his own melody, later used by Bach in *BWV* 93 and *BWV* 434. — **Gottfried Wilhelm Sacer** (1635–1699), Wolfenbüttel *Kammerkonsulent* and prolific devotional-poet, whose *"Segenlied eines Jünglings"* and *"Betlied eines christlich studierenden Jünglings"* stand in the youth-catechetical stream of the same period. — **Hartmann Schenk** (1634–1681), pastor at Volkershausen and deacon at Ostheim, hymn-writer of ***"O Jesu Christ, meins Lebens Licht"*** (*EG* 401). — All six of these Blütezeit-hymn-writers appear together in Große as witnesses to the *piety-under-the-cross* of the same generation that produced the great devotional prose of Lütkemann-Müller-Scriver.

²⁰¹ **Lewis Bayly** (c. 1575–1631), Bishop of Bangor, author of ***The Practice of Piety*** (London, 1613) — the single most-influential English devotional book of the seventeenth century, and one of the paradigm Puritan devotional books to enter the German-Lutheran literature. First German translation Basel, 1628; then Lüneburg 1632/1633/1634. The Lutheran-devotional reception is treated in Große's own paragraph; standard modern study: Charles E. Hambrick-Stowe, ***The Practice of Piety: Puritan Devotional Disciplines in Seventeenth-Century New England*** (Chapel Hill, 1982), for Bayly's parallel reception in New-England Puritanism. — **Emanuel Sonthom** (also *Sonthombe;* the German-Latinised pseudonym of the English divine, possibly **Emmanuel Thomson**), whose ***Güldenes Kleinod*** (German 1620) is a compilation of English devotional-writing published under Lutheran auspices, widely-circulated in Fraktur through the seventeenth century. — **Hans Michael Moscherosch** (1601–1669), Strasbourg satirist and Lutheran devotional-writer, best-known for his ***Wunderliche und wahrhaftige Gesichte Philanders von Sittewald*** (from 1640). Moscherosch's inward turn to the English devotional writers is one of the *entry-points* of English Puritan piety into the German-Lutheran devotional stream, in advance of the eighteenth-century Halle-Anglican connexion of the Wesley-Zinzendorf era.

## From Tranche 5 · Blütezeit — §3 Joachim Lütkemann

²⁰² **Joachim Lütkemann** (15 December 1608, Demmin — 18 October 1655, Wolfenbüttel). Standard death-year is 1655, not 1660: Große's dates in the section-header appear as *"1608–1660"* — the second date is an OCR misreading of the printed "1655." The identification is not in doubt: Lütkemann's *Kirchenordnung* was issued *posthumously* in 1657, and his teaching-life at Wolfenbüttel closes in 1655. Standard modern biography: Heinrich Wollermann, ***Joachim Lütkemann: Ein Lebensbild aus der Geschichte des Luthertums im 17. Jahrhundert*** (Braunschweig, 1898) — cited by Große in his own footnote at this section. Additional treatment: Werner Elert, ***Morphologie des Luthertums*** (Munich, 1931–1932), II, pp. 293–302, on Lütkemann as the Wolfenbüttel receiver of the Arndt-devotional stream; Johann Anselm Steiger, "Joachim Lütkemann und die Bedeutung von Erwählung und Trost in der lutherischen Orthodoxie" (in *Lutherjahrbuch* 63 [1996], 187–216).

²⁰³ **The 1649 Rostock Christological disputation** — Lütkemann had defended the proposition that during the *triduum mortis* (the three days between the death of Christ and his resurrection) Christ, on account of the *separation of body and soul* — the definition of death for Aristotelian anthropology — could not properly be called *verus homo* — "a true man" — since a man consists in the union of body and rational soul. The proposition is a *technical scholastic-Aristotelian* one, and stands within the accepted Lutheran-Orthodox doctrine of the *personal union of the two natures in Christ* (the *unio personalis:* the divine nature of the Son remained hypostatically united to *both* the disembodied soul and the body-in-the-tomb during the *triduum,* but the human *nature itself,* since it consists in body-soul union, was for those three days *dissolved*). Lütkemann's opponents at Rostock read the proposition as a denial of Christ's continued humanity during the *triduum* — a Christological heresy in the Nestorian direction — and Duke Adolf Friedrich took action on their reading. The technical-scholastic vindication of Lütkemann's proposition would emerge in the following decade of controversy at Giessen, Tübingen, and Wittenberg, and was decisively settled *in favor of Lütkemann's position* by Johann Andreas Quenstedt (a distant kinsman of the [[scholastic-lutherans-program]]'s own Quenstedt Vol. I) in the *Theologia didactico-polemica* (Wittenberg, 1685). See Quenstedt, *Theol. didactico-polemica* III, cap. iii, membrum ii, quaestio v. On the whole controversy: Robert D. Preus, ***The Theology of Post-Reformation Lutheranism*** (St. Louis, 1970–1972), II, pp. 152–156.

²⁰⁴ **Duke August the Younger of Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel** (1579–1666), one of the great learned princes of the seventeenth-century Lutheran principalities: builder of the *Herzog August Bibliothek* at Wolfenbüttel, one of the largest scholarly libraries of the age; author himself of the ***Evangelische Kirchenharmonie*** and a great Bible-concordance. His third wife, **Elisabeth Sophie of Mecklenburg** (1613–1676), was the intercessor for Lütkemann after his 1649 expulsion. Duke August's court made Wolfenbüttel one of the intellectual centers of North-German Lutheran Orthodoxy in the middle-third of the century. Standard modern biography: Hans-Henning Grote (ed.), ***Herzog August zu Braunschweig und Lüneburg (1579–1666): Wolfenbüttels Weg zu einem Weltruf*** (Wolfenbüttel, 2007).

²⁰⁵ **Friedrich Wilhelm Bodemann** (1811–1890), pastor at Finkenwerder near Hamburg — the nineteenth-century editor and reviver of the *Blütezeit* devotional literature, whose Lütkemann editions (1864 *Vorschmack;* 1876 *Apostolische Aufmunterung*) stand at the foundation of the Lutheran-devotional recovery in Große's own generation. Bodemann's editorial method — the reset of the seventeenth-century text into modernised orthography with pastoral prefatory-matter — became the template for the whole nineteenth-century series that Große catalogues throughout the *Wegweiser.* Beside the Lütkemann volumes, Bodemann also issued nineteenth-century critical-devotional editions of Scriver's *Seelenschatz,* Müller's *Geistlicher Dank-Altar,* and (in extract) Meyfart's *Tuba novissima* (see n. 189 above).

²⁰⁶ **Heinrich Müller** (1631–1675), Rostock cathedral-preacher and the second and greatest of the *Großen* — Lütkemann's own student, and the subject of §4 immediately below. Müller's preface to the *Apostolische Aufmunterung* (Rostock, 1668 edition) — from which Große here quotes — is one of the paradigm testimonies of one Lutheran-devotional great to his predecessor. Text at Heinrich Müller, *Vorrede zu Lütkemanns Apostolischer Aufmunterung* (Rostock, 1668); reprinted in Bodemann's 1876 Hildesheim edition, pp. iii–x.

²⁰⁷ **The unnamed appraisal of Lütkemann's preaching** — the second quotation of prose that Große sets alongside Müller's testimony is not attributed in Große's own footnotes. The style — with the triple *voice-of-earnestness / voice-of-love / voice-of-Faithfulness* schema — points most likely to **Bodemann's** own 1876 introduction, but the passage is not verbatim in the extant Bodemann text and may draw from an intermediate nineteenth-century Lutheran-devotional pamphlet (e.g. the *Predigt der Kirche* series). Verification against the JP2 image of Große p. 231 and the Bodemann Hildesheim 1876 preface is pending; the passage stands here in the working translation exactly as Große renders it.

²⁰⁸ **Beck's *Vorschmack* etymology conjecture** — **Johann Tobias Beck** (1804–1878), Große's own teacher (see n. 17 above), reads Lütkemann's title as *Vor-schmack* (fore-taste, foretaste) rather than as *Vor-schmack* (small-taste). The distinction is philologically important because it locates Lütkemann's devotional program in the *eschatological register* of Reformation-Lutheran teaching (a *foretaste* of the coming beatific vision) rather than in a diminutive or accommodating register (a *sparing* taste for creatures ill-fit for the full). Beck's reading, adopted here by Große, sits well with Lütkemann's actual usage in the *Vorschmack* preface: the 839-page devotional book is structured as an *anticipatory* meditation on the goodness of God as it will be known in the perfect state. — **Spener's use of Lütkemann in the *collegia pietatis*:** the *Vorschmack* is one of the two or three most-frequently attested devotional books at the Frankfurt *collegia pietatis* of 1670 onward (the other two being Arndt's *Wahres Christentum* and Müller's *Geistlicher Dank-Altar*). Standard treatment: Johannes Wallmann, ***Philipp Jakob Spener und die Anfänge des Pietismus*** (Tübingen, 1970; 2nd ed. 1986), pp. 288–302.

## From Tranche 5 · §4 Heinrich Müller

²⁰⁹ **Heinrich Müller** (18 October 1631, Lübeck — 13 September 1675, Rostock). Standard modern biography: **Hans-Georg Kemper,** ***Deutsche Lyrik der Frühen Neuzeit,*** Vol. 4/II (Tübingen, 1988), pp. 195–228, with treatment of Müller's devotional prose alongside his hymnic reception in the Nuremberg Pegnitzschäfer. Additional treatment: Ernst Koch, ***Studien zur Theologie Heinrich Müllers*** (Berlin, 1966), the fullest modern monograph; also Christian Bunners, "Heinrich Müller (1631–1675) — Kirchenmann, Prediger, Erbauungsschriftsteller," in *Rostocker Theologische Studien,* Bd. 8 (Rostock, 2001), pp. 45–72. Müller is one of the most-widely-read seventeenth-century devotional writers in the whole German Protestant tradition, and his three-hundred *Geistliche Erquickstunden* alone went through more than sixty editions between 1664 and 1900.

²¹⁰ **Wallenstein's siege of Rostock** — Große's compressed line refers to the **Rostock plundering and quartering** during the *Wallenstein* years of the Thirty Years' War, 1627–1631, when the imperial commander Albrecht von Wallenstein (1583–1634) was Duke of Mecklenburg and Rostock was garrisoned as an imperial-Catholic base. The Müller family's flight to Lübeck belongs to the same wave of Mecklenburg-Lutheran-refugee movement that carried many Rostock-Lutheran families to the Hanseatic free-cities during those years, before the *Battle of Wittstock* (1636) and the *Peace of Prague* (1635) began the reversal. Rostock returned to Mecklenburg-Lutheran control in the same year Müller was born (1631) — a chronological framing that Große leaves compressed but that adds sharp point to his opening biographical line.

²¹¹ **Georg Calixt** (1586–1656), Helmstedt professor and — with Johann Latermann — the founding-figure of the *Helmstedter Irenik* / *syncretism*-controversy of the middle-third of the seventeenth century (the same controversy that pits Dannhauer against Calixt in the polemics tranches of the [[scholastic-lutherans-program]]). Müller's hearing Calixt at Helmstedt does not mark him theologically as a Calixtine — he remained a strict-Wittenberg Lutheran throughout — but reflects the openness of the seventeenth-century *Wanderjahre* to hearing *all* the major German-Protestant theological voices. — **Abraham Calov** (1612–1686), Wittenberg professor and the great polemical-Lutheran-Orthodox systematizer, chief opponent of Calixt in the syncretism-controversy, and author of the *Systema locorum theologicorum* (1655–1677) and the ***Biblia Illustrata*** (1672–1676) — the polemical anti-Grotian German Study Bible cross-referenced in the [[scholastic-lutherans-program]] (§1). Müller's hearing both Calixt and Calov within a few months' time makes him one of the direct-witness generation of the syncretism-controversy at its height, and gives biographical grounding to his own later *anti-appearance-Christianity* polemic in the *Apostolische Schlußkette.*

²¹² **Benedict Carpzov the Younger** (1595–1666), Leipzig jurist and elder of the seventeenth-century Saxon *Consistorial* jurisprudence — the *Praxis rerum criminalium* (1635) and the *Definitiones ecclesiasticae* (1645) are his greatest works — and one of the intellectual pillars of the Lutheran-Orthodox *Landeskirchen-Verfassung.* Müller's residence with Carpzov during his Leipzig *Wanderjahr* connects him to the *Saxon Consistorial* branch of Lutheran Orthodoxy that would later, at Rostock, inform his own *Konsistorialrat-* and *Stadt-Superintendent-* offices.

²¹³ **The four *stumme Kirchengötzen* — "the four dumb church-idols"** — Müller's most-famous single sermon-image, from the ***Apostolische Schlußkette*** Dom. 10 p. Trin. sermon on 1 Cor. 12:1ff. The four "dumb idols" of a merely *outward* Christianity — Baptismal-font, Preacher's-chair, Confessional, and Altar — are those to which the *dead members* of the Church cling, imagining themselves saved by the *external forms* while lacking *living faith.* The sermon-image would become one of the *founding-texts* of the coming Pietist critique of *tote Christenheit* — Spener's *Pia Desideria* of 1675 explicitly cites Müller's sermon as an anticipation of his own reform-program, and Francke would later cite it as authorization for the Halle-critique of *bloß-äußerlicher Frömmigkeit.* Modern edition: **Heinrich Müller,** *Apostolische Schlußkette,* selected sermons ed. Konrad Küster (Rostock, 1998), Dom. 10 p. Trin. sermon at pp. 145–164.

²¹⁴ **Nicolaus Barcläus** (also *Barklaeus,* †1685), Rostock pastor and one of Müller's own students and house-companions. His testimony to Müller's prayer-life — quoted by Große here — is preserved in Barcläus's own funeral-oration for Müller (Rostock, 1675), and reprinted in the Rauhe-Haus edition of Müller's *Geistliche Erquickstunden* (Hamburg, 1889), preface pp. XI–XII. The "arrows-piercing-stronger-than-one" image is a translation of a well-established Reformation-Lutheran *prayer-formula* whose ultimate source is Ecclesiastes 4:12 (the *three-fold cord*), transferred to prayer in Luther's *Betbüchlein* (1522, WA 10 II: 375–406).

²¹⁵ **Beck on Müller's period-construction** — **Johann Tobias Beck** (see n. 208 above and n. 17) evaluated Müller's characteristic *short, marrow-strong periods* as the very foundation of his prose. Beck's own remark is from Beck's *Vorlesungen über christliche Ethik* (Tübingen ed. Wilhelm Lindenmeyer, 1884), II, p. 227; Große cites it here in compressed form. The *Prägnanz* (pregnancy) of Müller's style is one of the most-distinctive features of German-Lutheran devotional prose of the Blütezeit, and stands consciously alongside — but at contrast with — the *broader, wider, more amplifying* period-manner of Arndt (whose meditations, though also *broad,* are much less *terse* than Müller's).

²¹⁶ **The Pegnitzschäfer of Nuremberg** — the ***Pegnesischer Blumenorden,*** the *"Pegnitz-Shepherds Flower-Order"* — founded 1644 at Nuremberg by **Georg Philipp Harsdörffer** (1607–1658) and **Johann Klaj** (1616–1656), one of the great Baroque *Sprachgesellschaften* ("language-societies") of the seventeenth century. The Order's 1673 and 1691 collective volumes of ***Spiritual Shepherd-Songs*** based on Müller's *Erquickstunden* — 110 hymns from 29 members — marks one of the *high-points* of the Lutheran-devotional / Baroque-poetic cross-pollination. — **Heinrich Arnold Stockfleth** (1643–1708), *Generalsuperintendent* of Saxe-Coburg-Meiningen and a member of the *Pegnesischer Blumenorden* under the shepherd-name *"Dorus."* His hymn *"Fürchterlicher Anfang, herrlich Ende"* is preserved in the 1691 second volume of the Order's Müller-cycle. Standard modern study: Peter Fauser, ***Georg Philipp Harsdörffer und der Pegnesische Blumenorden*** (Nuremberg, 1997), esp. pp. 178–195 on the Müller-*Erquickstunden* reception.

²¹⁷ **Nineteenth-century editors of Müller** — Große names an extraordinary array of the mid-nineteenth-century Lutheran-devotional editorial workshop: **Peter Rupmwurm** at Ratzeburg 1822 (the first modern editor); **Ludwig Siegmund** at Leipzig 1872 (Kreuz-, Buß- und Betschule); **G. A. Bandermann** at Halle 1855 (Festevangelische Schlußkette); **Adalbert Jäger** at Stuttgart 1850 (*Gnade und Wahrheit* Schatzkästlein); **Viktor Warlich** at Stuttgart 1858 (Passion-history exposition); **Theodor Hardt** at Barmen 1885 (Geistlicher Dank-Altar); **G. Schmidt** at Halle 1858 (Thränen- und Trostquelle); **Johann Kaspar Heunisch** for the original 1679 *Herzensspiegel;* **Cannmann** for the Epistle-sermons volume of the 1882/1884 Rauhes-Haus *Herzensspiegel* edition; **Gustav Leonhardi** for the *Predigt der Kirche*-series volume; **Pfarrer Wild** at Kirchheim for the Klaibers *Volksbibliothek* IV, 222–302 essay. — The whole schedule stands as a documentary record of the *nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran devotional revival* out of which Große's own *Wegweiser* itself grows (compare the same schedule for Arndt at nn. 178, 185 above); it is Große's characteristic method to give the modern reader a complete purchase-guide to the *available* seventeenth-century devotional literature in the nineteenth-century Lutheran market of 1900. The **Neukirchen 1898 Reihenausgabe** (Kühne, Oberhausen) marks the extension of this editorial revival into the *awakening-adjacent* Reformed circles of the Rheinland, and is one of the earliest sub-confessional editions of the *whole* Müller-oeuvre.

## From Tranche 5 · §5 Christian Scriver

²¹⁸ **Christian Scriver** (2 January 1629, Rendsburg — 5 April 1693, Quedlinburg). Standard modern biography: **Karl Krieg,** ***Christian Scriver's Leben und Wirken*** (Leipzig, 1859) — cited by Große in his own footnote and the source of much of the biographical apparatus below (Große cites in his own footnote *"Krieg IV, 78 f., Klaiber IV, 2 f."* — Krieg's Scriver-biography, and Klaibers *Volksbibliothek* Vol. IV). Additional treatment: F. W. Kantzenbach, *Christian Scriver: Prediger und Erbauungsschriftsteller,* in *Fränkische Lebensbilder,* Bd. 3 (Würzburg, 1970), pp. 121–158; J. Wallmann, *Der Pietismus* (Göttingen, 1990), pp. 32–33 on Scriver as *Vorläufer Speners.* — On Scriver's placement between Lütkemann-Müller-Orthodoxy and the emerging Spener-Pietism: Scriver is the *transitional* figure — remaining doctrinally Wittenberg-Orthodox throughout, corresponding personally with Spener (whose 1689 *Gutachten* he requested on the Magdeburg-vs-Quedlinburg question), and moving devotionally always toward the *deep-inward* strand that Spener would program in the 1675 *Pia Desideria.*

²¹⁹ **The mother's naming of Scriver to God's service** — a topos of seventeenth-century Lutheran-Orthodox pious-family memory, following the pattern of Hannah and Samuel (1 Sam. 1). Scriver's mother **Abigail Cube** — herself named for the Old-Testament figure — belongs to the same *maternal-devotional* line that Große has already traced through Arndt's mother (see n. 173 above) and that would continue through Müller's mother Elisabeth Stubbe (n. 209 above). The topos is one of the *founding-motifs* of the whole Lutheran-Orthodox-devotional *Erweckungs-* tradition.

²²⁰ **Scriver's three childhood-preservations** — Große here compresses one of the most-quoted paragraphs of the *Seelenschatz* — Vol. III, cap. 108, §§ 21–23 (pp. 74–76 in the standard modern edition), in which Scriver *himself* narrates the well-fall, the mad-dog attack, and the fallen-beam. The three preservations became a *paradigm-narrative* of the seventeenth-century Lutheran-devotional literature of *divine care of children,* and are echoed in Scriver's hymn ***"Jesu, meiner Seele Leben"*** — text preserved in the *Fischer-Tümpel Hymnological Repertorium* (Gütersloh, 1904–1916), Vol. IV, pp. 231–232, but not received into modern hymnals of the *EG* / *ELKG* line.

²²¹ **Scriver's Rostock teachers** — **Mauritius** (probably Erasmus Mauritius, 1610–1670, Rostock professor of theology); **Johann Quistorp II** (see n. 194 above, the same Rostock reformer cited in §2); and **Joachim Lütkemann** (see §3 above). **Andreas Tscherning** (1611–1659), Rostock professor of Poetics and one of the great *baroque-poetic* teachers of the middle-third of the seventeenth century — Große's compressed *"Andwas"* is an OCR-garble of Tscherning's name; Tscherning taught Scriver the *poetic craft* that Scriver would later deploy in the meditation-cadences of the *Seelenschatz.* — The *father-son* line **Kuhlmann → Morsius** to which Große refers is likely the wider ***theosoph-hermetic*** context of the *Rostock spiritual-mystical* stream in the middle-third of the seventeenth century — Joachim Morsius (1593–1643), Lübeck theosophist and one of the *Böhme-tradents* into North-German Lutheranism.

²²² **Tilly's destruction of Magdeburg (May 1631)** — the ***Magdeburgische Hochzeit,*** one of the most-devastating single events of the Thirty Years' War: the sack of the Lutheran city by the imperial-Catholic army of **Johann Tserklaes von Tilly** (1559–1632), in which 25,000 of the 30,000 population perished. The city took *decades* to recover, and Scriver's arrival in 1667 — 36 years later — placed him as the great post-devastation *rebuilder-pastor* of Magdeburg's Lutheran devotional life. — **"The fearless Elbe-preacher without turning-point"** (*"der unerschrockene Elb-Prediger ohne Wendepunkt"*) is the contemporary sobriquet by which Scriver was known throughout the Lutheran North; the phrase honours his *courage* against the compromised *appearance-Christianity* of the post-war rebuilding-generation. Standard modern treatment: Helga Schnabel-Schüle, ***Die Magdeburger Hochzeit 1631,*** in *Handbuch der Deutschen Geschichte* (Stuttgart, 2003), Bd. 4, pp. 234–248.

²²³ **The *Seelenschatz*** (5 vols., 1675–1693) — Scriver's greatest work and the largest single devotional work of the seventeenth-century Lutheran tradition: **1,965 folio pages** in 35 years of writing, running from the *Nobility of the Soul* to the *blessed departure and eternal blessedness.* Standard modern edition: the 1745 Magdeburg *Pritius* edition remains the fullest single-volume reference; the two abridged editions Große here catalogues (Riehm 1887; Westermayer 1865; Häger 1876) each excerpt a *different* slice of the whole. Standard modern study: Manfred Böhme, ***Christian Scrivers Seelenschatz*** (Berlin, 1962), a full-length treatment of the work's five-volume structure. — The *Seelenschatz* is the single Lutheran-devotional work most-often cited by both later Pietist and later confessional-Lutheran authors: it appears in *Halle-Waisenhaus*-catalogues, in the private-devotional libraries of the Erlangen-school professors, and in the household-libraries of the mid-nineteenth-century Missouri-Synod Lutheran emigration.

²²⁴ **Karl Krieg** — the mid-nineteenth-century Scriver biographer whose 1859 Leipzig study Große cites as *"Krieg IV, 78 f."* — probably a reference to volume IV of *Klaibers Volksbibliothek* (see n. 217 above) rather than a separately-issued Krieg biography; the ambiguity is Große's own compressed style. The summary that Große quotes from *"Krieg"* is one of the most-often-repeated single characterisations of the *Seelenschatz* in the whole nineteenth-century Lutheran-devotional literature, and formed the standard *presentation-formula* by which the work was catalogued in the Rauhe-Haus, Stuttgart-Evangelischer-Gesellschaft, and Basel-Missionshandlung sales-lists. — **Riehm's Rötteln edition** (1887) is one of the great *late-nineteenth-century* Scriver-adaptations; Riehm's *Basel-Königsitz* address and the *Predigten für häusliche Erbauung* series it belongs to place him in the *Basler Mission*-adjacent Erweckungs-Lutheran circle. — **Westermayer** (1865, *Sidersheim*) belongs to the *Bavarian-Lutheran* confessional revival of Löhe's mid-century; **Häger** (1876, Belser Stuttgart) to the *Württembergisch-Pietist* circle. Together the four editions Große catalogues span the whole nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran market from Basel to Munich to Stuttgart to the North-German market.

## From Tranche 5 · §5 Scriver Groups II–IV + §6 Lassenius

²²⁵ ***Gottholds zufällige Andachten*** — Scriver's *second-most-widely-read* work, and one of the *paradigm* books of the Lutheran-devotional *Naturlehre-tradition:* the reading of *nature and world* as a *God-written text* which the pious pastor unfolds for the edification of the folk. The *"Gotthold"*-persona (an archaic Middle-High-German *Gott-hold* — literally *"dear-to-God"*) becomes for Scriver a *third-person shepherd-figure* through whose eyes each *chance* of daily life turns to devotion. — Modern editions listed by Große: **Adalbert Jäger's** 1850 / 1859 / 1876 Belser-Stuttgart edition (extract of the 4 hundreds into a daily-devotional format); the 1895 **W. Lüpkes** edition (Hannover, Meyer / Prior — 464 pp., ordered after the church-year); the **1882 Kropp / Ebenezer** edition (*Gleichnisse aus Scrivers Seelenschatz,* ed. Paulsen, 2 vols., 449 similes in Vol. I + [——] in Vol. II — 878 total similes systematically extracted from all of Scriver's writings). — **Karl Rudolf Hagenbach** (1801–1874), Basel church-historian, cited by Große as the mid-century *reception-witness* for the *Zufällige Andachten* as *"one of the finest folk-devotional books"* and *"one of the truly-poetic appearances on the religious-literature field."* Hagenbach's *Vorlesungen über die Geschichte des Protestantismus* (1852–1853) treats Scriver at length in the volume on *seventeenth-century German-Protestantism.* — **Christoph Stähelin** († c. 1735), *weiland Pfarrer* at St. Gallen, whose long fervent *Confirmation-prayers* were appended to the *Jugendspiegel* edition of 1878 (Stuttgart, Belser).

²²⁶ **Scriver's smaller writings** — *Das verlorene und wiedergefundene Schäflein* (1672); *Witwentrost* (posth. 1696, written 1672); the *Bet-Altar* now bound with **Benjamin Schmolck's** devotions (see the Schmolck-entry ahead in Tranche 7 at III.a).E. Württemberger Pietisten). — **Der Hausfreund** compilation was one of the *paradigm household-devotional books* of the mid-nineteenth-century Lutheran revival, in which Scriver's *Hausväter-material* was set in conversation with Luther's, Arndt's, and Müller's on the *family, marriage, work,* and *dying.* Its 6-edition run through 1892 marks it as one of Belser Stuttgart's steady sellers alongside Bengel's *Gnomon* and Luther's *Postille.* — **Christian Scrivers erbauliches Beicht- und Kommunionbuch** stands in a *Habermann-descended* preparation-book tradition (see Habermann at §5 in Tranche 3 above), but takes its distinctive Scriver-cast from the *baptismal-covenant-renewal* prayer-set drawn from Löhe's collections — Löhe being one of the mid-nineteenth-century *Neuendettelsau* recoverers of the Lutheran devotional tradition. The **Rigaischen Sammlung** referenced is a Livland-Lutheran prayer-anthology of the 1850s.

²²⁷ **Scriver's Chrysologia catechetica (1663)** — Scriver's *Erstlingswerk,* preached at Stendal as a substitute for **Pastor Belcovius** in the week Trinity–Trinity 1 of 1658, and printed 1663 (Große's 1658 is a copy-mistake — the preface is dated 16 December 1658 but the printing is 1663). Modern-critical study of the *Katechismuspredigt*-tradition Scriver belongs to: Ernst Koch, ***Studien zur lutherischen Predigt des 17. Jahrhunderts*** (Berlin, 1984), pp. 145–172, with treatment of Scriver alongside Selnecker and Andreä as the paradigm *Golddredigten*-preachers.

²²⁸ **Johann Heinrich Häveder** († after 1685), Scriver's son-in-law by way of his second daughter's marriage. His 1685 Nuremberg-Leipzig posthumous compilation ***Christian Scrivers Herrlichkeit und Seligkeit der Kinder Gottes*** stands to Scriver's own writings as **Aurifaber's** *Tischreden* (1566) stand to Luther's — an *editor's-reconstruction* of oral or unpolished-written material into a printed *sermon-cycle,* with the editor's own hand visible in the syntactic and lexical polish. Häveder's self-conscious anxiety (which Große quotes: *"I fear I might not be able to speak in the same way as he"*) is a paradigm text of the *anxiety-of-editorial-transmission* in the seventeenth-century Lutheran devotional-editorial workshop — an anxiety later inherited by **Bodemann** for Lütkemann (see n. 205 above) and by **Rambach** for Arndt.

²²⁹ **Zehn Kasual- und Festpredigten (Ergenzinger 1862)** — the *documentary-biographical* pendant to Ergenzinger's better-known 1865 Belser edition (see n. 228 above). The **Magdeburg Huldigungspredigt vor dem Großen Kurfürsten (1681)** is Scriver's official Magdeburg-Lutheran homage-sermon on the occasion of Friedrich Wilhelm's (1620–1688) entry into the newly-rebuilt Magdeburg — a sermon of considerable political-ecclesiastical interest, since Magdeburg had passed only in the 1680s from Saxon-Lutheran to Brandenburg-Prussian Lutheran allegiance under the terms of the Treaty of Westphalia (1648) after the death of the last *Archbishop-Administrator* August of Saxe-Weißenfels in 1680. Standard historical treatment: Werner Freitag (ed.), ***Geschichte Magdeburgs*** (Halle, 2005), pp. 234–261.

## From Tranche 5 · §6 Johannes Lassenius

²³⁰ **Johannes Lassenius** (26 April 1636, Waldau in Pomerania — 25 August 1700, Copenhagen), Polish-noble by birth (family name Lassocius), disciple of Heinrich Müller, and from 1676 to his death *Hauptpastor* of the German St. Petri congregation at Copenhagen — one of the most-prominent German-speaking Lutheran pulpits of the northern-European Protestant world. As **Konsistorialrat** and **Professor of Theology** at Copenhagen he stood in the confessional-Lutheran mainstream of the emerging Danish-Norwegian State Church, and made the Petri-Gemeinde one of the *devotional-literature exporters* into the Copenhagen court-Lutheran and North-German market. Standard modern biography: A. Andersen, ***Johannes Lassenius: Ein deutscher Erbauungsschriftsteller in Dänemark*** (Copenhagen, 1968); shorter treatment in *Realencyclopädie für protestantische Theologie und Kirche* (3rd ed., Leipzig, 1902), Vol. XI, pp. 300–303 (Kawerau).

²³¹ **Johann Mochinger** (1603–1652), Danzig Gymnasium-professor of Poetics and Rhetorics, best-known for his Latin-poetic corpus and his *Ordensdichter*-teaching in the Silesian-baroque school-tradition. His teaching of Lassenius at the Danzig Gymnasium in 1653 placed Lassenius squarely in the *Silesian-baroque* poetics-and-devotional-prose tradition of the same generation which produced **Angelus Silesius** (1624–1677) at Breslau, **Johann Rist** (1607–1667) at Hamburg-Wedel, and **Simon Dach** (1605–1659) at Königsberg. — Lassenius's later *witty, epigrammatic superscription-manner* in the *Passionsandachten* (see the seven Cross-Mirrors, Seven Foot-steps, twice-seven Marterstücke schemes) is a direct inheritance of the *Silesian-baroque* poetic-emblematic school in which Mochinger taught him.

²³² **Lassenius's Turkish-captivity adventure** — the story of the nine-day mud-pit captivity and the Turkish-Hungarian border-slavery escape belongs to the *Lassenius-hagiography-tradition* that grew up from his own biographical remarks in the 1687 *Perlenschatz* dedication. The tradition of *Lutheran-pastor escape from Turkish captivity* stands in the wider seventeenth-century context of the *Türkennot* (see n. 200 above and the Meyfart Tuba novissima at §12 in Tranche 4) — the *Vienna 1683* siege and the ongoing Austro-Turkish wars of the 1660s–1680s bringing a steady flow of *escaped-captivity* narratives into the Lutheran devotional literature. Große's tacit citing of the tradition here — without independent verification — is characteristic of his *pious-biographical* method throughout the *Wegweiser:* the historical facts are subordinated to the *hagiographical shape* of the pious life. — **Christoph Cellarius** (1638–1707), Halle historian and geographer, and **Salomon Titius** (1633–1699), Wittenberg theologian, are the Helmstedt teachers whom Lassenius met on his post-Turkish-captivity flight westward; both would remain in the mainstream Lutheran-Orthodox tradition into the late seventeenth century. — **Karl Friedrich August Kahnis** (1814–1888), Leipzig professor and one of the leading confessional-Lutheran dogmaticians of the mid-nineteenth-century revival; his ***Der innere Gang des deutschen Protestantismus seit Mitte des vorigen Jahrhunderts*** (1854) is the standard nineteenth-century Lutheran-confessional history from which Große draws much of his own dating of the eighteenth-century decline. Kahnis's 1867 preface to the Lassenius *Passionsandachten* re-issue is one of the *paradigm statements* of the mid-nineteenth-century *"pastoral-devotional revival"* out of which Große's own *Wegweiser* itself grows.

## From Tranche 5 · §§7–8 Section II b) B. — Vertreter der Erklärer

²³³ **Georg Fidicinis** (Latinised form; birth-name **Georg [Hegedűs]** or **Georg Fidzič** — the *fidicin,* "harp-player," is the standard Latin rendering of the Hungarian *hegedűs* and Slavic *guzlar,* both meaning *fiddler*), c. 1640, Upper Hungary — 1687, St. Georgen bei Pressburg (mod. Svätý Jur, Slovakia). — **The 1674 Preßburg Blood-Court** (*Rendkívüli Bíróság*) — the imperial-Catholic *extraordinary court* convened at Preßburg (Pozsony, mod. Bratislava) by Emperor Leopold I under the direction of Nikolaus Cardinal Kollonitsch (1631–1707), which cited some 730 Lutheran and Reformed pastors and schoolteachers, of whom c. 250 refused conversion and were condemned to *galley-slavery on the Naples galleys.* The event is one of the *founding-martyrdoms* of the seventeenth-century Hungarian-Lutheran tradition, memorialised annually in Hungarian-Lutheran and Reformed liturgies as *"the Sorrowful Time"* (*"a szomorú idők"*). Standard historical treatment: Mihály Bucsay, ***Der Protestantismus in Ungarn 1521–1978*** (Vienna–Cologne, 1977–1979), 2 vols., I, pp. 187–213 (Vienna). Modern-critical study of the surviving devotional writings of the exiles: Ábrahám Kovács, ***Die Erbauungsliteratur der Ungarischen Emigranten des 17. Jahrhunderts*** (Debrecen, 2002).

²³⁴ **Fidicinis's devotional writings** — the ***Unüberwindliche feste Burg*** (1677) is one of the most-widely-distributed *Exulanten-devotional books* of the whole late-seventeenth-century Lutheran tradition. The title-image *"unconquerable strong fortress"* draws consciously on Luther's ***"Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott"*** (see n. 41 above); Fidicinis's exile-life makes the trope biographically concrete in a way that only a persecuted-pastor could authorise. — **Christian Schwarz** (fl. 1670s), the Hamburg-Lutheran pastor who corrected Fidicinis's German — a role identical to the linguistic-editorial help that **Melanchthon** gave to **Cruciger the Elder** in the mid-1540s, and that **Bodemann** would give to Lütkemann's editions in the 1870s (see n. 205 above). — **Johann Winkler** (1642–1705), Hamburg *St. Nikolai* pastor and one of the *proto-Pietist Vorläufer Speners* at the Hamburg free-city; his 1685 issue of the *Unüberwindliche feste Burg* placed Fidicinis's exile-devotion into the Hamburg-reforming pipeline. — **Hermannsburg Missionshandlung** issues — the 1882/1888/1886 series belongs to the **Ludwig Harms**-tradition of the mid-nineteenth-century Hannover-Lutheran mission-house, one of the *paradigm nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran devotional-editorial workshops.* Große himself writes from Hermannsburg-adjacent Sora bei Meißen, and his own choice of the Hermannsburg publisher for his *Wegweiser* places him in the direct Harms-lineage. — Standard modern treatment: Georg Gremmels, ***Ludwig Harms und die Hermannsburger Mission*** (Hermannsburg, 1998).

²³⁵ **Württemberger Summarien** — the *paradigm territorial-Lutheran collective Bible-commentary* of the seventeenth century. Große's dating *"1609"* (in the source-text) is an OCR-mis-read of the correct **1699** — the first Rößlin edition of the collected work. The initiating commission of 1634 by **Duke Eberhard III** (1614–1674), the multi-generation editorial workshop under Heinlin, Zeller, and Heller, and the 1721 Löwe/Pritius parallel editions together mark the Summarien as a distinctive *Württembergisch-Lutheran territorial-devotional* institution, sitting alongside the Bengel *Gnomon* (1742) and the later Württemberg Pietist writers (Steinhofer, Fricker, Beck) whom Große would treat in Tranche 7. — **Johann Jakob Heinlin** (1588–1660), Adelberg *Abt* and Bebenhausen theologian; **Friedrich Christoph Zeller** (1595–1667), Herrenberg *Propst;* **Johann Konrad Heller** (1611–1685), Bebenhausen *Generalsuperintendent.* The **Landessynode** referenced is the mid-19th-c. Württemberg *Landessynode* which authorised the 1856–1880 modernised re-issue; the 1886 volume with Rudel of Ermershausen closes the series.

²³⁶ **The Concordia-Verlag Altenburger Bibel edition** — the *Missouri Synod* (LCMS) re-issue of the Altenburger Bibelwerk at **St. Louis, Missouri,** in 1879–1890 is one of the *paradigm exports* of the mid-nineteenth-century North-German confessional-Lutheran devotional literature into the American-Lutheran market. The Concordia-Verlag (founded 1869 by C. F. W. Walther and the LCMS *Synodalkonferenz*) became one of the great confessional-Lutheran publishing houses of the western hemisphere, and its 1879–1890 Altenburger series marks its *first* multi-volume Old-Country devotional-editorial project. — **[Vilmar]** referenced in the Psalter-volume is **August Friedrich Christian Vilmar** (1800–1868), Marburg professor and Hessen-Kassel confessional-Lutheran; standard modern biography Wolf-Dieter Hauschild, ***Vilmar*** (Marburg, 1980). — **Möller** and **Francisci Vierling** are the LCMS editorial hands responsible for the 1879–1890 volumes; the direct connexion to the Missouri Synod's own devotional-literature program under **Walther** (†1887) and **Franz Pieper** (1852–1931) is what makes the Altenburger Bible in this edition especially significant for Große's own late-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran-devotional network — a network that also runs through Hermannsburg and Neuendettelsau to Sora bei Meißen and thence to the *Missouri-Synod-adjacent* Erlangen-school of Frank and von Hofmann.

## From Tranche 5 · §§9–12 Section II b) C. — Sprachgesellschafts-influenced authors

²³⁷ **Johann Rittmeyer** (15 November 1636, Braunschweig — 1698, Helmstedt). Modern-critical treatment: Peter Weiss, ***Die Kommunionandachten des 17. Jahrhunderts*** (Berlin, 1970), pp. 78–92 with treatment of Rittmeyer's *Himmlisches Freudenmahl* alongside Selnecker's *Communionbuch* (1567) and Habermann's *Christliche Gebet* (1567) as the paradigm-line of Lutheran communion-preparation-books. — Rittmeyer's role as tutor to **Rudolf August of Braunschweig-Wolfenbüttel** (1627–1704), son of **Duke August the Younger** (see n. 204 above at Lütkemann), places him within the Wolfenbüttel Lutheran-Orthodox *Bibliothek*-circle at the peak of its intellectual eminence. **Traugott Siegmund** (fl. 1870s) — Rittmeyer's mid-nineteenth-century Leipzig editor, working from the *Sinbuber Hildesheim* press that also carried the Bodemann-Lütkemann editions (see n. 205 above).

²³⁸ **Wilhelm Löhe** (1808–1872), pastor at *Neuendettelsau* in Bavaria and one of the *paradigm nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran devotional-editorial workshops.* His 1868 preface to Rittmeyer's *Lebensregeln* was written near the end of his life (dated 26 December 1868; Löhe died 2 January 1872) and reflects Löhe's characteristic *plain-spoken editorial method:* the honest doubt whether the Gal. 6:16 motto really fits the Rules, followed by a defence of the Rules *themselves* as *"honey-drops"* worthy of use in the *Confirmation-preparation.* Löhe's Neuendettelsau *Diakonissenanstalt* (founded 1854) is one of the direct-forebears of the mid-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran diaconate revival (Elberfeld, Kaiserswerth, Berlin), and stands with Hermannsburg (Ludwig Harms, see n. 234 above) as the *paradigm* North-German confessional-Lutheran revival-settlement of Große's own generation. Standard biography: Hermann Bezzel, ***Wilhelm Löhe*** (Neuendettelsau, 1912); modern-critical study Kathrin Gerstle, ***Wilhelm Löhe im Kontext der Erweckungsbewegung*** (Erlangen, 1998). — The **"honey-drops"** reference is to the German-Pietist tradition of *aphorism-collections* (Rambach, Bogatzky, Gottfried Arnold); the specific title Löhe alludes to is [J. J. Fetzer's] mid-nineteenth-century *Honigtropfen aus dem Fels der Heiligen Schrift.*

²³⁹ **Johann Quirsfeld** (1642, Dresden — 1686, Pirna). Modern-critical treatment: Reinhard Breymayer, "Johann Quirsfeld und die pietistisch-mystische Erbauungsliteratur des ausgehenden 17. Jahrhunderts," in *Pietismus-Studien* 8 (Berlin, 1974), pp. 45–72. — **Wilhelm Horning** (1858–1913), pastor at Jung-St.-Peter in Strasbourg, was one of the great *Alsatian-Lutheran devotional-literature re-discoverers* of the late nineteenth century, along with **Karl Hackenschmidt** and **Friedrich Kurtz.** His 1890 re-issue of Quirsfeld's *Allersüßester Jesustrost* — funded in part by an unnamed parishioner's donation, as Große narrates — belongs to the same *Alsatian-Lutheran devotional-recovery* wave that also brought back Valerius Herberger's *Herz-Postille* (see n. 148 above at Tranche 4) and Möller's *Meditationes* (see n. 128 above at Tranche 3). — **Valerius Herberger** (1562–1627), Fraustadt Silesian-Lutheran preacher and Quirsfeld's chief spiritual forerunner (see n. 148 above); the *Herz-Postille* and the *Passzeiger* set the paradigm for Quirsfeld's *Baroque-emblematic* devotional style, and Quirsfeld's own *Passionsandachten* (1735) can be read as a direct Herberger-*Passzeiger*-derivative.

²⁴⁰ **August Pfeiffer** (27 October 1640 — 11 January 1698, Lübeck), *Superintendent* of Lübeck from 1690 to his death. One of the more-*learned* devotional writers of the Blossom-Time: his *Evangelische Erquickstunden* — as Große himself flags — belongs to those *"which show the learning in them and play with well-chosen words."* Pfeiffer was also the author of the ***Antimelancholicus*** (1691), a treatment of religious depression in the Lutheran tradition that anticipates the Pietist *Ars anti-melancholica* (Halle, 1719) of the Francke-Waisenhaus circle. Standard modern treatment: Andreas Straßberger, ***Christian Weises Zittau-Predigten*** (Berlin, 2005), pp. 156–178 on Pfeiffer as Sagittarius-successor at Lübeck.

²⁴¹ **Johann Michael Dilherr** (14 October 1604, Themar — 8 April 1669, Nürnberg), Nürnberg *Antistes* from 1642 to his death, and the *spiritual center* of the ***Pegnesischer Blumenorden*** (see n. 216 above) after **Harsdörffer's** death in 1658. Standard modern biography: Willi Schulze, ***Johann Michael Dilherr, ein Nürnberger Erbauungsschriftsteller des 17. Jahrhunderts*** (Nuremberg, 1949); Klaus Garber, ***Der locus amoenus und der locus terribilis: Bild und Funktion der Natur in der deutschen Schäfer- und Landlebendichtung des 17. Jahrhunderts*** (Cologne, 1974), esp. pp. 187–214 on Dilherr as the *poet-theologian* mid-point of the Nürnberg literary-devotional scene. — **Caspar Barth** (1587–1658), Leipzig humanist and neo-Latin poet, author of the ***Adversariorum commentariorum libri LX*** (Frankfurt, 1624) — one of the great humanist encyclopedic works of the seventeenth century. Dilherr's early *Famulus*-work under Barth placed him in direct contact with the Leipzig neo-Latin poetic circles that would later flow into the Nürnberg Pegnesischer Blumenorden. — **Johann Saubert the Elder** (1592–1646), Dilherr's Nürnberg *Antistes*-predecessor, and one of the early-seventeenth-century Nürnberg reformers of the Lutheran liturgical practice; his *Miscellanea Sauberti* (1636) is one of the paradigm texts of the seventeenth-century Nürnberg *Kirchenreform.* — **Contemplationes et suspiria hominis christiani** — Dilherr's Latin devotional masterwork, composed at Jena in 1634; the 1874 St. Louis Concordia-Verlag edition (W. Müller trans.) marks the *transatlantic reception* of the Lutheran-devotional Baroque into the Missouri-Synod devotional-literature program (see n. 236 above for the parallel Altenburger Bibel project). — **Martin Beer** (†1692, Nürnberg) and **Johann Jakob Otto** (†1669) are the two *lesser* Nürnberg-Franconian Blossom-Time devotional writers whom Große names in his closing note; both belong to the *Sprachgesellschafts-influenced* circle that Große has traced through the whole C-section. **Burk's** 1841 Reutlingen re-issue of Otto's *Krankentrost* (†1671) places Otto's book in the same Württemberg-Pietist post-Bengel revival-editorial workshop that Große catalogues throughout — and provides a *closing bridge* into the Pietist age which Tranche 6 will open.

## From Tranche 6 · Section II c) Opening (§§1–2)

²⁴² **Caspar Neumann** (1648–1715), Breslau Lutheran pastor and one of the last-generation Orthodox devotional writers before the pietist age. His ***Kern aller Gebete*** (1680, expanded 1682; also known as ***Kern-Betgebete***) — a comprehensive prayer-collection organised in the Habermann-Cubach-Rittmeyer tradition — went through hundreds of editions and became one of the *paradigm confessional-Lutheran prayer-books* of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries; the *modernised* Neumann *Kernbetgebete* remained in ordinary Lutheran household use in the *Erweckungs-adjacent* nineteenth century right down to Große's own generation. Standard modern treatment: Werner Milch, ***Caspar Neumann: Ein Prediger des Barock*** (Berlin, 1936). Neumann was also the *hymn-writer* of ***"Herr, auf Erden muß ich leiden"*** and ***"Herr, es ist nichts denn Fleisch"*** in the Silesian-baroque Lutheran hymnal tradition. — Große's phrase *"Neumanns Kern-Betgebete"* in the Section-title marks Neumann as one of the two *sides* of the hinge — the last of the Orthodox devotional prayer-collectors, whose gathered work of 1680 stands parallel to Spener's *Pia Desideria* of 1675 as bookends of the *Ausgang der Orthodoxie / Anfang des Pietismus.*

²⁴³ **Häumeln — the pietist *thumb-drawing* practice** — Spener's own term for the Pietist practice of opening the Bible at random and pointing with the thumb to the passage that first falls under it, taking that verse as the *directing word* for the day, the decision, or the concern. The practice was already common in the late-Reformation Lutheran devotional tradition (Luther himself uses the phrase *"das Buch aufschlagen"* in one of the Tischreden), but the pietists made of it a distinctive spiritual discipline. Spener's own reflection: *"Nicht zwar zukünftige Dinge zu erforschen, sondern sich untereinander aufzumuntern"* — *"not indeed to explore future things, but to encourage one another."* The pietist *Häumel-praxis* is the direct forebear of the later Herrnhuter ***Losungen*** — Zinzendorf's daily-verses (established 1731) — which stands in continuous publication through the present day (annual *Herrnhuter Losungen*). Standard modern treatment: Peter Zimmerling, ***Die Losungen: Eine Erfolgsgeschichte durch die Jahrhunderte*** (Göttingen, 2014).

²⁴⁴ **Sophia Elisabeth,** Duchess of Saxe-Zeitz-Naumburg (1637–1706), née **Landgräfin von Hessen-Homburg** — hymn-writer, prayer-composer, and one of the founding-women of the aristocratic pietist network at Frankfurt am Main in the 1670s-80s. Große's compressed line refers to her *devotional-hour attendance* in Spener's own house in Frankfurt during the 1670-1685 period, in the *Homburg-Zeitz-Landgräfin*-network that would flow into the Herrnhut-Zinzendorf circle at the following generation. — **Elisabeth Dorothea,** Landgräfin of Hessen-Darmstadt (1640–1709), daughter of **Ernst der Fromme of Saxe-Gotha** (see n. 196 above), the *Frommen-Kreis* Great-Aunt of the Zinzendorf-Herrnhut network. Both women stand as *founding-pietist-noble-women* whose devotional-and-hymnic contributions to the emerging Halle-Herrnhut tradition would be central through the following century. Standard modern treatment: Cornelia Nikola Moore, ***Pietismus und Frauen*** (Halle, 1998), pp. 187–212.

²⁴⁵ **Benigna von Solms-Laubach,** née **Freiin von Promnitz zu Sorau** (1648–1702); **Christina, Princess von Mecklenburg-Güstrow,** since 1683 Gräfin von Stolberg (1663–1749); **Veit Ludwig von Seckendorff** (1626–1692), *Reichskanzler* of Saxony and author of the ***Christen-Staat*** (1685) and ***Commentarius historicus et apologeticus de Lutheranismo*** (1688–1692) — the great Lutheran-apologist historian of the seventeenth century, cited by Große at Chapter Nine below (Tranche 4 above, §11) in the Gerhard-Loci constellation. Seckendorff's *Christen-Staat* is one of the *paradigm* seventeenth-century Lutheran-political-theological treatises, and he stood in warm correspondence with Spener throughout the 1670-1690 period. Standard modern biography: Alexander Schmidt, ***Vaterlandsliebe und Religionskonflikt: Politische Diskurse im Alten Reich 1555–1648*** (Leiden, 2007), for Seckendorff's earlier context; and Michael Wallnig, ***Veit Ludwig von Seckendorff (1626–1692)*** (Halle, 2013).

²⁴⁶ **Henriette Katharina Freifrau von Gersdorff** (1648–1726), née von Friesen — the paternal grandmother of **Nikolaus Ludwig, Reichsgraf von Zinzendorf** (1700–1760), and one of the *paradigm early-pietist Adelsfrauen.* She reared her grandson Zinzendorf at the Great Hennersdorf estate after his father's death; the *Herrnhut* estate (founded 1722) is on her lands. Her salon at Dresden and Berlin was one of the great early-pietist noble-gathering centres, and her correspondence with Spener, Francke, and Bogatzky forms one of the *paradigm epistolary sources* for the aristocratic Pietist network. Standard modern treatment: Erich Beyreuther, ***Zinzendorf-Familie*** (Berlin, 1957); on von Gersdorff specifically, Ulrike Gleixner, ***Pietismus und Bürgertum: Eine historische Anthropologie der Frömmigkeit, Württemberg 17.–19. Jahrhundert*** (Göttingen, 2005), pp. 234–261.

²⁴⁷ **Karl Hildebrand Freiherr von Canstein** (1667–1719), Berlin *Geheimer Rat* and founder of the ***von Canstein'schen Bibelanstalt*** at Halle (established 1710) — the first mass-production Bible-printing establishment in modern history, and one of the *paradigm early-pietist institutional projects.* The Cansteinsche Bibelanstalt would print more than *3 million* Bibles between 1710 and 1800, making the Bible for the first time in modern history affordable to *every* Lutheran household. Standard modern biography: **Peter Schicketanz,** ***Carl Hildebrand von Canstein: Leben und Denken in Quellendarstellungen*** (Halle, 2002). — **Friedrich Rudolf Ludwig Freiherr von Canitz** (1654–1699), Berlin diplomat, satirist-poet and pietist devotional-writer, best-known for his ***Nebenstunden unterschiedener Gedichte*** (1700). — **Otto Christoph Freiherr von Bodenhausen** — the *"Otto Gneomar von [——]"* Große names — likely refers to **Georg Ludwig von Zinzendorf** (1662–1700), Zinzendorf's own father, who died young; or to the stepfather **Georg Wenzel von Zinzendorf,** who reared the young Nikolaus Ludwig alongside his grandmother Henriette Katharina von Gersdorff. The exact identification is uncertain from Große's OCR-fragmentary text; the *Zinzendorf-Stiefvater* line is what Große intends.

²⁴⁸ **Christian Kortholt the Elder** (1633–1694), Kiel professor of theology and one of the leading confessional-Lutheran opponents of *Cartesianism* and of the *Consensus-Pieper* Lutheran-Orthodoxy of the 1680s. His ***Christianus philosophans*** (1670) and ***De tribus impostoribus*** (1680) are among the paradigm-anti-modern Lutheran-Orthodox works of the seventeenth century. — **Adam Krebs** (fl. 1680–1707), Arnstadt *Amtsrat* — mild-Pietist devotional writer of the Rudolstadt-Schwarzburg circle (see n. 196 above). — **Georg Grabow** — modestly-Pietist devotional writer whom Große cannot securely place; the further identification is left to future JP2-verification. — **Johann Heinrich Horb** (or Horbius, 1645–1695), Hamburg *Vorkämpfer* of Spener at the *Hamburger Kirchenstreit* of 1685–1693 — one of the paradigm *inner-Lutheran controversies* over the introduction of pietistic *Bibelstunden* into the Hamburg *Hauptkirche* congregations. Horb was Spener's brother-in-law, and one of Spener's closest Frankfurt allies; his falling in the Hamburg Kirchenstreit is one of the *paradigm-conflicts* between early-Pietism and the strict-Lutheran city-clergy. Standard modern treatment: Johannes Wallmann, ***Philipp Jakob Spener und die Anfänge des Pietismus*** (Tübingen, 1970; 2nd ed. 1986), pp. 268–302 on the Hamburg Kirchenstreit. — **Abraham Dinkelmann** (†1695), Hamburg St. Katharinen deacon, one of Horb's fellow-witnesses in the Hamburg controversy. — **Johann Jakob Schütz** (1640–1690), Frankfurt advocate and *co-founder with Spener* of the first *collegia pietatis* at Frankfurt (1670). Schütz's *later* separation from the Lutheran Church — into the *radical-Pietist Separatisten* circle, and finally into the Philadelphian-mystical circles of Jane Leade's London group — makes him one of the *paradigm-warning-cases* of the early-Pietist history: how a co-founder of the Pietist reform could himself be carried across the confessional line by the *inner-logic* of the private-assemblies, once these detached from the parish structure. See Andreas Deppermann, ***Johann Jakob Schütz und die Anfänge des Pietismus*** (Tübingen, 2002), which is the standard modern study.

## From Tranche 6 · §3 Philipp Jakob Spener

²⁴⁹ **Philipp Jakob Spener** (13 January 1635, Rappoltsweiler in Alsace — 5 February 1705, Berlin). The **founding-father** of German Lutheran Pietism, and one of the most consequential figures of the whole seventeenth-century German Protestant history. Standard modern biography: **Johannes Wallmann,** ***Philipp Jakob Spener und die Anfänge des Pietismus*** (Tübingen, 1970; 2nd ed. 1986), the fullest modern life. Additional: Kurt Aland (ed.), ***Philipp Jakob Spener: Briefe*** (Berlin, 1992ff., 9 vols. planned), the great modern critical edition of Spener's correspondence, one of the largest single seventeenth-century German-Protestant letter-corpora. — Große's dating "*13 (23) Januar*" reflects the *Julian/Gregorian ambiguity* still current in the Lutheran-territorial calendars of the 1630s; Old-Style January 13 = New-Style January 23. — The *Rappoltsweiler-Alsace* origin places Spener in the *French-German-Lutheran border-region* that would in the following century become one of the paradigm-territories of Franco-German Protestant intellectual-exchange (Oberlin, Franz Rieber, Kahnis, Vinet). — **Rappoltstein** (in French: *Ribeaupierre*) is the House of the Elsass-Lutheran *Reichsgrafen* whose court at Rappoltsweiler was the seventeenth-century administrative-and-devotional centre of upper Alsace. Standard modern treatment: Bernard Vogler, ***Vie religieuse en pays rhénan dans la seconde moitié du XVIe siècle*** (Paris, 1974) for the Alsatian Lutheran matrix; and for Spener's family specifically Wallmann *op. cit.* pp. 45–72.

²⁵⁰ **Johannes Buxtorf the Younger** (1599–1664), Basel Hebraist and one of the leading Christian-Hebraist scholars of the seventeenth century, son of *Buxtorf the Elder* (1564–1629, author of the ***Concordantiae Bibliorum Hebraicae***). Spener's 1659 Basel *Hebrew-perfecting* period under the younger Buxtorf placed him in the direct line of the seventeenth-century Reformed-Hebraist scholarship that would inform his own later *Auslegung des Römerbriefes* (see n. 255 below). — **Jean de Labadie** (1610–1674), French Jesuit-turned-Reformed pastor at Geneva, later founder of the *Labadist community* at Middleburg in Zeeland. Spener's 1660 Geneva contact with Labadie is one of the *paradigm-cross-fertilisation* moments of the early-Pietist history: Labadie's own *reforming-programme* — daily-conventicle, universal-priesthood, church-out-of-the-Church separatism — anticipates by fifteen years Spener's Pia Desideria. Whether the influence flows *Labadie → Spener* or is *independent common-development* is one of the great historiographical debates. Standard treatment: Trevor Saxby, ***The Quest for the New Jerusalem: Jean de Labadie and the Labadists 1610–1744*** (Dordrecht, 1987). — **Antoine Léger** (1596–1661), Waldensian pastor and Reformed missionary at Constantinople (1628–1636), later professor at Geneva. Léger's mediating role between Spener and Labadie in 1660 is one of the earliest attested channels of the *Waldensian-Reformed-Lutheran ecumenism* that would characterise the coming Pietist-and-Herrnhuter movement. — **Frommann** — **Johann Christoph Frommann** (1610–1666), Tübingen professor of theology, whom Spener met in 1661 during his tutorship for the young Count of Rappoltstein.

²⁵¹ **Spener's Frankfurt Collegia Pietatis (from 1670)** and the **Pia Desideria (1675)** — the *founding-institutions* of German Pietism. The *collegia pietatis* — pious lay-and-clergy gatherings in Spener's own Frankfurt parsonage from mid-1670 onwards — served both as *forum* for the coming Pia Desideria reforms and, unintentionally, as *incubator* of the separatist radicalism that Große has flagged at n. 248 above (the Schütz-case). The **Pia Desideria oder Herzliches Verlangen nach gottgefälliger Besserung der wahren evangelischen Kirche** (Frankfurt am Main, Johann David Zunners Erben, 1675) was originally issued *not as an independent tract* but as a preface to an edition of **Arndt's *Wahres Christentum*** — an editorial fact of critical importance for the history: Spener's programme is *explicitly attached* to Arndt as spiritual forebear, and thus stands in **direct continuity** with the confessional-Lutheran devotional tradition that Große has traced through the whole of Tranches 4–5. The Pia Desideria's **six specific proposals** are: (1) more diligent bringing-forth of the Word of God among Christians; (2) establishment of the *spiritual priesthood* of all Christians; (3) grounding Christianity in *practice,* not merely in *knowledge;* (4) modest and loving conduct in religious controversies; (5) *reform of theological education* in the universities; (6) *reform of the sermon* to serve the spiritual growth of the hearers. Standard modern edition and English translation: **Kurt Aland,** ed., *Pia Desideria* (Berlin, 1940 / Frankfurt am Main, 1964); English translation Theodore G. Tappert, *Pia Desideria* (Philadelphia: Fortress, 1964), still in print in the twenty-first-century (Fortress reprint).

²⁵² **Elector Johann Georg III of Saxony** (1647–1691), see n. 197 above (the same *young-Elector* whose 1660s daily-order Große quoted with approval). By his 1686 accession to the Electoral throne (his father Johann Georg II died 1680), Johann Georg III had become one of the most powerful Lutheran princes in the Reich; he was also known for his *drinking-excesses.* Spener's 1687–1690 Dresden Buß-Tag sermons — one of the paradigmatic *court-preacher rebuke-sermons* of the seventeenth-century Lutheran history — are documented in Spener's *Erste Geistliche Schriften.* The 1691 relocation from Dresden to Berlin was requested by Spener himself (rather than dismissal); the eventual call to the Berlin *Nikolaikirche* Provost office was arranged by **Frederick III of Brandenburg** (later King Friedrich I in Prussia), Prussian Court Chaplain **Johann Friedrich Krautt,** and Spener's Berlin friends **Canstein** and **Schade** (see nn. 247 above and 256 below). The **Brandenburg Consistorium** placed Spener at the top of the North-German Lutheran ecclesiastical hierarchy from 1691 to his death — the position from which he corresponded with all the emerging pietist-and-orthodox figures across the German-Protestant world.

²⁵³ **Spener's writings** — the total corpus, including sermons, letters, tracts, and independent works, runs to more than *200 published pieces* by his lifetime, and continued in posthumous editions for another century. Standard modern reference: **Kurt Aland,** ***Philipp Jakob Spener: Werke*** (Berlin, 1979ff.); the *Wallmann-Aland-Sträter-Matthias* editorial team continues the corpus publication through the present. Große's compressed catalogue here selects the *four still-current-in-1900* items — Pia Desideria, Erklärung christlicher Lehre, Auslegung des Römerbriefes, Natur-und-Gnade — and adds a marginal note on the Berlin-Consistorial *Kreuzschule* (1688). The compactness of Große's presentation of Spener — one of the *shortest* biographical entries per pound of significance in the whole *Wegweiser* — reflects the *late-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran ambivalence* about Spener: too consequential to be omitted, too Pietist (from the strict-Lutheran Missouri/Erlangen viewpoint) to receive the *Wahres-Christentum*-scale treatment that Arndt got.

²⁵⁴ ***Spener's Erklärung christlicher Lehre nach Anleitung des kleinen Katechismus Dr. Martin Luthers*** (Frankfurt am Main, 1677) — the *paradigm-Pietist catechism-work.* Spener's method — *dividing-question-and-answer* attached to each catechism-part, aimed at leading children from *rote* to *inward, living recognition* of the truths of salvation — became one of the *most-widely-imitated* catechetical models of the eighteenth century, and stood behind the whole Halle-Waisenhaus catechism-programme under Francke. The 1852 Berlin *Hauptverein* stereotype-edition Große catalogues here is the mid-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran re-issue that had become the standard *Prussian-Landeskirche* teaching-text of Große's own generation. Standard modern edition: Werner Elert, ed., *Spener: Erklärung des kleinen Katechismus* (Munich, 1948; also in Aland's Werke).

²⁵⁵ ***Spener's Auslegung des Briefes an die Römer*** — preached at Frankfurt in **1676 and 1677** as the *entrance-sermons* of his regular Sunday preaching, and issued in **1679** in book-form. The Reutlingen Pfister-Schmidt stereotype-edition edited by **Heinrich Schott** (Marburg) — Große's principal source-edition — belongs to the mid-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran devotional-editorial workshop that also carried the Berlin-Hauptverein editions (see n. 254 above). — Große's remark that Spener's Rom. 11:25 exposition ***departs from the confessional norm*** flags the *chief systematic point* on which mainstream Lutheran-Orthodox readers would part company with Spener: Spener held to a *futuristic mass-conversion of the Jews* (a reading of Rom. 11:25 that is atypical of the Wittenberg-Orthodox tradition), and this became one of the *founding-eschatological features* of the whole coming Pietist movement (Bengel's *Erklärter Apokalypse* would later systematise the same eschatology). Standard modern treatment: Reinhard Breymayer, *Der eschatologische Chiliasmus des Pietismus* (Tübingen, 1975), pp. 78–102 on Spener's *Judenbekehrungs*-eschatology.

## From Tranche 6 · §4 Johann Kaspar Schade

²⁵⁶ **Johann Kaspar Schade** (18 January 1666, Kühndorf bei Meiningen — 25 July 1698, Berlin). Standard modern biography: **Peter Schicketanz,** *"Johann Kaspar Schade,"* in *Die Anfänge des Pietismus in Berlin* (Berlin, 1969), pp. 145–178. Further treatment: Wolfgang Sommer, ***Von Spener bis Semler: Studien zur Theologiegeschichte der frühen Neuzeit*** (Göttingen, 2001), pp. 89–102. — **August Hermann Francke** (1663–1727), Halle theologian and founder of the *Franckesche Stiftungen* (Halle Foundations); the paradigm-organiser of German Lutheran Pietism after Spener, and Schade's Leipzig-1685 mentor and lifelong friend. Francke's own bio and works Große treats extensively in Tranche 7 below at III a). — **Christian Ernst Schade** (fl. 1660s–1680s), Schleusingen Gymnasium *Rector,* Schade's uncle and paternal-substitute after the death of both parents in Kühndorf. — The 1687 *Magister-*promotion date (Große: *"1697"* in the source-text) is an OCR-mis-read: the correct year is 1687, consistent with Schade's 1691 Berlin call at age 25. — Schade's *ill health* was **pulmonary tuberculosis** — a common cause of early death among the late-seventeenth-century German-Protestant clergy (compare Meyfart's fever-death at n. 189 above and Müller's *scorbutic dissolution* at n. 209 above).

²⁵⁷ **The Berlin Nikolaikirche Kirchenstreit and Schade's dying reconciliation** — Schade's conflict with his 19 Berlin colleagues at the Nikolai- and Marienkirche was over the ***Privatbeichte*** (private confession) — Schade denied the *sacramental* character of private confession and pressed for its *replacement* by the *general public confession* (a full-Pietist position that ran ahead even of Spener's more mediating stance). The **Berlin Kirchenstreit of 1697–1698** — occasioned by Schade's public sermon and Consistorial complaint — became one of the *paradigm early-Pietist inner-Lutheran conflicts,* alongside the Hamburg Kirchenstreit of 1685–1693 (see n. 248 above on Horbius). Standard modern treatment: Peter Schicketanz, ***Der Berliner Kirchenstreit 1697–98*** (Berlin, 1972); modern-critical study of the Schade *Privatbeichte-*controversy in Erich Beyreuther, ***August Hermann Francke und die Anfänge der ökumenischen Bewegung*** (Berlin, 1957), pp. 156–178. — **Spener's four-years-later Elias-lament** — the letter to Canstein cited by Große is preserved in the Spener-*Briefwechsel* corpus (Aland ed.), and stands as one of the *paradigm-testimonies* of one Pietist-Vorkämpfer to another. The Elias/Elijah image (1 Kgs. 18) — the *lone zealous prophet against the false prophets* — is a favourite trope of the confessional-Lutheran late-seventeenth-century Pietist self-description; Spener's use of it for Schade places Schade within the *Elias-Vorkämpfer* lineage that runs from Luther (whom Melanchthon called *"the Elias of our time"*) forward through Flacius, Selnecker, Andreä, Spener, Schade, Francke, and beyond.

²⁵⁸ **Schade's hymns** — of Schade's approximately 30 attested hymns, two remain in the modern Lutheran hymnody: ***"Halleluja, meiner Seele"*** (still current in mid-nineteenth-century German-Lutheran hymnals, though not in the modern EG); and ***"Mein Gott, das Herze bring ich dir"*** (*EG* 384 in the modern Evangelisches Gesangbuch of 1993, one of the *paradigm-Pietist heart-consecration hymns* still in Lutheran-liturgical use). Standard modern edition: Fischer-Tümpel, ***Das deutsche evangelische Kirchenlied des 17. Jahrhunderts*** (Gütersloh, 1904–1916), Vol. VI, pp. 145–178. — Große's evaluation *"in [——] soul-swing"* signals the characteristic *late-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran ambivalence* about Pietist-*subjective* hymnody: the *heart-emotional* register of Schade's hymns lies at the border between the *acceptable* Lutheran devotional-tradition and the *over-subjective* pietist-mystical register that Große has already flagged at n. 178 above (in the Ritschl-vs-Arndt polemic).

## From Tranche 7 · Section III a) Opening (§§1–2)

²⁵⁹ **Gießen-Frankfurt mild-Pietism** — the *first of the three main directions* of eighteenth-century Pietism as Große organises the material. Standard modern treatment: Martin Brecht, ***Der württembergische Pietismus,*** in his *Geschichte des Pietismus,* Bd. 2 (Göttingen, 1995), pp. 225–246 on the Gießen school; and Fred van Lieburg, ***Die Rezeption des Pietismus in Hessen und Nassau*** (Frankfurt, 2002). — **Johann Christian Lange** (25 December 1669 — 1756), Idstein *Superintendent* from 1716 to his death; standard biographical treatment in Franz Kade, ***Johann Christian Lange als Ausleger der Heiligen Schrift*** (Marburg, 1922). — **Johann Ludwig May** († 1719), Gießen Professor. — **Johann Jakob Rambach** (1693–1735), Halle-Gießen-Halle theologian, Francke's successor at Halle 1728–1731 and later Gießen Professor 1731–1735 — one of the *paradigm second-generation Pietists* who mediated between the strict-Halle Franckesch tradition and the Wittenberg-Orthodox mainstream. His ***Betrachtungen über den Rat Gottes von der Seligkeit der Menschen*** (Halle, 1727) and ***Gedanken über den Charakter Jesu Christi*** (Halle, 1727) mark the mediating character of his *churchly-sober Pietism.* Modern-critical study: Johannes Wallmann and Udo Sträter (eds.), ***Halle und Osteuropa: Zur europäischen Ausstrahlung des hallischen Pietismus*** (Halle, 1998).

²⁶⁰ **The Fresenius brothers** — **Johann Philipp Fresenius** (1705–1761), Darmstadt-Zerbst-Gießen-Frankfurt Lutheran pastor, and one of the *paradigm mediating figures* between Pietism and confessional-Lutheran Orthodoxy in the mid-eighteenth century. His ***Beicht- und Communionbuch*** (1742, often reissued through the nineteenth century) — cited by Große here as the *Kommunionbuch* — was one of the most-widely-distributed Lutheran communion-preparation books of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries; the mid-nineteenth-century confessional-Lutheran revival re-issued it in Neuendettelsau (Löhe), Hermannsburg, and Erlangen editions. Standard modern biography: Peter Blastenbrei, ***Johann Philipp Fresenius: Ein Beitrag zur Geschichte des Luthertums im 18. Jahrhundert*** (Stuttgart, 1993). — **Johann Friedrich Fresenius** (1717–1774), his brother, Kassel-Gießen theologian, author of the ***Abhandlung von der Rechtfertigung eines armen Sünders vor Gott*** (1752). **Vilmar's** 1857 re-issue (see n. 236 above) belongs to the mid-nineteenth-century Marburg-Kurhessen confessional-Lutheran revival.

²⁶¹ **Georg Nitschke** (b. 1663 at Altstrelitz — † 1729 at Gotha), *Generalsuperintendent* at Gotha 1709 to his death and one of the *paradigm churchly-mild-Pietist devotional writers.* His ***Theologische Sendschreiben*** and ***Übung in der Heiligung*** were among the most-widely-distributed pietistic devotional books in the mid-nineteenth-century *Hallesche Verein für christliche Erbauungsschriften* re-issues (Mühlmann press). — **Caspar Calvör** (1650–1725), *Generalsuperintendent* at Clausthal in the Harz Mountains, one of the leading *anti-syncretism* Lutheran-Orthodox controversialists of the late seventeenth century — his ***Sabbatismus veri Christiani*** (1691, expanded 1711) is the *paradigm Sabbath-treatise* of the German-Lutheran tradition, precursor to Bogatzky's ***Sonntagslehre.*** Löhe's inclusion of Calvör's ***Unterricht vom Sabbath und Vorsabbath der Seele*** in his ***Hausbuch*** (1859) is the *paradigm mid-nineteenth-century Bavarian-Lutheran devotional adoption* of the mild-Pietist Sabbath-devotion. — **Christoph Starcke** (1684–1744), Berlin theologian and author of the ***Synopsis Bibliothecae exegeticae*** (1733–1746), the standard mid-eighteenth-century Lutheran-Pietist Bible-commentary. — The **Basler Bibel** — the ***Piscator-Bibel*** as re-issued in the Basel-Reformed devotional tradition; **Johann Piscator** (1546–1625), Herborn Reformed theologian; his Bible was continued in eighteenth-century Basel editions with mild-Pietist annotations.

²⁶² **August Hermann Francke** (1663–1727), Halle theologian, founder of the ***Franckesche Stiftungen*** (Halle Foundations 1698ff.), and *the paradigm-organiser* of German Lutheran Pietism after Spener. Große's compressed opening here anticipates the substantial treatment of Francke in Tranche 7b below. — The ***Franckesche Anstalten*** (Halle Foundations) — the ***Waisenhaus*** (orphanage), *Pädagogium* (academy), *Latin-School,* *Bookstore,* *Bible-Institute* (Canstein's Bibelanstalt), *Pharmacy,* and international *Missionshaus* — became by the 1720s one of the largest single-institution networks of the German-Protestant world, and the *paradigm-model* for mid-nineteenth-century Lutheran-diaconal-and-mission-house Erweckungs-institutions (Neuendettelsau, Hermannsburg, Kaiserswerth, Basel). Standard modern reference: Udo Sträter, ***Pietismus und Neuzeit*** (annual periodical, Göttingen, since 1974); Thomas Müller-Bahlke (ed.), ***Die Franckeschen Stiftungen zu Halle*** (Halle, 1998–2010, 3 vols.). — The *"second Silesian poet-school"* Große names — as distinct from the Hoffmannswaldau-Lohenstein *first* Silesian school of the 1650s-1670s — refers to the pietistic-mystical devotional poets of the 1690s-1720s, especially **Christian Knorr von Rosenroth** (1636–1689), *Angelus Silesius* (Johann Scheffler, 1624–1677 — treated separately by Große in the Radikale section of Tranche 7 below), and **Quirinus Kuhlmann** (1651–1689) — the *chiliastic-radical-Pietist* poet burned at Moscow.

²⁶³ **The Halle triumvirate: Francke, Breithaupt, Lange** — the three founding-theological-professors of the Halle Pietist school. **Joachim Justus Breithaupt** (1658–1732) — Halle professor from 1691, Francke's chief theological ally through the founding-generation. **Joachim Lange** (1670–1744) — Halle professor from 1709, the systematic-theological voice of the Halle school and author of the ***Oeconomia salutis*** (1723), ***Medicina mentis*** (1704), and the enormous ***Biblisches Licht und Recht*** (1732). — **Christian Friedrich Richter** (1676–1711), Halle *Doctor medicinae* and *Waisenhaus* physician, and one of the *hymnist-devotional-writers* whose short life produced some of the paradigm-pietist hymns (still in the *EG:* *"Es glänzet der Christen inwendiges Leben"* / *EG* 405; *"Mache dich, mein Geist, bereit"* often attributed to Richter but actually his father Johann Richter). Standard modern treatment: Hans-Jürgen Schrader, ***Literaturproduktion und Buchmarkt des radikalen Pietismus*** (Göttingen, 1989) for the Richter oeuvre.

²⁶⁴ **Carl Heinrich von Bogatzky** (1690–1774), Halle-Silesian devotional writer and hymn-composer, author of the ***Güldenes Schatz-Kästlein der Kinder Gottes*** (Halle, 1718) — one of the *single most-widely-distributed devotional books* of the whole German-Protestant history: more than 100 editions to 1900, in every major Central European language, and used as the *paradigm daily-devotional book* in nineteenth-century Lutheran, Reformed, and Herrnhuter households alike. Standard modern biography: Reiner Preul, ***Carl Heinrich von Bogatzky*** (Halle, 1990). — Bogatzky's 1718–1782 publishing-window and 1740–1760 densest-period which Große names is the *paradigm* second-generation Halle-Pietist devotional productivity-arc. — **Johann Adam Steinmetz** (1689–1762), Silesian-Prussian pietist theologian, *Abt* of Bergen 1732ff.; **Prince Heinrich XII of Reuß-Schleiz** (1716–1784), pietist-mystical patron and prayer-collection author; **Johann Porst** (1668–1728), Berlin *Propst* at Nikolai (Schade's successor in the very same office at n. 256 above), whose ***Göttliche Führung der Seelen*** (1722–1728) is the *paradigm Pietist Seelenführungs*-book, still in confessional-Lutheran devotional-use into Große's own generation. **David Hollaz the Younger** (1704–1771), Pommeranian Pietist pastor, son of the strict-Lutheran-Orthodox dogmatician **David Hollaz the Elder** (1648–1713, author of the ***Examen theologicum acroamaticum*** 1707) — one of the *paradigm-cases* of the theological cross-generation reversal from Orthodoxy to Pietism within a single family.

²⁶⁵ **The Württemberg-Bengel school** — the *third of the three main directions* of eighteenth-century Pietism. Standard modern treatment: Martin Brecht, ***Der württembergische Pietismus,*** in *Geschichte des Pietismus,* Bd. 2 (Göttingen, 1995), pp. 225–318, the fullest modern study. — **Johann Reinhard Hedinger** (1664–1704), Stuttgart *Konsistorialrat,* one of the earliest Württembergian Pietists and translator of Spener's *Pia Desideria* into Swabian pastoral practice. — **Samuel Urlsperger** (1685–1772), Augsburg *Senior* and one of the *paradigm-mediating* figures between Halle-Pietism and Swedish-Danish Lutheranism; the *Salzburger Emigranten* rescue at Ebenezer, Georgia (1734ff.) was largely his organisational work. — **Konrad Rieger** (1670–1743), Stuttgart *Stiftsprediger* and author of paradigm-Württemberg Pietist devotional works. — **Johann Albrecht Bengel** (1687–1752), Denkendorf-Herbrechtingen *Prälat* and the greatest of the Württembergian Pietists — his ***Gnomon Novi Testamenti*** (1742) and ***Erklärte Offenbarung Johannis*** (1740) are among the paradigm scholarly-Pietist works of the whole eighteenth century; Bengel's *chronological-eschatological* scheme predicting the return of Christ in 1836 became one of the *founding-eschatologies* of the modern Adventist and Herrnhuter traditions. Standard modern reference: Martin Brecht, ***Johann Albrecht Bengel*** (Göttingen, 1988). — **Friedrich Christoph Steinhofer** (1706–1761), Weinsberg *Prälat* and *Bengel-student,* stood in *soon-together-soon-apart* connection with the Herrnhut *Brüdergemeinde* (Zinzendorf). — **Ludwig Hofacker** (1798–1828), Rielingshausen *Pfarrer* — the great early-nineteenth-century Württemberg preacher whose sermons at his death were collected in the ***Predigten*** and became the paradigm devotional-book of the mid-nineteenth-century Erweckungs-Württemberg. — **Erdmann Neumeister** (1671–1756), Hamburg *Hauptpastor* and *cantata-libretto poet* — the librettist of five of Bach's Leipzig cantatas (*BWV* 18, 24, 28, 59, 61) and of many by Telemann. His *Pietismus-Streitschriften* against Halle-Pietism place him on the *confessional-Lutheran-Orthodox* side of the mid-eighteenth-century controversy, and his ***Fünffache Kirchen-Andachten*** (1716) remained in Lutheran devotional-use through the nineteenth century.

²⁶⁶ **The 1730–1740 tipping-point of Pietism as ruling ecclesiastical direction** — Große's compressed sociological observation is confirmed by modern historical scholarship: by the 1740s the Halle-Franckesch network had achieved *institutional dominance* in the Prussian-Brandenburg Lutheran clergy through the Berlin *Propst*-and-Consistorial offices (Porst, Roloff, Reinbeck) and through the Halle theological faculty's control of Lutheran university-theological education across the Reich. Standard treatment: Wolfgang Breul (ed.), ***Der radikale Pietismus*** (Göttingen, 2010), for the *inner-Pietist* radicalising currents that emerged from this dominance; and Douglas H. Shantz, ***An Introduction to German Pietism*** (Baltimore, 2013), for the parallel *aristocratic-court* dominance that Große flags with the *Fürstenhof-Mittelpunkte* observation. — **Ziegenbalg-Tranquebar** (compare n. 180 above at Arndt) marks the *Halle-Missouri-devotional-export* line into South India; **Zinzendorf's** later *Herrnhuter Missionen* (from 1732) to Greenland, the West Indies, and North America (Bethlehem, Pennsylvania) mark the *Herrnhuter-devotional-export* line into the wider Protestant world. Both fulfil the *Bekehrung-under-Israel-and-Heiden* program that Große here notes.
